**The join key.** Each organization has a join key that only holders of its organization key can compute, and the server can't: `joinKey = HKDF-SHA256(ikm = the organization's 64-byte symmetric key, salt = empty, info = "passbundle join key v1", 32 bytes)`.

**The install command carries a secret the server never sees.**
- When an owner or admin makes or replaces the command, their browser makes a 32-byte join secret and the server stores it only encrypted with the organization's key (an `EncString`, so owners, admins, and MSP staff who confirm there can read it, and the server can't). The command shows it next to the token: `-EnrollmentToken '<token>' -JoinSecret '<secret>' -OrgKey '<fingerprint>'`, each 43 base64url characters, checked and single-quoted like the rest.
- `<fingerprint>` is the SHA-256 of the organization's public key (SPKI, DER). The browser derives the public key from the organization's private key, which it decrypts with the organization key (owners and admins fetch it encrypted from `GET /keykeeper/organizations/<id>/private-key`), never from the public key the server reports.
- The install script writes all three to the extension's managed settings. The extension never sends the secret or the fingerprint anywhere.
- **Joining:** the extension refuses an organization whose public key (from the server) doesn't hash to the fingerprint, so a swapped key never gets a user key for account recovery. It sends, with the token, a proof: `HMAC-SHA256(secret, "passbundle join v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ user id ‖ 0x00 ‖ SHA-256(the person's public key))`, where the public key is the one derived from the person's own private key on that device. The server stores the proof with the join.
- **Confirming:** an app confirms a token join on its own only when the proof checks out against the public key it's about to encrypt to (constant-time compare), and the command is limited to email domains (see "No click only with domains"). Otherwise the join waits for an owner or admin's Confirm on the Members page, with the fingerprint. A hacked server can't make a proof for a key of its own without the secret.

**Email invitations carry a seal.**
- When an owner or admin invites someone from upstream's member dialog, their browser seals the invitation: `HMAC-SHA256(joinKey, "passbundle invite v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ lowercased email)`. The server keeps it by organization and email (`join_seals`), and an invitation sent without one removes any earlier one.
- An app confirms an invited member on its own only with a seal that checks out for that email. A membership the server made up for an address nobody invited waits for approval.
- **What it doesn't catch:** the server could still present a key of its own as someone an admin really invited (or as an invited address) at the moment they join. Only a person comparing fingerprints catches that, which is what requiring approval is for. The seal narrows the risk from "anyone, any time" to "the addresses an admin invited", and the limits and notice below make it loud.
- **MSP staff:** an MSP admin's browser seals each staff member's place in each client it reaches (`"passbundle staff v1"`, with that client's join key) when it adds them, widens their access, makes them an admin, or makes a client (for the staff the console lists as reaching every client). The server removes a staff member's seals when they leave or stop reaching a client, when the client removes or revokes them, and every staff seal in a client that removes its MSP (see "Offboarding"). Staff without a seal wait for approval, as do invited people with only an invitation seal. An invitation's seal goes once the person is confirmed, or removed: it has done its work, and an old one must never vouch for them again. When an admin changes someone's access or makes them an admin, the seal names the email the console showed in the row the admin acted on, never a fresh answer from the server; if the server now gives that person another email, nothing changes and the console says so.

**The settings carry a seal too.** Whether an organization lets people in without approval, its email domains, and where that setting comes from are sealed with its join key by the browser that saves them, with when it was sealed, so a setting the organization has since changed can't come back (version 3, November 2026; below). An app treats a setting without a valid seal as "an admin approves each one", so the server can't turn approval off. An MSP's default is sealed per client by the MSP admin's browser that saves it. Saving the setting keeps the domains only if the old seal vouches for them, and saving domains keeps "let people in", and where the setting comes from, only if the old seal vouches for them (otherwise approval, as the organization's own setting), so a change the server slipped in is never resealed.

**Limits and notice.**
- An app confirms at most 20 people per organization per day on its own; past that, the rest wait for an admin's Confirm, and the app says so.
- Each confirm on its own shows a notice on that device naming the person and the organization ("Let ana@acmedental.com into Acme Dental"), so an unexpected one is seen where it happened, besides the owners' email.

**Version 2: seals that end, used once (October 2026).** A version 1 invitation or staff seal vouched forever, so a hacked server could keep one and present a key of its own as that address years later. Version 2 replaces both; the join key and join proofs stay version 1, and joining seals have versions of their own (below). The test vectors are in `docs/vectors/joins-v2.json` (made by `make-joins-v2.mjs`), and the apps refuse version 1 invitation and staff seals.
- **The form:** `v2.<expires>.<nonce>.<mac>`, where `expires` is when it stops vouching (Unix seconds, in decimal), `nonce` is 16 random bytes and `mac` the HMAC, both base64url without padding: `HMAC-SHA256(joinKey, "passbundle invite v2" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ lowercased email ‖ 0x00 ‖ expires ‖ 0x00 ‖ nonce)`, with `"passbundle staff v2"` for staff. The end and the nonce are inside the MAC, so neither can be changed.
- **How long:** a browser seals for 14 days from when it seals. An app takes a seal only before its end, and never one that says it lasts more than 30 days from now (a sealing device whose clock runs ahead gets some slack, a forged long seal none). Invitations last at most 5 days in the apps, so an invitation accepted in time has more than a week to be let in on its own; after that, an owner or admin approves the person, or invites them again. Someone the directory invited (see "Provisioning from Entra ID") who signs up more than 14 days after an admin let them in waits for approval the same way.
- **Used once:** an app that lets someone in on a seal remembers that seal (by organization and nonce, until it ends, past sign-out) and never lets anyone in on it again, whatever the server says later. In the web vault this memory, and the day's count, live in localStorage (November 2026: before, they were in the tab's sessionStorage, which a closed tab loses); in the extension, in its local storage. The server deletes a person's invitation and staff seals in an organization once they're confirmed there, in upstream's confirm, which every way in ends at, so an honest server never offers a used seal again either. A new invitation, or staff access given again, comes with a new seal.
- **The server** takes only version 2 for invitation and staff seals, and refuses one that has already ended. It still can't read or make them.

**The client seal (October 2026).** The server says which organizations are an MSP's clients, and an MSP admin holds the keys of other organizations too (their own family's, another company's). So that a hacked server can't point an MSP admin's browser at those, every client carries a seal made with its own key: `HMAC-SHA256(joinKey, "passbundle msp client v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ MSP id)`, standard base64 (vectors in `joins-v2.json`). The browser that makes a client seals it at once; a client made before this has none until an MSP admin confirms it by name in the console ("Confirm client", with what that means). The server keeps it with the client (`msp_clients.client_seal`) and sends it with the MSP's clients. An MSP admin's browser writes staff seals, joining seals for clients that follow the MSP's default, and template copies only into clients whose client seal checks out with the key it holds, and moves template copies to the trash only there.

**Changing the MSP's default safely (October 2026).** Saving the MSP's joining default reseals each client the server lists as following it, but only a client the browser trusts (its client seal checks out) whose current seal says it follows the default (below) as the admin saw it on the page (approval or not, and its domains). The server drops the seal of every other client it lists as following, so their joins wait for approval until someone saves their setting: an organization that isn't a client, a client whose own setting differs from the default the admin saw, or a client that chose its own setting, even the same as the default, never gets "let people in" this way. If the server's default isn't the one the admin saw, nothing is saved. "Use default" seals the default the person saw, never a second answer from the server, and in the console only for a trusted client.

**Version 2 joining seals: where the setting comes from (November 2026).** A version 1 joining seal said what the setting is, not where it comes from, so a client that chose approval while its MSP's default was approval too looked like one that follows the default; when the default then changed to "let people in", a hacked server could list that client as following, and the admin's browser resealed it with "let people in", overriding the client's choice (a known limit until this). Version 2 records the source: `v2.<source>.<mac>`, where `source` is `own` (the organization's own setting) or `default` (a default it follows: its MSP's, or the server's outside an MSP), and `mac` is `HMAC-SHA256(joinKey, "passbundle joining v2" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ "1" or "0" ‖ 0x00 ‖ the domains, sorted and joined with "," ‖ 0x00 ‖ source)` in base64url without padding, so the source can't be changed. Version 3 (below) keeps all of this and adds when it was sealed; the apps and the server take only version 3 now.
- **Who seals what:** an organization's Joining and security page seals its own setting `own`; "Use default", saving the MSP's default for the clients that follow it, a new client, and a new organization outside an MSP seal `default`; new email domains keep the old seal's source when it vouches, and are sealed `own` (with approval) when it doesn't.
- **What an app checks:** letting someone in takes a seal of either source that vouches for the setting and domains. Saving the MSP's default reseals only clients whose seal vouches with source `default`. Version 1 joining seals vouch for nothing, so their organizations' joins wait for approval until someone saves how people join.
- **The server** took only version 2 joining seals (now only version 3), checks their form, and checks that the source matches the change it comes with (`own` for an organization's own setting; `default` for "Use default" and the MSP's default; for a new organization's or client's seal, how its setting is kept), and never makes or reads the MAC. It still decides which clients it lists as following; a seal's source is what keeps its word from mattering.
- **Test vectors:** `docs/vectors/joining-v2.json`, made by `make-joining-v2.mjs` with Node's crypto alone: seals from each source, with and without domains, and checks an app must make (the source asked for, an own setting presented as following the default, the source changed in the seal's text, version 1, another organization, a changed MAC, a source the apps don't know). The web vault's unit tests check its sealing against a copy (`libs/admin-console/src/common/keykeeper/joining-v2.vectors.json`), and the server's against another (`src/db/models/vectors/joining-v2.json`), each with its own HMAC; tier 1 checks both copies match and that the generator makes the file.
- **For the test server:** nothing made before this needs keeping (there are no users yet). Organizations whose joining seal is version 1 wait for approval until an owner, an admin, or an MSP admin saves how people join (or, for an MSP's client, uses the default or saves the MSP's default).

**Version 3 joining seals: when it was sealed (November 2026).** A version 2 joining seal said what the setting is and where it comes from, but not when, so the seal of a setting an organization once had stayed good forever: a hacked server could keep it and show it again after the organization changed its mind. "Let people in" after it chose approval, so the apps let invited people in on their own again; email domains it has since removed, so install command joins got in with no click again; or "follows the default" after a client chose its own setting, so the MSP admin's browser resealed it with the MSP's next default. (Showing an older setting of approval does nothing a server can't already do by dropping the seal.) Version 3 says when it was sealed, and an app never goes back to an older setting than one it has seen. The join key and join proofs stay version 1, and invitation and staff seals version 2. The test vectors are in `docs/vectors/joining-v3.json` (made by `make-joining-v3.mjs`).
- **The form:** `v3.<source>.<sealedAt>.<nonce>.<mac>`: `source` as in version 2; `sealedAt` when it was sealed (Unix seconds, in decimal, at most 12 digits, no leading zero); `nonce` 16 random bytes and `mac` the HMAC, both base64url without padding: `HMAC-SHA256(joinKey, "passbundle joining v3" ‖ 0x00 ‖ organization id ‖ 0x00 ‖ "1" or "0" ‖ 0x00 ‖ the domains, sorted and joined with "," ‖ 0x00 ‖ source ‖ 0x00 ‖ sealedAt ‖ 0x00 ‖ nonce)`. All of it is inside the MAC, so none of it can be changed.
- **Which is newer:** the one with the later `sealedAt`; of two sealed in the same second, the one with the greater nonce (compared as text). So every device puts any two seals in the same order.
- **What an app remembers:** for each organization, the newest joining seal it has seen (its `sealedAt` and nonce), per account on that device, past sign-out (`keykeeperJoins`, `joiningSeen`): one it checked (in the lists of who's waiting, in the list of every organization's setting below, or on the Joining and security page and in the console before saving) or one it made, once the server took it. A seal counts as seen only if its MAC checks out, so the server can't move what a device remembers. In the web vault this lives in localStorage, so a closed tab doesn't lose it; in the extension, in its local storage.
- **What an app checks:** a joining seal vouches only if it's version 3, its MAC checks out for the setting, domains, and source the server reports (and the source the app asks for, where it asks), and it isn't older than the newest this device has seen for that organization. An older one vouches for nothing, like a missing one: that organization's joins wait for approval on that device, and saving a change there keeps neither "let people in" nor the domains (see "The settings carry a seal too"). A newer one that checks out becomes the newest.
- **Keeping up:** every owner's and admin's web vault and extension, on each run of the background work (when it unlocks, when items change, and every five minutes), gets the setting and seal of every organization where they're an owner or admin along with who's waiting (`joining` in `GET /keykeeper/organizations/pending`), and checks each, whether or not anyone is waiting. So any of their devices unlocked since a change remembers it, and a server hacked after that can't show it the older setting.
- **How a browser seals:** at its clock's now, or one second after the newest seal it has seen for that organization if that's later. It checks the current seal before it seals a change, so a new seal is newer than the one it replaces even if the sealing device's clock is behind the last one's. Each seal gets a new nonce.
- **What it can't catch:** an app has only its own memory; the server holds everything else and can roll all of it back. So a device that never saw the newer setting can still be shown an older one: a new owner's or admin's first device, a browser whose site data was cleared, a device that wasn't unlocked between the change and the server being hacked, or, if the server was already hacked when someone saved the change, every device but theirs. Such a device still needs, as before, an invitation's or staff seal for the person (14 days, once per seal on each device) or an install command join's proof, within the daily limit and with the notice. Seals don't end, by choice: a setting that lapsed would stop "let people in" for every organization nobody touches, and the devices that would renew it on their own are the ones that can't tell a current setting from a replayed one, so renewing would launder a replay. An organization that wants no window at all requires approval, and compares fingerprints.
- **Not covered: the install command's join secret.** The server keeps it encrypted with the organization's key and can't read it, but it can serve an older one: after an MSP replaces a command (say it leaked), a hacked server could hand the apps the old command's encrypted secret, and a join proof made with the old command would check out, where the joining setting lets install command joins in with no click. Binding the secret into the joining seal would close it (a replaced secret means a newer seal); it isn't done, since every place that makes or replaces a secret would then reseal how people join. Until then, an organization worried about a leaked command removes its email domains (install command joins then wait for approval) as well as replacing it.
- **The server** takes only version 3 joining seals, checks their form and their source as for version 2, and never makes or reads the MAC. It doesn't compare times: it can't check them, and a hacked server would skip it anyway. Restoring the server from a backup looks like a replay to devices that saw newer settings: on those, the organizations whose setting changed since the backup wait for approval until someone saves how people join again, which seals newer than anything seen.
- **Version 2 joining seals** vouch for nothing, as version 1 before them, and the server refuses new ones. For the test server (there are no users yet): organizations whose joining seal is version 2 wait for approval until an owner, an admin, or an MSP admin saves how people join on the organization's Joining and security page; for an MSP's client that should follow the default, save it there and then use the default. Saving the MSP's default doesn't reseal them, since their seal doesn't vouch that they follow it.
- **Test vectors:** `docs/vectors/joining-v3.json`, made by `make-joining-v3.mjs` with Node's crypto alone: seals from each source, with and without domains; the checks an app makes of one seal (the source asked for; the setting, domains, source, time, or nonce changed; another organization; a changed MAC; version 2 and version 1; a source the apps don't know; a malformed time); which of two seals is newer, and whether a seal is current against the newest one seen (older, the same, newer, the same second with a smaller or a greater nonce); and when a browser seals, given its clock and the newest it has seen. The web vault's unit tests check its sealing and its checks against a copy (`libs/admin-console/src/common/keykeeper/joining-v3.vectors.json`), and the server's unit tests the form and the MAC against another (`src/db/models/vectors/joining-v3.json`); tier 1 checks both copies match and that the generator makes the file. The version 2 vectors stay, and the apps check they refuse them.

**Organizations made before this** have no join secret, seals, or settings seal, so their joins wait for approval until an owner or admin saves how people join, and their invitations until they're sent again. The first time an owner, admin, or MSP admin opens an install command without a join secret, their browser adds one (the token stays); commands already run without one still let people ask to join, and an admin approves them.

**What the server still decides:** who is on an MSP's staff when a client is made, what an organization's members see, and everything else it decides today. It can't decrypt anything, and with these rules it can't get an organization's key from an app on its own, except by posing as an invited address or a staff member's email at the moment they join, within a seal's 14 days and once per seal on each device, and, on a device that never saw an organization's newer joining setting, by showing it an older one (version 3 joining seals, above).

**Tests:** unit tests for each computation against the vectors (`libs/admin-console/src/common/keykeeper/join-seals.spec.ts`, which keeps copies of every version, and `join-checks.spec.ts` for who's let in: the daily limit, seals that ended, a seal used once, which clients are trusted, and a joining seal older than the newest seen, learned from a list of who's waiting or from every organization's setting, made on the device, or kept past sign-out); unit tests for the MSP admin's browser (`apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/msp/msp-api.service.spec.ts`: staff seals and the default's reseals only for trusted clients that follow the default the admin saw, "Use default" with the default the person saw, sealing after the newest seal seen, and not going back to an older one) and the background work (`msp-work.spec.ts`: confirming only as an owner or admin, copies only into trusted clients, checking every organization's joining seal on each run); API tests for who may leave seals and secrets, what the server checks (version 2 only for invitations and staff, not ended; version 3 only for joining seals), client seals, that the pending lists carry them (and every organization's joining setting for its owners and admins), that a change made without a seal drops the old one, and that seals are used up once someone is confirmed (`e2e/tests/api/join-seals.spec.ts`); and a web vault test where the server's answers about who is waiting are altered in flight (a made-up person, a stripped joining seal, a stripped invitation seal) and nobody is let in until the answers are real (`e2e/tests/joins-forged.spec.ts`), and one where an organization that let people in chooses approval, the server shows the owner's web vaults the older setting with its real seal, and nobody is let in (by the web vault that saved the change, nor by the owner's web vault on another device, signed in after the change, which learned it on its own background run from the list of every organization's setting), until the owner lets people in again (`e2e/tests/joining-replay.spec.ts`). The install-to-recovery test runs the whole command, secret and fingerprint included.

**Status:** built: the server's storage and routes (`keykeeper_seals.rs` and a migration), the shared checks and computations (`join-seals.ts`, `join-checks.ts`; version 3 joining seals and what each device has seen, November 2026), the web vault's sealing (`join-sealer.service.ts`, used by upstream's invite, the console, the Joining and security page, and organization creation), the extension's fingerprint check and proof, and the installer's two parameters.

### MSP templates

An MSP makes an item template once (a firewall, a Microsoft 365 tenant, a line-of-business app) and every client gets it, so technicians and the client's own people document things the same way everywhere. See "Item templates" for what a template is.

- **The source** is an ordinary custom template the MSP admin can see and share (a shared folder's share permission, "Folders and permissions", since its copies go to other organizations), in an organization all of the MSP's admins share, so any of them can change it. It's never in someone's own vault (a hacked server could otherwise name anyone's own templates as a source and have them copied out to clients; security pass, October 2026), and never one of the MSP's clients. The server records which item it is, never what's in it. The server names the source, so each admin's device checks it before copying: a template (never an ordinary item), in an organization, not itself a copy from an MSP, not in one of the MSP's clients, and one they can still share. Copies go only to clients whose client seal checks out (see "Joins a hacked server can't forge"), whatever else the server lists. A device whose person can't share it (someone took share away, or another admin chose it) skips it and says why, and the server records copies only from someone who can share the source. And it replaces only a copy of that template in each client, never another item the server points it at, and moves to the trash only items that are copies.
- **It applies automatically,** like a default policy: an MSP admin adds a template for every client (including clients added later) or for chosen ones, and that's all. Nobody sends anything.
- **How:** each client gets a copy, with the same template id, in its "Templates" collection, encrypted with that client's key. The server can't read templates, so it can't make copies; a browser holding the clients' keys must. Any MSP admin's web vault or browser extension does this in the background: when they unlock, whenever their items change (such as after editing a template), when the MSP console opens, and every five minutes in the extension. The server makes the client's Templates collection the first time, named by the admin's browser with the client's key: new, empty, and given to nobody, since every member gets to read it (security review, October 2026: the console used to name an existing collection, which could have been one holding anything). The server records each copy's item and which version of the source it was made from (the source's revision date), so any admin's vault can tell which clients are behind. It never sees a template's name or fields. If no MSP admin is signed in to either, changes wait until one is. The desktop agent doesn't do this work: it holds the user key only behind Windows Hello or Touch ID, and working in the background would mean keeping it unlocked. Two devices writing a client's copy at the same moment leave a duplicate; the next run moves the one the server didn't record to the client's trash.
- **Updating:** edit the template (the console's Edit opens it). Clients get the change on their own. The console shows "Update" only if a client couldn't be updated, to try again; a failure isn't retried automatically, so it can't repeat forever. A copy is the MSP's: an update replaces whatever the client changed in it. A client that wants something different makes its own template, which is just a template in its organization.
- **Removing:** MSP admins can remove a template, and choose whether its copies stay in the clients or go.
- **Who sees copies:** everyone with access to the client's Templates collection: MSP admins and technicians who see vault items, the client's owners and admins, and the client's own members, read-only. Members get it when the collection is first made and, after that, when they're confirmed, by any way in (an email invitation, the install command). Read-only, because the MSP keeps the copies: members use them in "New item from a template". A member an admin already gave the collection keeps that access, and the MSP's people reach it through the MSP as before.
- **Templates from an MSP** show where they are, like any template: under the client's name for its people. Someone who sees the same template in many clients (MSP staff) gets it once: copies identical to one already offered (the MSP's own template, when they see it) are left out, and a copy that differs shows where it is. The apps group them the same way (`core/src/templates.rs`; until November 2026 they made a "From <MSP>" group from the copy's own text). Nothing says who a template came from: a copy's definition says it's a copy (`from`), but anyone who can edit an item could write that, naming any MSP, so it's never shown as a sender (security pass, October 2026). The item view says only that it's a copy kept up to date elsewhere, so changes made there may be replaced.
- **Who:** MSP admins add and remove templates, and their vaults keep copies current. Technicians see which templates their clients get.
- **Audit log:** templates added, changed (which clients), applied to clients, and removed. Entries name the template only by id; the console shows the name to people who can see it.
- **Tests:** API tests for recording templates and copies (only the MSP's own clients, only items in that client, never a client's copy as a source), what a limited technician sees, and the permission matrix. A console e2e test creates a template in the MSP's own organization, adds it for every client, sees a new client get it, edits it and sees every copy change with nothing else clicked, and finds it once in the vault, where it is, with nothing saying who it came from. An extension e2e test, with no vault tab open, edits a template in the popup and sees the client's copy change.
- **What the server records (security review, October 2026):** a copy only when it's a secure note in that client's Templates collection and not another template's copy, from an admin with a place of their own in the client (see "Offboarding"); and the Templates collection only as one it makes, logged in the client's event log as a new collection by that admin. A client that deletes its Templates collection gets a new one with the next template sent there; until then confirming people goes on as usual (the server forgets the deleted one, and the MSP's Activity says so).
- **Status:** built (`keykeeper_msp_templates.rs`, a migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL, the console's Templates section, and `libs/admin-console/src/common/keykeeper/`: `msp-work.ts`, the background work the web vault and the extension share, and `msp-template-sender.ts`, which writes copies with upstream's own item code). A copy's definition marks it as a copy, so "New item from a template" offers identical copies once and the item view says changes may be replaced. Copies stay in the client's Templates collection: the web vault's and the extension's lists leave them out unless that collection is open, since every member has them and nobody edits them (`isMspTemplateCopy`). Someone's own templates stay in the lists, where they edit them. Clients, on a template's row, changes which clients get it in place; copies in clients that no longer get it can go to their trash or stay as those clients' own templates. Members get the Templates collection read-only (server: `give_templates`, called when the collection is recorded and in upstream's confirm), with an API test.

### Seats by client

PassBundle bills an MSP for one pool of seats across all its clients (the site's MSP page). An MSP that bills its own clients, for time and materials or per seat, needs each client's seats, so the console breaks the pool down by client, month by month. Billing itself (Stripe, invoices, prices) isn't built.

- **What counts:** a seat is a member of a client who has joined (accepted or confirmed) and isn't revoked, in the site's words "each person in a client's organization". People invited who haven't joined don't count, revoked members don't, and neither do the MSP's staff: no membership the MSP made (`msp_memberships`), and no one on its staff now, even where their place in a client is the client's own (they were a member there before they joined the staff). Someone in two clients counts in each. On a server that sends no email, upstream accepts an invitation for an existing account at once, so there that person counts from the invitation; the hosted service sends email. Staff are free, so a client's own person added to the staff stops counting there; who belongs on the staff is for the terms of service, not the server.
- **When it's counted:** the server records each client's seats once a day, with the job scheduler that runs upstream's purges (`MSP_SEATS_SCHEDULE`, default 23:55 UTC, near the day's end; blank turns it off). A reading counts for the UTC day it's taken on, and a later reading the same day replaces it, so a day keeps its last; a client that left since an earlier reading that day keeps that one. A day the server was down at that time has no reading. Today is counted live whenever someone asks.
- **Storage:** `msp_client_seats`, one row per MSP, client, and day: the client's organization id, the day (`YYYY-MM-DD`, UTC), the seats, and the client's name that day. Nothing refers to the organization, so a month stays readable after a client leaves its MSP, is renamed, or is deleted; rows go only with their MSP, and stay as long as it does. Counts and names only, never who. A migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL. There are no figures from before the server had it.
- **A month's figures:** for each client, its most seats on any day, its average over the days recorded for the MSP that month (a day it wasn't a client counts as none, so a client that joined or left mid-month averages less, and the clients' averages add up to the total's), and, this month, today's. This month counts today as it is now for clients now, in place of a reading taken earlier today. The totals are the sum of the clients' most, the average of the MSP's daily totals, and today's. Averages round half up to one decimal. A client shows its name on its last day that month (today's, for a client now), and whether it's still a client.
- **API:** `GET /api/keykeeper/msp/<id>/seats?month=YYYY-MM` (this month without one) answers `month`, `thisMonth`, `months` (this month and earlier months with figures, newest first), `days` (the days the figures cover), `clients` (each with `id`, `name`, `current`, `most`, `average`, and `today`, which is null in earlier months and for a client that left), and `totals` (`most`, `average`, `today`). `&format=csv` gives the same month as a file, `seats-YYYY-MM.csv`: Month, Client, Most on any day, Average, Today (empty in earlier months), one line per client and a Total line. It starts with a byte order mark so spreadsheets read names with accents, quotes what needs quoting, and puts `'` before a name that starts with `=`, `+`, `-`, or `@`, so a spreadsheet never runs it as a formula (a client's own owners can rename it). A month that isn't `YYYY-MM`, one that hasn't started, or another format is refused with 400.
- **Who:** MSP admins only. Technicians, a client's own owners, other MSPs' staff, and everyone else get 404 ("MSP not found"), never 403, which would sign the web vault out.
- **Console:** a Seats section for MSP admins: one line saying what counts, a month picker (this month and earlier months with figures), a table (client, most on any day, average, and today in this month only; a client that left says so), the total, and Download CSV, which saves the server's CSV.
- **Open, the owner's decision: what the invoice is based on.** Proposed: each client's most seats on any day of the month, added up. Then the per-client lines add up exactly to the MSP's invoice, and an MSP can pass each client's line on as it is. The average is shown for MSPs that prefer to bill their clients on it. The other choices: the MSP's busiest single day across all clients (can be less than the clients' most added up, so the lines wouldn't add up to the invoice), or the average (lower for MSPs whose clients come and go, but harder to check against a count on any one day). Prices aren't decided here (site/README.md).
- **Tests:** server unit tests (most and average, rounding, today counted as it is now, a client that left keeping its readings, month parsing, the CSV's quoting and formulas); API tests (`e2e/tests/api/msp-seats.spec.ts`): who counts (the MSP's admin and a technician, a client's own member who joined the staff and counts again after leaving it, someone invited who never joined, someone accepted but not confirmed, someone revoked and then restored, and someone in two clients), the permission matrix (MSP admin 200; technician, the client's own owner, another MSP's admin, and a stranger 404; no login 401), the refused inputs, and the CSV; and, on a server of the tests' own whose seats job runs every second, the daily record, a later reading replacing the day's, a client that left its MSP and was deleted keeping its month by its name, and an earlier month's most, average, months, and CSV, written into that server's SQLite database since its clock can't be moved. Web vault unit tests (`msp-seats.spec.ts`), and a console e2e test (`e2e/tests/msp-seats-console.spec.ts`): this month's table and totals, an earlier month from the picker, and both CSV files.
- **Status:** built (`keykeeper_msp_seats.rs` in the server's API and models, the migration, and `msp-seats.component.ts` in the web vault). Not built: billing, invoices, and syncing seats to a PSA.

### Server

- New endpoints live under `/api/keykeeper/msp`, so they never collide with upstream routes. Every one checks the caller's MSP role and that the org belongs to that MSP, and every action on one client also the caller's own confirmed place there (see "Offboarding").
- New tables: `msps`, `msp_staff`, `msp_clients`, plus an `msp_id` on memberships that came from an MSP.
- **Databases:** PostgreSQL in production and SQLite for self-hosting and tests. We drop MySQL and CockroachDB: fewer code paths, and our own migrations only need writing twice. CI runs the API tests on both.
- Creating MSPs is off by default on self-hosted servers (`MSP_SIGNUPS_ALLOWED`).

### Status

- **Built, server:** MSPs, staff roles, clients, staff kept in step with every client, the list of memberships waiting for a key, and a client's view and removal of its MSP (`src/api/core/keykeeper_msp.rs`, migrations for SQLite and PostgreSQL). MSP tables cascade, so upstream deletes of users, orgs, and members always work. A client's single-organization, two-step login, and other member policies don't apply to MSP staff. Upstream exempts owners and admins; the server exempts every membership that came from an MSP the same way, so a technician with a custom role, who is a regular member in each client, still works in every Business client. **Known gap:** the apps still apply a client's member policies, such as removing the individual vault, to a technician with a custom role, because they exempt only people who can manage policies.
- **Built, console:** "MSP console" in the web vault, shown to MSP staff: set up an MSP, clients with member counts and a link into each client's admin console, add a client with the Business defaults (its keys made in the browser with upstream's own code), each client's RMM install command, password health per client (expiring items, weak, reused, and exposed passwords; computed in the browser, so the server never sees it, with each client's items to fix listed by name; see "Exposed passwords"), and add, re-role, or remove staff. Opening it confirms everyone waiting whom the person can confirm (as an owner or admin there) and whose organization lets them in without approval; MSP staff's web vault and browser extension also do it in the background (see "Joining"). An MSP admin sees "Confirm client" on a client made before client seals, to seal it once by name. People in a client that requires approval wait for an admin's Confirm on its Members page, with the fingerprint.
- **Built, a client's actions (November 2026):** each client's row ends with its actions, one to a line so each label stays whole and they line up from row to row: **Admin console** (into the client's admin console), **Install command** (the panel with the one-line command an RMM runs on the client's computers, which installs PassBundle and joins their people to the client), **Entra ID sync** (the client's page for provisioning from Entra ID, for MSP admins), then **Confirm client** where it shows. Each says what it does in one sentence: as a tooltip when pointed at or reached with the keyboard, and to screen readers as its description. The column's header, Actions, is for screen readers only. On a narrow screen the table scrolls sideways.
- **Built, audit log:** every MSP action is recorded with who did it, from which address, and which client and person it touched: the MSP and clients created, staff added, invited, re-roled, and removed, install tokens replaced, people joining with an install command, members confirmed in a client, and a client removing its MSP. Names and emails are copied in, so entries stay readable after an account or organization is deleted. The console shows it as Activity, newest first, in plain sentences.
- **Built, invitations:** adding someone without an account invites them. The invitation's link is emailed when the server sends email, and shown to the admin either way. Accepting needs the link and an account with the invited address, so neither a leaked link nor someone registering that address is enough. Admins can cancel pending invitations.
- **Fixed:** upstream lets only owners confirm admins and owners, so a technician's console no longer tries to confirm other staff; it gets only the regular members it may confirm.
- **Built, client side:** a client's Members page says which MSP manages it and lists its staff; the client's own owners can remove the MSP there, which takes all of its staff out at once. MSP staff see the banner without the button, and the server refuses them anyway.
- **Built, tests:** behavior tests, the MSP and organization permission matrices, and a console e2e test where a technician gets into a client with no manual confirm. CI also runs `cargo fmt`, clippy, and the API tests on PostgreSQL.
- **Decided (October 2026):** deleting a client needs one of its own owners while it has any, and the MSP's people can't remove, revoke, demote, or recover those owners (see "Offboarding").
- **Built, default policies:** see "Default policies".
- **Built, templates:** see "MSP templates".
- **Built, seats:** see "Seats by client".
- **Not built yet:** key fingerprints; showing the MSP's actions to the client's own owners; choosing between several MSPs; MSP signup on our hosted service; a domain breach report (waiting on the owner, see "Domain breach report").

### Tests

- **API tests (tier 1):** a black-box suite that drives the real server binary over HTTP, with a fresh database per run. It's the backend's safety net, for upstream's endpoints and ours.
- **Permission matrix:** every MSP endpoint and the org endpoints it relies on, times every caller: MSP admin, technician, another MSP's staff, the client org's owner and members, a stranger, and no login. Each cell is an expected status code, and the table is the test.
- **Console e2e:** add a client, add a technician, see the member confirmed automatically, open the client's admin console, remove the MSP from the client's side.
- **Mail tests:** a second test server sends email to a local mail catcher (`e2e/mail-server.mjs`), so tests follow real emailed invites and cover features the server only offers with email, such as account recovery.

## Onboarding

A domain is never required. Each org picks a preset: Solo, Family, or Business.

- **Solo:** install, create an account, automatic import from local browsers, extension added to the default browser profile. Done.
- **Family or small team:** the owner creates an org and shares an invite link or QR code. Members join with any email address. The owner sets approval to automatic or one tap.
- **Business:** the admin adds emails (any domain), runs the RMM script, or connects Entra (optional). Users added any of these ways are pre-approved. The org-key confirmation runs silently on any admin device that is online.

The target is a working vault, with imported passwords, after zero decisions beyond the org name. Tier 2 tests this for every preset.

### MSP staff and account recovery

An MSP staff member's account reaches every client they work in, so whoever could recover it could reach all of them. So no organization recovers a staff account, through any membership: the server refuses enrolling a staff member in any organization's account recovery (accepting an invitation, the install command, or enrolling later), refuses recovering one, and ends every enrollment someone has when they join a staff or set up an MSP. This is per person, not per membership, so a membership a client grants a staff member itself is covered too. MSP staff are never recoverable.

### MSP staff and two-step login

A client's "Require two-step login" policy applies to its MSP's staff, who reach its items (decided October 2026). Staff without two-step login can't be confirmed into a client that requires it, and a technician who turns it off loses that client, as a regular member would. An MSP's admins own its clients, so the policy never removes them, as upstream never removes owners; the console asks them to turn it on. Separately, the MSP requires two-step login (an authenticator app, a security key or passkey, or Duo with the server's own Duo account; not email codes) of everyone on its staff by default, and a client's own setting covers everyone the MSP brings in (see "Two-step login for admins").

### Adding MSP staff

Adding someone to an MSP's staff always sends an invitation they accept themselves, with or without an account, and the answer to the admin is the same either way: adding an existing account directly would make them an owner of every client without asking, and would say which emails have accounts. Invitations expire with the server's invitation lifetime, need a verified email where the server sends email, and end when the person joins or leaves the staff. Where the server sends email, an MSP sends at most 20 invitations in any hour, and 3 a day to one address, counting cancelled ones, so the staff page can't flood an inbox. The database keeps an invitation's token only as its SHA-256 (security review, October 2026), as it keeps SCIM and script tokens: the link with the token goes out once, by email and to the admin who invited, and a copy of the database can't take an invitation.

### Login limits

Upstream limits logins per address: a burst of 10, then one a minute, and 50 requests shared by signup, pre-login, and the password hint. On rollout day a whole office comes from one address, so it stopped after ten to twenty-five people. What stops someone guessing a password is a limit on the account, wherever the guesses come from, so that's where the strict one is now.

- **Each account:** 10 login attempts from one network (an IPv4 /24 or IPv6 /64), then one a minute (`LOGIN_RATELIMIT_ACCOUNT_*`), counting the 2FA step and the email two-step code request, which checks the password too. Across every network, 100, then one every 36 seconds (`LOGIN_RATELIMIT_ACCOUNT_ANYWHERE_*`). Keyed by the email as typed (trimmed, lowercase), whether or not an account has it, so being refused says nothing about which emails have accounts. Keys are 32-byte hashes, and the server forgets the ones whose limits have filled back up.
- **Nobody can lock someone else out (security review, October 2026):** counting per network means guesses from elsewhere don't use up the owner's own allowance, and a device already signed in to the account skips the per-network limit, so the owner's own devices get through.
- **But a device id isn't a password (server security pass, October 2026):** device ids aren't secret, so a login naming a device already signed in to the account still counts against a limit across the whole account, with the same allowance as the one across every network (100, then one every 36 seconds), counted apart from it, so guesses from unknown devices can't use it up.
- **Addresses:** every limit keyed by address counts an IPv6 /64 as one address (a home or office gets a whole /64), and each forgets the addresses whose limits have filled back up now and then, so requests from many addresses can't grow it without bound.
- **Each address:** 100 logins, then one every 6 seconds (`LOGIN_RATELIMIT_*`, upstream's settings with new defaults). Pre-login, which every login starts with, gets the same allowance, counted on its own; it only says how to derive the key, the same way for an email without an account.
- **Signup and the rest:** the shared allowance for signup, the password hint, recovery emails, and Send access is 150 at once (was 50), still refilling at one a minute, so 50 people can sign up together.
- **What it costs:** one address can now try 10 accounts' passwords a minute instead of one. Every guess also needs the client's KDF (600,000 PBKDF2 rounds by default), each account still allows only 10, and the hosted service has Cloudflare's rate limits in front.
- **Self-hosters** who set these settings keep their values.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the account key (case, spacing, emails without accounts, per network, across networks, IPv6 and mapped IPv4); API tests on a third test server that keeps the real limits (the other two relax them), where 30 people at one address sign up, pre-login, and log in, and guessing one account stops at its 11th attempt, the right password included, while a colleague logs in and an email without an account is refused the same way. Both fail on upstream's limits.

### Onboarding through the RMM (Business, version 1)

No desktop agent needed: browsers already let policy hand an extension its settings, and our installer already writes browser policy.

1. **The MSP copies one command.** In the MSP console, each client has an install command: our install script with the server address and that client's enrollment token, join secret, and key fingerprint. The MSP picks Windows, Mac, or Linux and runs it from their RMM on the client's machines. A Mac takes two: macOS takes managed settings only from a configuration profile an MDM installs, so its first command makes that profile with `make-profile.sh` (the MSP uploads it to their MDM), and the second installs the app and its agent, with nothing secret (see "Installer and agent").
2. **The script configures the browsers.** It force-installs the extension where targeted, turns off the browser's password manager, and gives the extension two managed settings: the server address (upstream already reads this) and the enrollment token.
3. **The person signs up or signs in in the extension.** The server is already filled in. After unlock, the extension sees the token, asks the server which organization it belongs to, and asks the person once, in a small window: "Join Acme Dental?", what joining means, and, where the organization uses account recovery, that its admins will be able to reset their master password and sign in as them (with its fingerprint phrase, and a word that this would reach any organization where they're an owner or admin). On Join, it encrypts the person's user key to that organization's public key for account recovery (upstream's own enrollment code, as an emailed invite does) and sends the join. "Not now" sends nothing and asks again a week later.
4. **MSP staff's apps confirm them** in the background, like any member waiting for the org key, when the join doesn't need approval (see "No click only with domains"): the web vault or browser extension of anyone on the MSP's staff who can confirm in that client, while unlocked. The extension checks every five minutes, and the console confirms on opening. Joins that need approval wait for an owner or admin's Confirm on the client's Members page, which shows the person's fingerprint. The desktop agent doesn't confirm, for the same reason as in "MSP templates".

**The enrollment token** is per client, random (256 bits), and shown to the MSP's staff in the console. It lets an account ask to join that client as a regular member; it never grants the org key. Joins through it are auto-approved, per the Business defaults, so the token is a credential: the console can replace it (old installs then stop working for new joins), and each join is recorded with when it happened. The database keeps its SHA-256, to find it by, as it keeps SCIM and script tokens, and a copy sealed with AES-256-GCM under a key derived from the server's private RSA key (over "passbundle install command tokens v1", bound to the organization), so admins can see the command again but a copy of the database alone reveals no token (server security pass, October 2026; before, the token itself was kept). If the server's RSA key changes, the token still works but can't be shown, and admins make a new command. Test vectors: `docs/vectors/server-derived-keys-v1.json`. An account in another organization can't join (single organization).

- **Limiting it to email domains:** an MSP admin can list a client's email domains (acmedental.com, and others), and then only accounts at exactly those domains can join with the command; mail.acmedental.com is its own entry. An email counts only once it's verified, or anyone could sign up as ceo@acmedental.com and walk in, so the limit needs a server that sends email, and the extension tells an unverified person to verify first. Someone the client's admins invited by email can still join with the command from any address: an admin chose them. Changes go in the MSP's activity log.
- **No click only with domains (decided October 2026):** a join with the install command gets in without anyone approving it only when the command is limited to email domains (verified emails), and the join's proof checks out against the command's join secret (see "Joins a hacked server can't forge"). Without domains, the command can be copied off any machine it ran on and used with any account, so those joins wait for an owner or admin to approve them, and the owners' email says so. The console asks MSPs to set domains.
- **Invitations through the command:** someone the client invited by email can join with the command instead of the link, but only for a regular invitation (not admin, owner, access to all items, or a custom role) and, where the server sends email, from a verified address. Otherwise registering an invited address would be enough to take its invitation. Joining gives Can edit in the Everyone collection, except to someone whose invitation already gave them a grant there, which stays as the admin set it.
- **Who hears about a join:** the client's own owners get an email for each one ("<email> joined Acme Dental with your IT provider's install command", or "is waiting to join" where an admin approves joiners), with a link to the Members page. Its MSP's people don't: a rollout to 50 machines would be 50 emails, and the console's Activity has every join. A person who was invited by email first is the inviter's to hear about, as for any invitation.

- **What the extension won't do:** send the token to any server but the one the same policy names (an account on another server never sees it); join for MSP staff, who sign in on their clients' computers; or keep the token itself (it remembers a hash, to ask once per token).
- **The command can't carry code:** the console checks each part of the command it shows (the token, join secret, and key fingerprint are 43 base64url characters each, the server a plain HTTPS address, the script names a `.ps1`, a `.sh`, and a `.mobileconfig` file from the brand) and single-quotes them for the shell that runs them: PowerShell on Windows (doubling any of its four quote characters), the POSIX shell on a Mac and Linux (where nothing in single quotes is expanded). A token that isn't one isn't shown or copied, so a hacked server can't turn the command into code the RMM runs on every client machine. The Mac and Linux commands also refuse an IPv6 address, which their scripts don't take. Every script checks the token and address again. The Mac and Linux scripts take a value only if all of it is one line with no control characters (grep checks line by line, so a line break could carry an unchecked second line into the profile or policy file), and check that again where they write each value. All three commands come from one function (`libs/admin-console/src/common/keykeeper/install-command.ts`), with unit tests for each kind of computer, the quoting, and the refusals.
- **Comparing fingerprints:** next to the command, the console shows the organization's fingerprint phrase, made from the key the command's fingerprint comes from. The extension's join prompt shows the same phrase and says to ask IT to read theirs, and that Not now asks again in 7 days.
- **Signed in elsewhere:** someone who signs in to another server than the one the command named gets one notice naming both servers and saying to sign in to the command's instead; the token still goes nowhere. A command that names no server says so.

**Status:** built. The server issues a token with each new client and joins through upstream's invite acceptance; the MSP console shows each client's install command for Windows, Mac, or Linux with a copy button, the number of people who joined with it, and (for MSP admins) a way to replace the token; the install script writes the extension's managed settings; the extension asks, joins after unlock, and says so once. An end-to-end test runs the whole path with real keys on the mail test server: the extension asks, the person joins, an MSP admin approves them on the Members page and recovers their account, and they sign in with the new password to an intact vault. Another says "Not now" and checks nothing joined, and that a token for another server goes nowhere. Email domains and join emails are built too (`enrollment_domains` on the server, the console's install command panel), with mail tests for both and a console test. Not yet: the install script's download URL with a pinned signature (the console shows the command for a local copy of the script).

**Tests:** API tests for the token endpoints and their permission matrix, joining (with and without account recovery, already a member, single organization, a replaced token), and that a join never includes the org key. The extension's join logic gets unit tests with managed settings stubbed, including the notice for another server. Tier 2 runs the real install script on Windows and checks the policy values the browsers will read. `e2e/tests/install-commands.spec.ts` copies the command for each kind of computer from the console, with the same token, join secret, and fingerprint in each, runs the Mac one with the real `make-profile.sh` and checks the profile holds them, and runs the Linux one with the real install script, which takes every argument and stops only at its root check. The recovery test checks the phrase next to the command is the one the join prompt shows.

### A company's own IT

A company that runs its own IT gets what an MSP's client gets, without setting up the MSP console: the Business defaults, the Everyone and Admins collections, and an install command. An IT team that runs several companies or locations uses the MSP console instead.

- **"New organization" asks what it's for.** A business (the default choice) gets the Business preset in one server call (`POST /keykeeper/organizations`), so a closed browser tab can't leave it half set up: single organization, master password requirements, account recovery with automatic enrollment, the Everyone and Admins collections, and an install command. A family or a group gets upstream's organization: no policies, one collection. Both follow upstream's rules for who may make an organization (`ORG_CREATION_USERS`, and not while a member of an organization with single organization on).
- **Joining and security** (November 2026) is the organization's settings page for its owners and admins: two-step login for admins, Joining, the install command, and script access. They used to be on Organization info, which is upstream's page for owners only (it loads `GET /organizations/<id>`, which refuses admins), so a company's own IT admin couldn't reach what the server lets them manage. The page opens for owners and admins (upstream's `isAdmin`), and each card asks the server for its part and stays hidden on "not found", so it shows exactly what the server allows: owners and admins get Joining and the install command; two-step login for admins and script access go to owners and admins and an MSP's admins, not its technicians, so a technician who is an admin in a client sees Joining and which IT provider manages the install command. Managers and custom roles get neither the menu entry nor the page. Nothing on it answers 403 to the people it opens for. The server's rules didn't change.
- **The install command is on Joining and security**, below Joining, for its owners and admins: the command for Windows, Mac, or Linux with a copy button, how many people joined with it, email domains (with a server that sends email), and Replace token. An organization without one (made before this, or for a family) has "Create install command". Everything else is the MSP version's: the same token, the same join (`/keykeeper/enrollment/join`), Everyone for people who join, and the owners' email, which says "your install command" instead of "your IT provider's".
- **An MSP's client's command stays in the MSP's console.** Making, replacing, or limiting it through the organization routes is refused for an MSP's client, even for its owners, so a technician the MSP didn't give install commands can't get one through the client's admin console, where they're an admin. Reading it answers only which IT provider manages it, and the client's Joining and security page says so.
- **Reading never answers 403.** Upstream's web vault signs out on a 403, and every owner's and admin's Joining and security page reads the command, so anyone else gets "not found" (the card stays hidden) and an MSP's client gets the provider's name, not a refusal.
- **A client that removes its MSP** keeps its preset and Everyone collection as its own, but not the token, which was the MSP's: its owners make a new command.
- **Storage:** `org_setups` (organization, preset, token, Everyone collection), the counterpart of the same columns in `msp_clients`. Joins are counted in `msp_enrollment_joins`, which is keyed by organization.
- **Tests:** API tests for the preset, joining, the permission matrix (owners and admins only; members, managers, outsiders, anonymous, and an MSP's admin and technicians refused), a client that removed its MSP, and upstream's creation rules; a mail test for the owners' email and email domains; a web vault test that makes a business and a family organization, joins one with its command, and replaces the token; and one where a company's admin who isn't an owner opens Joining and security, sees all four parts, and changes how people join, without a refused request, while a custom role that manages policies gets neither the menu entry nor the page (`e2e/tests/joining-security.spec.ts`).

### Which browser profiles

In order:

1. The profile signed in with the invited email.
2. A profile whose signed-in email matches an org domain, if the admin set one.
3. The browser's default profile.
4. Ask once.

Personal profiles are never imported by default.

## Defaults (Business preset)

| Setting | Default |
|---|---|
| Account recovery by admin | On, with automatic enrollment. Disclosed at signup. Turning it on later enrolls users silently at their next unlock. |
| Single organization | On. A work account belongs to one organization; personal items go in a separate personal account. Account recovery opens a member's whole account, so this keeps a client's admins out of members' family organizations. Owners and admins, including MSP staff, are exempt. |
| Members added by portal, RMM, or Entra | Auto-approved (Entra's: through the install command at the client's domains, or once an admin lets them in; see "Provisioning from Entra ID") |
| Members joining through an invite link | Approval required |
| Two-step login | Required for owners, admins, custom roles that manage members, policies, or account recovery, and everyone an MSP brings in: an authenticator app, a security key or passkey, or Duo with the server's own Duo account, not email codes (see "Two-step login for admins"). Everyone else: not required by default; an organization can require it of everyone, and anyone no rule covers who has no two-step login gets a nudge to set it up (see "Two-step login for everyone"). |
| Master password | Minimum strength, plus a breached-password check. If the breach service can't be reached (offline or firewalled), signup continues without it and Health re-checks later. Upstream silently failed the form instead. |
| Vault lock | When the computer locks; in the web vault, which can't see that, after 15 minutes without use (`docs/core.md`, "Locking by policy") |
| Browser password manager on managed devices | Saving and password autofill off. Card and address autofill: optional toggles, default unchanged. |
| Private items when someone leaves | Stay with the org |
| Offboarding | Access revoked immediately. Shared passwords the person could see are listed for rotation: removing or revoking someone says what they can open, then lists the logins whose passwords they could see (built; see "Access and offboarding"). |
| Collections | "Everyone" and "Admins" created automatically |

The Solo and Family presets have no admin recovery. They use emergency access instead.

**Status:**

- **Built:** an MSP's "Add client" applies the MSP's default policies (see "Default policies"), in one server call so a closed browser tab can't leave a client half set up. A new MSP's defaults are the Business preset: account recovery with automatic enrollment, single organization, master password requirements (12 characters and a strength score of 3 out of 4; existing passwords aren't forced to change), vault lock when the computer locks, and two-step login for admins. Add client also creates the "Everyone" and "Admins" collections. All of these are upstream policies and collections, which the client's owners can change in its admin console.
- **Account recovery needs email.** The server only offers it when it can send email, since a member must be told when an admin resets their password. Our hosted service sends email. Self-hosters without SMTP don't get account recovery. The tests need a local mail catcher to cover it end to end, which also lets them test emailed invites; that comes with the enrollment work below.
- **MSP staff are never part of a client's account recovery (built).** Recovery lets a client's admins reset a member's master password and take over the whole account, and an MSP staff member's account can see every other client. So the server refuses account recovery enrollment, and recovery itself, for memberships that came from an MSP. Without this, a technician could enroll with upstream's own "join account recovery" option. A mail test proves the refusal and that a regular member invited by email is still enrolled.
- **Account recovery stays on by default (decided 2026-10-23).** An MSP must be able to reset a client's member's master password from day one, without asking each person to enroll afterwards: that chase after a migration is what this replaces. So the Business preset, and with it every new MSP's defaults and every new Business organization, turns on account recovery with automatic enrollment. Owners and admins can still turn it off per organization. Briefly changed to off on 2026-10-22 and reverted the next day.
- **Single organization is part of the Business preset (decided 2026-10-15).** Account recovery opens a member's whole account, including any other organization they belong to, so Business clients keep work accounts to one organization, as Bitwarden requires. MSP staff are exempt, as upstream exempts owners and admins; tests check that staff, including a technician with a custom role, reach every Business client while a member of one can't be confirmed into another, and that turning on two-step login or single organization in a client leaves staff alone.
- **Enrolling members who didn't use an emailed invite:** members who join through the RMM enrollment link enroll at join, like an emailed invite (see "Onboarding through the RMM"). What remains is enrolling members of organizations that turn the policy on later; that waits until someone needs it. Upstream enrolls a member in account recovery only when they accept an emailed invite. Members added any other way (the MSP console, the RMM script, members added before the policy was on) would never be enrolled. Our clients will close that gap: on unlock, for each organization whose account recovery policy has automatic enrollment and where the member isn't enrolled, the client enrolls them with upstream's own enrollment code, which encrypts the member's user key to the organization's public key exactly as the invite flow does. The member sees a one-time notice naming the organization. The trust is the same as upstream's invite flow: the client takes the organization's public key from the server. Tests: a member added through the MSP console unlocks and is enrolled; an owner then sees them as enrolled; a member of an organization without automatic enrollment is never enrolled.
- **Two-step login for admins (built):** upstream's policy exempts owners and admins, the opposite, so it's our own setting, on in the Business preset and every new MSP's defaults (see "Two-step login for admins").
- **Not possible with upstream policies, so not done yet:** keeping private items with the organization needs upstream's newer data ownership policy, which Vaultwarden doesn't support yet.
- **Built for a company's own organization too:** "New organization" for a business applies the same preset and makes an install command (see "A company's own IT").
- **Not built:** new members invited by email getting the "Everyone" collection automatically (people who join with the install command get it), and the Solo and Family presets.


Every setting explains itself in one sentence. Changing a setting needs no action from users.

## Folders and permissions

One word, "folder", for every place an item lives, grouped by who owns it, like a drive: **Personal** holds your private folders, and **each organization** holds its shared folders. An item in Personal is yours alone; to share it, move it into an organization's folder, and from then on it belongs to that organization. Each shared folder says, per person or group, exactly what they can do there, as a grid of capabilities with presets for speed. (Decided by the owner, October 23, 2026.)

### The model

- **Personal** is upstream's individual vault, and its folders are upstream's folders: only you see them, and nothing in Personal has permissions, because it's yours.
- **An organization's shared folders** are upstream's collections. Everything in them belongs to the organization, encrypted with its key; who reaches a folder is upstream's collection access (directly or through a group), with the capabilities below.
- **Sharing is moving:** "move to an organization" re-encrypts an item with the organization's key (upstream's share), into one of its folders. Nothing moves back out of an organization (upstream's rule); a copy is a new item.
- **An organization item can be in several of its folders** (upstream). Upstream also lets anyone file any item they see in one of their personal folders, which is per person; the web vault no longer offers that for organization items, and a personal folder in the menu shows whatever its owner filed there.
- **Words:** "vault" leaves the screens (see "Where 'vault' stays" below). Code, wire, and database names stay upstream's (collections, folders, organizations), as the naming rule says.

### The grid

Ten capabilities, set per shared folder for each person and each group that reaches it:

| Capability | Lets them | Enforced by |
|---|---|---|
| See items | Get the folder's items at all: in sync, by id, their attachments | Server |
| See passwords | See and copy every value that isn't plain: passwords, one-time codes, hidden fields, card numbers and codes, SSH private keys, SSNs, passport, license, and national ID numbers, and bank account numbers, PINs, IBANs, and SWIFT codes. Plain values (names, usernames, websites, notes, contact details, addresses, dates, a card's brand and expiry, a bank's routing and branch numbers, an SSH key's public key) stay visible. Without it, people can still fill these into web pages, and a page can read what's filled (see below) | Apps |
| Create | Make new items in the folder, or add an existing item to it (with share on that item) | Server |
| Edit | Change an item's contents and attachments, and restore an earlier version | Server |
| Delete | Move items to the trash, restore them from it, and delete them for good | Server |
| Export | Include the folder's items in an export | Apps |
| Share | Move an item out of the folder, add it to another folder, or copy it somewhere else | Apps (the server also refuses moving it out and adding it to another folder) |
| Print | Print an item | Apps |
| View item history | See an item's earlier versions (who and when only, without see passwords in the same folder) | Server |
| Manage | Change the folder's name, and who reaches it and with what | Server |

- **What needs what:** every capability needs see items, so taking see items away takes the person or group out of the folder. Export, print, and share need see passwords, since each puts an item's secrets somewhere this folder's rules don't reach. Manage includes every other capability: whoever manages a folder can give themselves anything in it, so the grid says so. Create, edit, and delete are independent: a drop folder has create without edit, and a reference folder edit without delete. The server refuses a grid that breaks these rules; it never fills in a capability that wasn't asked for.
- **Presets**, in the access selector, with Custom opening the grid:

  | Preset | Capabilities |
  |---|---|
  | Can view | See items, see passwords, print, view item history |
  | Can view but not see passwords | See items, view item history |
  | Can edit | See items, see passwords, create, edit, delete, share, print, view item history |
  | Can manage | All ten |
  | Custom | Any other set |

  Export is in Can manage only, as upstream lets only folder managers and admins export an organization's items; Custom adds it. Upstream's fifth preset, "Can edit except passwords", is a Custom set (see items, create, edit, delete, view item history).
- **More than one way to a folder:** someone who reaches a folder directly and through groups gets, for each capability, the most open of those ways. So see passwords is off only if every way turns it off: the Access page's rule, and now the server's on every path (upstream's server let a direct grant override all of a person's group grants on some paths, and not others).
- **An item in more than one folder:** for each capability, what any folder holding it allows, as upstream decides edit and see passwords. Adding an organization's item to another folder needs share on the item and create there, and taking it out needs share in the folder it leaves (below). Two things need two capabilities in the same folder, never one from each of two: an item's old versions' contents (view item history and see passwords) and clearing its history (view item history and delete).
- **Who the grid doesn't limit:** the owner of a personal item, and owners, admins, and managers with access to all items (upstream's custom role that manages all collections) have every capability in every folder. Other members with access to all items (an MSP's technician with vault items, or someone in a group with access to all) have every capability in every folder but manage: a custom role never manages every folder ("Roles and permissions"). Everyone else gets what their grants say, upstream's Manager role included (Vaultwarden showed a Manager's editable folders as managed without letting them manage them; now what it shows is what it allows).

### What the server enforces, and what the apps do

**The server enforces see items, create, edit, delete, manage, and view item history.** No client can get past these, whatever it runs:

- **See items:** only people who reach a folder get its items, in sync or one by one, and their attachments (upstream).
- **Create:** every way an item comes into a folder: making an item in it, moving a personal item into an organization (share), importing into the organization, and adding an existing item to the folder (one item or several at once, from the vault, the admin console, or upstream's share request for an organization's item).
- **Adding an existing item to another folder is sharing it:** that folder's people then see it, under that folder's rules, which can let the person who added it delete it or read its history where the folders it was in didn't. So it needs share on the item (what any of its folders allows, the same rule the apps use for copying it) as well as create in the new folder, on every path above. Someone who can edit an item without share can't move it somewhere they could do more with it. An MSP template's source needs share too, since its copies go into other organizations.
- **Edit:** saving an item's contents, as one item or as part of a move, and adding or deleting its attachments. Restoring an earlier version is an edit.
- **Websites need see passwords (security review, November 2026):** autofill sends the password where an item's websites point, so someone who could edit them without seeing the password could send it to a site of their choosing. Without see passwords, the websites are read-only in the web vault's and the extension's item form, the command-line tool refuses a change, and the apps show them read-only; every save sends them back exactly as stored, still encrypted (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/hidden-websites.ts`, `core/src/vault.rs`), and refuses when they wouldn't open as they were. The server can't read the addresses, but it sees their ciphertext, so it refuses any change to it from a member without see passwords: another address or checksum, another match setting, one more, or one less (`e2e/tests/api/hidden-websites.spec.ts`).
- **Delete:** moving to the trash, restoring from it, and deleting for good, one item or several, from the vault or the admin console. Clearing an item's history, or deleting one of its versions, needs delete and view item history in the same folder.
- **Changing an item's folders:** needs edit and see passwords on the item (upstream's rule, so moving an item can't show someone passwords they couldn't see), share on the item and create in each folder it's added to, and share in each folder it leaves. An item must stay in at least one folder for anyone without access to all items, who would otherwise lose it. An item's `collectionIds`, in sync and in every response about one item, list each of its folders the person reaches, read-only ones included, so the item form posts back the folders it was in and a save never takes it out of one by leaving it off.
- **Manage:** the folder's name, people, and groups (`PUT /organizations/<org>/collections/<id>` and bulk access).
