A regular member who manages a folder now gets exactly what upstream's dialog needs for it, from a fixed list of routes (`keykeeper_folder_permissions.rs`): that folder's details and access, the names and emails of members who have joined (`users/mini-details`: accepted and confirmed, not invitations or revoked members), the group names (without a directory's id, as for custom roles), and the folder list, with access lists only for the folders they manage. Making and deleting folders stays with owners, admins, and managers (upstream's "limit collection creation" and "deletion" settings, which our server turns on). A confirmed member turned away from a manager's route gets 404, as for something that isn't there, not upstream's 401, which signs the web vault out. Custom members get 404 there too, since the web vault signs out on 403 as well (they keep 403 elsewhere, "Roles and permissions"). When the database can't say whether someone manages a folder, these routes answer 500, not 404, so an admin isn't told they aren't a manager.
- **View item history:** `GET /api/keykeeper/ciphers/<id>/history` answers 404 without it, as it does for a missing item. Versions come back with their contents only for someone with view item history and see passwords in the same folder of the item (or access to all items); otherwise with who and when only. Someone with history in one folder and passwords in another gets who and when: merging them would show old passwords where neither folder allows it. Clearing refuses with 400, not 403, which signs the web vault out.
- **Imports are checked before anything is saved:** every relationship names an item and a folder of the import, every folder is one the person can add to (or, for someone with access to all items, may make), and someone without access to all items places every item. Each item goes into its folders as soon as it's saved, and an item that can't be saved stops the import there, so no item is ever left outside the folders it named.
- **A database error refuses:** if our rows can't be read, the request fails rather than reading grants as their presets, which can be more open. Upstream's row and ours for a grant are written in one transaction, so they can't disagree.

**The apps enforce see passwords, export, print, and share.** Anyone who can decrypt an item can take it out with their own tools: a member holds the organization's key, because the item's other fields need it, and the server can't send the item without its password. 1Password documents the same limit for the same reason. So the server does what can't be bypassed, and our apps do the rest:

- **See passwords:** one rule in every app: every value is a secret except an allowlist of plain ones, so a value of a new kind, or a field added later, is hidden until someone decides it's plain. The apps keep the list in `core/src/vault.rs` (`Item::is_secret`, `PLAIN_DETAILS`, and the newer kinds' plain details); the web vault and the extension share one copy of it in `libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/hidden-secrets.ts` (`PLAIN_VALUES`), whose test lists exactly the values that are secret, the same as the apps'. An item that doesn't say whether the person may see its passwords (`viewPassword` missing) keeps them hidden: the apps fail closed, and the server says on every item it sends (sync, the item by itself, the organization's admin view, and version history), always from the person's own grid, which an API test holds it to. The web vault's admin console no longer sets it to yes itself. Plain: the item's name, notes, and websites; the username; a card's holder, brand, and expiry; an identity's name, company, username, email, phone, and address; an SSH key's public key and fingerprint; a bank account's bank, name on the account, account type, routing and branch numbers, and contact phone; a driver's license's or passport's names, dates, places, class, and type; text and yes or no custom fields, and linked ones that fill a plain value. Everything else is a secret: the password, the one-time code (its secret and its codes), a card's number and code, an identity's SSN and passport and license numbers, a driver's license's number, a passport's number and national ID number, a bank account's number, PIN, IBAN, and SWIFT code, an SSH key's private key, hidden fields and linked ones that fill a secret, passkeys (never shown), earlier passwords (password history), and version history. Without see passwords, none of them can be shown, copied, or dragged from an item's view, which holds a mask in their place rather than the value; the list's copy buttons, the copy service every copy button goes through (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/hidden-secrets.ts`), and, in the extension, the right-click menu, the autofill shortcut's copy of the one-time code, and the fill menu inside websites (its codes show masked, and still fill) refuse them; the editor can't show, overwrite, or clear any of them (their inputs are locked, and a save keeps them as they are), and won't link a custom field to one. A card's and a bank account's subtitles are built from their plain parts only (the brand, the account type), never the number's last digits (the lists, the right-click menu, the fill menu). A copy of an item (Clone) needs see passwords and share: every page refuses one otherwise, however it's asked for, the address included (`mayCloneItem`), and as a backstop the form builds a copy of an item whose secrets the person can't see from its plain values only, and does so again as it saves (`plainItem`), so a value added to items later is left out too. Filling a website still works, which is the point of the item ("use but don't see"), and it is also where see passwords ends: whatever fills into a page, the page can read, so filling a card puts its number and code in the payment form, readable by that site's scripts. Hidden fields and linked ones that fill a secret go only into password fields, which the page shows masked (`mayFillValue` in the extension's fill rules), and so does the login's password: never into a text field that only looks like a password field. One-time codes fill as they always have, since they last seconds and their fields are text. The extension's "Save login?" and "Update password?" prompts never offer a password the person can't see: not one equal to the password of one of the site's items that hides it, and nothing at all on a site in a tab where such an item filled last (`keykeeper/hidden-fills.ts`), so filling a hidden item and saving what the page sent can't copy its password into an item of their own. The server already holds back what it can: version contents in item history, and moving an item into a folder that would show its passwords.
- **Export:** an export includes only items in folders where the person can export. The export page offers an organization only if they can export one of its folders, and refuses an organization export that would include a folder they can't export. A personal export never includes organization items (upstream).
- **Print:** the web vault's item view has Print only where the person can print the item. The extension doesn't print.
- **Share:** the apps offer copying (clone) only where one of the item's folders lets the person share, and moving an item out of a folder (the item dialog's Folder picker, Add to shared folders) only where that folder does. A copy is a new item to the server, so only the apps can stop it; the move out is a server change, so the server refuses it too. The extension's Add to shared folder needs share on the item, and its Folder picker is locked, saying why ("Your organization doesn't let you move it to another shared folder."), for an item its folder doesn't let the person move.

### Storage and the API

- **Upstream's three fields stay the closest preset:** each grant (`users_collections` for a person, `collections_groups` for a group) keeps `read_only`, `hide_passwords`, and `manage`: manage if the grid manages, otherwise `read_only` is "can't edit" and `hide_passwords` is "can't see passwords". Upstream clients read those, so they show Can view, Can edit, or Can manage and the like, and see each item's `edit`, `viewPassword`, and `permissions.delete`/`restore`, which follow the grid exactly.
- **Our tables** `keykeeper_users_collections` and `keykeeper_collections_groups` hold the other six (create, delete, export, share, print, view item history) for a grant, with the three upstream values it was saved with. They count only while the grant's upstream values are still those; a grant without a row, or whose row no longer matches, is the preset its upstream values name (upstream's "Can edit except passwords" included). Rows go with their grant (`ON DELETE CASCADE`). No migration: nobody has data yet.
- **One object everywhere a grant goes:** every request and response carrying upstream's `{id, readOnly, hidePasswords, manage}` gains `keykeeper: {seePasswords, create, edit, delete, export, share, print, viewHistory, manage}`: making and editing a folder, bulk access, inviting and editing a member, making and editing a group, and the folder, member, and group lists. Each collection in sync carries the person's merged capabilities there. When a request has the object, it decides the grant and the server derives upstream's three fields from it.
- **A grant sent without the object keeps its grid while its fields are unchanged.** Every save resends every grant (a folder's people and groups, a member's folders, a group's folders, bulk access), and upstream clients can't send ours, so the server reads the grants a save replaces before deleting them: a grant whose upstream fields are the ones it had keeps its grid, and one whose fields changed becomes the preset they name, as an upstream client showed it. Our CLI sends each grant's grid back, and refuses a grant whose grid and fields disagree ("make readOnly/hidePasswords/manage match the grid, or remove keykeeper to use the preset"): sent without its grid, an edited grid would be dropped without a word while the fields stayed as they were.

### Who can give what

- **Owners and admins,** including an MSP's admins and admin technicians: anything, in any folder.
- **A folder's managers:** anything in that folder, to anyone in the organization, since they have everything there. Only for the folders they manage.
- **Custom roles that manage members, and MSP technicians with a custom role** ("never more than they have", in "Roles and permissions", now per capability): in a folder, only capabilities they have there themselves, merged over all their ways in; any capability except manage if they have access to all items; never manage. The rule checks the grant that would result, so a request with only upstream's fields is checked as the grant they'd keep or the preset they name. A grant an edit leaves exactly as it was isn't one the role gives, so it isn't checked: re-saving a member someone else gave more doesn't fail, and changes nothing. Editing someone leaves their other folders alone, and custom roles still can't change groups.
- **Technicians and client members are just people in the grid:** a client's admin can give an MSP's technician a folder like anyone else, and a technician without vault items sees only folders given to them.

### The Access page and offboarding

- **The Access page** shows each person's merged capabilities in each folder they reach: the preset's name when it's one, otherwise "Custom" and the capabilities in words. Reaching a folder several ways shows each way with its own. By folder, the same for each person.
- **Removing or revoking someone** still lists the logins whose passwords they could see: in folders where any of their ways gave see passwords. Export, print, and share don't change that list: whoever could see a password could have copied it. So does removing or revoking several people at once (see "Access and offboarding").

### Upstream clients

They keep working, a little less precisely. They show the closest preset and the server enforces the grid, so they can offer something it then refuses (creating where someone can edit but not create), but never get more than the grid allows. Export, print, and share are ours, so an upstream client doesn't hide them, the limit every app-enforced capability has anyway. Saving from an upstream client keeps each grant's grid unless the client changed that grant, which then becomes the preset it showed.

### Where "vault" stays

Only where it means the whole encrypted store and another word would confuse: locking and unlocking ("Your vault is locked", "Unlock your vault"), the vault timeout and its policy ("Vault lock"), encryption and key rotation ("Use for vault encryption"), signing out at the timeout, and the extension's sign-in asking "Where's your vault?" (which server holds the store). Also the web vault's name, which the site, the server, and the apps all use (where upstream now says "web app"). Everywhere else it's Personal, an organization, a folder, the product's name, or your items: the left menu, the item dialog, the admin console's Shared folders, the export and import pages, reports, the event log's wording, and the extension's tabs and folder filter ("Items", "All folders"). The kept strings are listed, each with why, in passbundle-clients' `brand/wording.en.json` (`kept`); the brand step applies the rest to upstream's English strings. Also kept: a server address example ("vault.example.com"), the steps for exporting from Bitwarden, which name Bitwarden's own menus, and the server's MSP audit lines and emails, which aren't screens of ours. Other languages keep upstream's words until they're translated.

### Status and tests

- **Built (October 23, 2026):** the server (`keykeeper_folder_permissions.rs`, its migration, every write path above, folder managers' endpoints), the web vault (the left menu's Personal and organizations with New folder asking where; the item dialog's one Folder picker, grouped by owner, showing the organizations' folders the person can add to, with the item's other folders named under it; the access selector's presets and Custom grid in the shared folder, member, invite, group, and bulk access dialogs; the Access page; export, Print, and share; version history only with view item history), and the words (`brand/wording.en.json`, which the brand step applies to upstream's English strings). The shared code the extension uses enforces export and share there too (`libs/common/.../keykeeper/folder-permissions.ts`).
- **The extension (October 26, 2026):** a folder filter beside the category chips: one button opening every place an item lives, grouped by owner as in the web vault's menu (All folders; Personal and its folders, nested by name, then No folder; each organization and its shared folders), each with how many items the list shows once it's picked, with the category picked now (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/folders-by-owner.ts`). The place picked shows as a chip under the categories ("Lakeside Clinic · Everyone"), whose x clears it. It replaces upstream's three filters (vault, collection, folder) and sets them the same way, so a new item goes where the list shows. Personal can't be picked where an organization's policy keeps items out of it. The item editor has the web vault's one Folder picker, grouped by owner, now with every shared folder the person reaches: those they can't add items to are listed locked, and the web vault shows them so too. See passwords, export, and share are enforced as above.
- **Not yet:** our apps (they have no organization folders yet), and a server-side export endpoint for non-admins (exports come from the person's own synced items).
- **Security review (October 26, 2026), fixed:** a copy (Clone) of an item whose secrets the person couldn't see carried them, since every section of the form refilled itself from the item, and the copy could be started from its address in the web vault and the extension (now refused on every way in, and built from plain values only, twice); editing could link a custom field to a secret, which filling then put into any field of a page (now refused, and hidden and linked secret fields fill only password fields); the newly secret values (SSN, passport, license, account, PIN, IBAN, SWIFT) could be overwritten or cleared blind in the editor (now locked); card and bank subtitles took the digits out of a subtitle rather than leaving them out (now built from plain parts); and an item that didn't say whether its passwords show counted as yes (now no, and the server says on every item).
- **Re-review (October 27, 2026), fixed:** an item from a template copied the template's hidden defaults for someone who couldn't see them (now empty, as in the apps), and naming a custom field `keykeeper.template-definition` turned a note into such a template (now refused); the extension's save prompt could copy a filled hidden password into a new item (now never offered); the login's password filled text fields that only looked like password fields (now only password fields); a few checks still let a missing `viewPassword` count as yes (now all through `maySeeSecrets`); and the admin view's `viewPassword` was a fixed yes (now from the person's grid).
- **Security review (October 23, 2026), fixed:** adding an existing item to another folder needed only create there (now share on the item too, on every path); an upstream-style save replaced custom grids with presets (now kept while the fields are unchanged); one item's `collectionIds` left out read-only folders, so saving the item form took it out of them (now every folder the person reaches); our rows failed open on a database error (now refused); item history merged history and see passwords across folders (now both in one folder); an import could leave items outside every folder (now checked first); refusals that signed members out (now 404 and 400); a folder manager's member list included invitations and group directory ids (now not); an MSP template's source didn't need share (now it does).
- **Re-review (October 23, 2026), fixed:** a custom member turned away on a folder's routes got 403, which signed them out (now 404); a database error checking who manages a folder said "not a manager" (now 500); the CLI dropped a grid that disagreed with its fields (now refused); template copies didn't check share again (now on every run and when copies are recorded); joining with the install command widened an invitation's grant to Everyone (now kept).
- **Tests:** server unit tests for presets, the rules, merging, stale rows, a grant re-saved without its grid, and ways to an item kept apart; an API matrix with a person for each capability on each write path (allowed and refused, with what changed checked), including adding an item to another folder through each path, custom roles and technicians per capability, folder managers (what they read and how they're refused), upstream-only requests and re-saves on every save path, an item's folders outside sync, history split across folders, broken imports, and groups; jest for merging (most open per capability; see passwords off only when every way is off), presets, the grid's rules, export and share decisions (folder assignment only with share), the CLI sending grids back, and the Access page; browser tests for the renamed menu and dialogs and for setting a Custom grid that the server then enforces; jest for the extension's folder filter (order, nesting, counts, picking), its locked Folder picker, Add to shared folder needing share, every way a secret could leave the extension without see passwords, and the one list of plain values, whose secrets are exactly the apps' (an identity's SSN, passport and license numbers, a bank account's number, PIN, IBAN, and SWIFT code, a driver's license's and a passport's numbers, all masked with nothing to show, copy, or count in the list); jest that renders the whole item form, with its real sections, copying each kind of item and checking that none of its secrets reach the server (`clone-hidden-item.spec.ts`), that editing locks the secret inputs and keeps them, and that a linked field can't be pointed at one; jest for clones refused by address in the web vault, the admin console, and the extension, hidden and linked fields filling only password fields, and an item that doesn't say being hidden; an API test that every item the server sends says (`view-password.spec.ts`); and a browser test of a member in the extension (`extension-folder-permissions.spec.ts`): her folders by owner with counts; an item in a folder where she can't see passwords, whose password, code, hidden field, password history, and version history show nothing, can't be copied from the list or the right-click menu (only its username), and whose label drags nothing; a card whose number and code are masked; an identity whose SSN, passport and license numbers are masked, with nothing to show or copy, there and in her web vault; an item she can edit but not share, which the Folder picker won't move and Add to shared folder isn't offered for; and a copy of the login by its address, refused in the extension and in her web vault.

## Folder icons

A shared folder can have an icon and a color of its own, like 1Password's vault icons, so "Banking" and "Servers" are told apart at a glance in a long menu. (Asked for by the owner, November 6, 2026.)

- **What:** one of our own flat glyphs and one of the tiles' tones, drawn as the same flat tile a category has ("The look"): 24 icons (the plain folder, people, bank, money, card, key, lock, shield, server, terminal, code, globe, browser, email, chat, phone, laptop, computer, Wi-Fi, ID card, license, document, star, and help), the categories' glyphs and passbundle.com's (`site/icons.mjs`), and the ten jewel tones, named as people name colors in the picker (blue, sky blue, teal, green, gold, orange, red, pink, purple, gray). Each tone keeps its glyph at 3:1 on its tint over every surface, light and dark, as the categories' do. No uploaded images. Until someone picks one, a folder keeps the plain glyph it always had; picking a color alone gives the folder glyph that color, and an icon alone gets blue.
- **Private:** the choice says something about what's inside ("bank"), so it's encrypted with the organization's key, as the folder's name is: the apps encrypt the names, `{"icon":"bank","tone":"emerald"}`, as an EncString, and the server keeps only that. A name an app doesn't know (a newer icon, say) still shows, as the folder glyph or in gray, and saving a folder never drops it. A look that doesn't open, or isn't one once open, is the plain folder, never an error. Organizations' keys don't rotate, so nothing has to re-encrypt it.
- **Storage:** a column of the folder, `collections.keykeeper_look` (null for the plain folder), the simplest shape: it goes in every answer about the folder (`Collection::to_json`: sync, the folder list, its details, the admin console's list, a save's answer), goes when the folder does, and upstream's rename keeps it, since a save writes back the row it read. Upstream's apps read only the properties they know (`BaseResponse.getResponseProperty`), so the extra `keykeeperLook` changes nothing for them.
- **Setting it:** `PUT /api/keykeeper/organizations/<org>/collections/<id>/look` with `{look: <EncString> | null}`, answered with the folder as a save is. The server checks only its shape (`2.<iv>|<data>|<mac>`, at most 1,024 characters), logs it as the folder being edited, and bumps the revision of everyone who has the folder, as a rename does, so their apps sync. It's our own endpoint, not a field of upstream's save, so the refusal can be ours (below); the web vault sends it after upstream's save, once a new folder has its id, and only when the look changed.
- **Who:** whoever may rename the folder, the same rule upstream's rename route applies: a confirmed member who manages it (owners, admins, managers with access to all items, and anyone whose grid there has manage, directly or through a group; `keykeeper_folder_permissions::manages`). Custom roles don't manage every folder ("Roles and permissions"), so only a manage grant in the folder lets one. Everyone else gets 400, "Only people who can rename this shared folder can change its icon.", the same words for a folder they can't rename, one that isn't there, and another organization's, never 401 or 403, which sign the web vault out. Nobody is refused anything they could read before: the look rides along with the folder in answers they already get.
- **Where it shows:** as a tile in place of the plain glyph, wherever the folder shows: the web vault's menu (24px, under its organization) and its list rows (40px, the web vault's and the admin console's shared folders), the extension's folder filter and the chip for the place picked, and the apps' menus (the computer's sidebar and the phone's Folders cards, `app/lib/folder_looks.dart`). The web vault and the admin console pick it in the folder's dialog (`vault-keykeeper-folder-look-picker`): a preview of the folder as the menu shows it, then Icon and Color, each a group of radio buttons: arrow keys move through a group and pick as they go, Home and End go to its ends, and Tab goes from one group to the next. It's enabled wherever the folder's name is.
- **Saving a folder kept it in the menu (fixed with this):** upstream's web vault reads a save's answer as the folder's access details and took the folder out of the person's own list when the answer didn't say `assigned`, which Vaultwarden's never did, so a shared folder made or renamed from the web vault's menu went missing from it until the next sync. Saves now answer with `assigned` (`Collection::to_json_saved`).
- **Personal folders: later.** The same mechanism would fit (a column beside the folder, upstream's folder format untouched), but a personal folder is encrypted with the account's key, which key rotation re-encrypts through upstream's rotation request, folders included. A look kept beside the folder would stay under the old key unless rotation learned about it, which is key-handling code, with its own design note and vectors. Until then personal folders keep their glyph.
- **Tests:** API (`e2e/tests/api/folder-looks.spec.ts`): a permission matrix setting and clearing a look (owner, admin, a folder manager directly and one through a group allowed; upstream's Manager role with edit, an editor, a viewer, a custom role that manages members, a member without the folder, another organization's owner, and someone not signed in refused), refusals as 400 in the same words whether or not the folder is there, only an encrypted look kept, the look in sync for everyone who has the folder (and not for anyone else), the folder list, its details, and the admin console's list, a rename keeping it, the folder's people's revision bumped, a save's answer saying `assigned`, and a new folder having none. Server unit test for the shape check. Jest: the icons and tones (each once, plain words, the categories' own glyphs and tones, 3:1 on every surface), encrypting only the names and reading them back, anything else as the plain folder, unknown names drawn as the folder glyph or gray, the collection service opening each look with its organization's key beside the SDK's names (and a look under another key as plain), the admin console's folders opening it, `setLook` sending it encrypted and updating the person's own copy, the picker (plain to start, labelled radio groups in plain words, picking by mouse and by arrow keys, one Tab stop per group, disabled with the name), the web vault's menu (the tile on the folder with a look, the glyph on the others), and the extension's folder filter, chip, and places. Browser (`e2e/tests/folder-icons.spec.ts`): an owner makes a family's organization and two shared folders from the web vault's menu, gives one the bank icon and then green by keyboard, sees the tile in the menu and the admin console's list and the plain glyph on the other, changes it to red in the admin console, and the extension, signed in afresh, shows the red bank in its folder filter and in the chip once it's picked. The apps: the core opens a look with the organization's key and leaves one under another key, one that isn't a look, and one that isn't encrypted plain, on the vectors (`core/src/vectors.rs`), and the names' rules (`core/src/places.rs`); the app's tests show the tile in the computer's sidebar and the phone's Folders card, the plain glyph for a look that doesn't open, and every tone at 3:1 (`app/test/app_test.dart`, `look_test.dart`).
- **Status:** built (November 6, 2026): server, web vault, admin console, extension, and the apps.

## Access and offboarding

Shared folders (upstream's collections) belong to the organization, and owners and admins see every item, so nobody leaving strands a vault that no one controls. What was missing was seeing who reaches what, and knowing which passwords to change when someone leaves.

- **Access page (built):** in each organization's admin console, next to Members, for owners, admins, and custom roles that manage members. By person: each member, with their role, MSP staff marked, and whether they're confirmed or revoked, and the shared folders they open, each with the permission in plain words (a preset's name, or Custom with its capabilities; see "Folders and permissions") and how they get it, directly or through which group. Owners, admins, and anyone with access to all items (upstream's custom role that manages all collections, MSP staff with vault items) show "Every item". Expanding someone shows each folder's item count. By shared folder: everyone who reaches it, and who sees every item. One search box covers people, groups, and folders.
- **One folder reached two ways:** for each capability, the more open way wins, and passwords stay hidden only if every way hides them, as the server decides for each item.
- **Removing or revoking someone (built):** the confirmation says what they can open ("Alex can open 3 shared folders with 42 items"). Once it's done, a dialog lists the logins whose passwords they could see: those in folders they opened without "can't see passwords", directly or through a group. A login only in folders where every way hid passwords is left out. Each row has the login, its folders, and Open, which opens it over the list so the list stays while each password is changed. For someone who saw every item, the dialog says so and offers every login in the organization. Copy list copies names and folders only, never a username, password, or anything else in an item. Nothing is stored: the dialog says it won't be shown again and links to the Access page. The same applies from the member dialog's Remove and Revoke, and to bulk remove and revoke (November 2026): the bulk dialog says under each person what they can open, the same sentence, and once it's done, one list has every login whose password any of those removed or revoked could see, with "Who could see it" beside each (and in the copied list). Only the people the server removed or revoked are in it (it reads the member list again after), not anyone already revoked before a bulk revoke, and not anyone never confirmed. It's the same computation, once for all of them, from the same requests: it shows nothing the person removing them couldn't already see on the Access page.
- **In the browser only:** no server changes. It's computed from requests the Members, Groups, and Collections pages already make (members with their folders and groups, groups with their folders, folder details) and the organization's items as the admin console's vault loads them. A custom role is sent only requests its permissions cover: it sees the folders it opens itself (the page says how many it can't see), counts items from its own vault, and shows groups without their names, since listing groups isn't one of its permissions.
- **Left out:** other items someone could see (notes, cards, identities, SSH keys) aren't listed, and the dialog says so; items in the trash; and anyone never confirmed, who never had the organization's key. Groups with upstream's old "access all" setting aren't detected.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the computation (direct and group paths, merging, owners, admins, access to all items, item counts, the confirmation's sentence), for the list (logins only, hidden-password folders left out, every login for someone who saw every item, copied text without secrets), and for several people's lists (each login once, every folder, who could see it, nothing added; only those removed or revoked now; the bulk dialogs' sentences). End to end, a bulk revoke of two members says what each can open and lists both logins with who could see each, and removing them afterward says what they could open before they were revoked (`e2e/tests/access-review.spec.ts`). End to end, on a test server with groups turned on: an organization with two shared folders and a group; a member reaches one folder directly and through the group, and the other only through the group with passwords hidden. The Access page shows the merged permissions, and removing the member lists the right logins.

## Offboarding

Removing someone, or a client removing its MSP, cuts what they could still use, and stays done (security pass, October 2026). Before this, a client that removed its MSP left the MSP's script and provisioning tokens working (and its own apps refreshed the MSP's script copies with each new password), a technician the client removed came back with the MSP's next staff change, an MSP admin the client revoked kept running it from the console, and someone removed could rejoin with the install command they kept and be let in with no click.

**Removing an MSP.** A client's own owner removes its MSP from the Members page. In one step:
- the MSP's people lose their memberships, and the client remembers each of them as removed (below);
- script tokens they made are revoked, with their copies; the provisioning token is revoked if one of them made it; event destinations they set up for the client are deleted; and every staff seal in the client goes;
- the MSP's install command stops working with it (the client makes its own), and how people join, the preset, and the Everyone collection stay as the client's own.

The owner then sees what went: who lost access, each token and destination by name, and the install command. Single sign-on stays as it is, since turning it off could lock people out, but the owner is pointed to it to review, with who last changed it and whether that was the MSP.

**Removing or revoking one of the MSP's people.** The client's own people remove or revoke them on the Members page (upstream's rules: only owners remove or revoke owners and admins), and it holds:
- the client remembers it (`removed_members`, with the MSP and whether it was a revoke), so the MSP's staff changes (access, role, a new client for everyone) never add them there again, change their role there, or confirm them;
- their staff seal goes, so no app lets them back in on its own, and when they're removed, the event destinations they set up there are deleted;
- the MSP's console shows them as removed or revoked by the client, on the client's row and on their staff row, and its Activity says so;
- only the client's own people undo it: inviting them again, or restoring someone they revoked. The MSP's admins can't invite them from the client's Members page, or restore them.

When the MSP's own people remove one of theirs from a client, nothing is remembered: that's the MSP managing its staff.

**The client sets its MSP's people's roles by removing or revoking them, never by changing their role** (decided October 2026). The MSP's console sets each person's role in every client, and would set a change back, so the client changing one (type, access to all items, or a custom role) is refused with a message that points to removing them, or to Remove MSP. Changing their collections or groups is fine.

**What the client controls** (decided October 2026, enforced by the server):
- The MSP's people (anyone whose membership came from the MSP) can't remove, revoke, demote, or recover (account recovery) the client's own owners: confirmed owners the MSP didn't add.
- Once the client has an owner of its own, only its own owners invite owners or make someone one (November 2026). Otherwise one of the MSP's admins could invite a second account of their own as an owner, which the MSP didn't add and so counts as the client's own, and use it to remove or demote the client's real owners.
- Deleting the client needs one of its own owners while it has any.
- A client with no owners of its own (one the MSP runs alone) is the MSP's to run: its admins keep all of these powers there.
- The client's own owners can always remove the MSP.

**A client's removal or revocation holds against the MSP's staff changes** (November 2026). Someone who joins the MSP's staff is kept out of each client that removed or revoked whoever invited them, recorded as removed by that client: otherwise an admin a client revoked could invite a second account of their own, which joins only the clients where its inviter has a place, but the next staff change another admin makes for it would add it everywhere. Only the client's own people invite them there. And the MSP's activity log no longer shows anyone the entries about a client that removed or revoked them (a client that removed its MSP isn't its client any more, and the MSP keeps its history of it).

**MSP-console actions on a client need your own place there.** Every action the console takes on one client needs the caller's own confirmed, never revoked, membership there as an owner or admin, besides their role in the MSP: the install command (reading it, for a technician any confirmed membership, since its access is the MSP's choice; replacing it; its domains), seals and join secrets, the Templates collection and template copies, and default policies (a client where the caller has no place is named as refusing, and keeps its policy). Changing the MSP's joining default leaves such a client's setting as it was, as its own. Staff changes add people to, and change their role in, only the clients where the person making the change has a place, and a new staff member joins the clients where whoever invited them has one; removals happen everywhere. So a client that revokes one of an MSP's admins is out of that admin's reach, while the MSP's other admins carry on.

**Tokens and event destinations stop when their maker loses access.** A script token or provisioning token works only while whoever made it is still a confirmed owner or admin of the organization (for an MSP's person, still an admin of the MSP), with an account that's on, checked on every use, not once. It's refused like any wrong token, its read is logged as refused for that reason, the Settings and Provisioning pages say it stopped, and apps are never asked to refresh its copies. Making its maker an admin again makes it work again. A script read is refused too once the organization is gone (this server has no switch that disables an organization).

An event destination is checked the same way on every delivery (November 2026), so one whose maker left the MSP's staff, or was demoted, before a client removed its MSP stops at once: an organization's destination sends only while whoever set it up is still a confirmed owner or admin there (for an MSP's person, still an admin of the MSP), and an MSP's feed only while its maker is still one of the MSP's admins, and never a client's events once that client removed or revoked them. Their account must be on, and once it's deleted the destination sends nothing. What it may not send is dropped rather than kept (it's still in the event log), the console shows it as "Stopped: its maker left", and making its maker an admin again makes it send again; otherwise someone deletes it, and sets it up again if it's still needed.

**Rejoining after a removal.** The organization remembers everyone its admins, its directory (provisioning), or its API key's import removed, and everyone who left it on their own (November 2026: leaving had skipped this, so someone who left and asked to join again with the install command was let in with no click). One of its MSP's people who leaves stays out of it until someone there invites them again, as the MSP's staff changes don't add them back. If they ask to join again with the install command they kept, they wait for an admin to approve them, however the organization lets people in; the pending lists say `autoConfirm: false`, and the owners' email says someone is waiting. An admin's approval, a new invitation from the organization's own people, or letting them in after the directory invites them again (which ends in an approval) settles it. After any removal, the Members page offers to replace the install command's token, and an MSP's console offers it on the client's row and in its install command, with how many people were removed since the token was made (`removedSinceToken`); a client of an MSP is told to ask the MSP.

**First sign-in with single sign-on.** Someone invited who signs in with single sign-on for the first time sets a master password, which upstream's server takes as accepting the invitation, without the account recovery key. Where the organization enrolls everyone in account recovery, ours leaves them invited at that step; the app's enrollment request, which follows at once, accepts the invitation with the key, as an emailed invitation's acceptance does, for an account its single sign-on linked. Until then nobody can confirm them. MSP staff, who are never recoverable, join as before.

**Status:** built (October 2026): the server (`keykeeper_offboarding.rs`, `removed_members` and the provisioning token's maker in a migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL, and checks in the MSP, enrollment, seals, joining, policies, templates, script access, provisioning, event streaming, and upstream member routes), the client's removal summary and the install command prompt in the web vault, and the MSP console's removed-by-the-client states. The follow-ups (November 2026): leaving remembered as a removal, owners granted only by the client's own owners, event destinations checked on every delivery, and a client's revocation holding against the MSP's staff changes and its activity log. **Tests:** server unit tests on a real database of their own (`DbPool::for_test`), one for each follow-up, that fail without it; API tests that began as the security review's scripts (`e2e/tests/api/offboarding.spec.ts`: removing an MSP and what it revokes, tokens and their makers, event destinations and their makers, removed and revoked staff staying out, a revoked MSP admin and the staff they invite afterward, the client's owners protected and granted only by them, the Templates collection and copies, the install command prompt) and mail tests for what needs email (`e2e/tests/mail/offboarding.spec.ts`: rejoining with a kept command after a removal or after leaving, the directory's removals, recovering the client's owners, first single sign-on with account recovery, invitation email limits); unit tests for the words in the web vault.

## Provisioning from Entra ID

People join and leave a client's Microsoft 365 every week, and adding and removing them by hand is an MSP's daily chore. With provisioning on, Microsoft Entra ID's provisioning service keeps the client's PassBundle organization in step over SCIM 2.0: someone assigned to the PassBundle app in Entra is invited, someone turned off or unassigned loses access, and someone deleted leaves. Entra's groups can become PassBundle groups with the same members. Built from RFC 7643 (the SCIM schema), RFC 7644 (the protocol), and Microsoft's public documentation for SCIM endpoints, never from another product's code (CLEAN_ROOM.md). Any SCIM 2.0 client should work; Entra is the one we test against.

### Setting it up

- **Where:** the organization's Settings, then Provisioning, for its owners and admins. For an MSP's client, its MSP's admins and the client's own owners and admins, but not technicians, as for script tokens. Anyone else gets "not found", never 403 (upstream's web vault signs out on 403).
- **What it shows:** the organization's SCIM URL, `https://<server>/scim/v2/<organization id>`, "Create token", and once there is one, when it was made and by whom, when it was last used and from where ("last sync"), Replace token, and Revoke.
- **In Entra** (the page says this in a few steps): Enterprise applications, New application, Create your own application, "Integrate any other application you don't find in the gallery". Then Provisioning, mode Automatic (or New configuration), Tenant URL = the SCIM URL, Secret Token = the token, Test connection, Save. Assign the users and groups who should be in the organization, and start provisioning. Entra's first cycle takes a while; after that it syncs about every 40 minutes. Adding `?aadOptscim062020` to the Tenant URL turns on Entra's more standard PATCH requests; we accept both.

### The token

- **Made by the server:** 32 bytes from the operating system's random generator (`ring`'s `SystemRandom`), written as `pbscim1_` and 43 base64url characters: 256 bits of entropy. The prefix lets secret scanners and people recognize it, and the version leaves room for a later format.
- **Shown once:** only in the answer to the request that made it. The page shows it with a copy button and says it won't be shown again.
- **Kept only as a hash:** the server stores the SHA-256 of the whole token (base64). A 256-bit random secret needs no slow hash: there's nothing to guess.
- **One per organization.** Create makes one. Replace makes a new one and the old one stops at once (Entra's requests fail until the new one is pasted in, then Entra retries). Revoke deletes it: provisioning stops, and Entra quarantines the job after repeated failures.
- **Checking a request:** the server reads `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, requires the token's shape, finds the stored hash of the organization named in the URL, and compares the SHA-256 of what was presented with it in constant time (`crypto::ct_eq`, the `subtle` crate). It never looks a token up across organizations: the URL's organization picks the one row a token can match, so a token only ever works at its own organization's URL. Every failure (no header, another scheme, a malformed or wrong token, an organization with no token, an organization that doesn't exist) is the same 401 SCIM error with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`, so the answer never says whether an organization exists.
- **Never in logs:** the server's request log leaves out SCIM query strings (filters carry names and emails) and never logs headers. Event and activity entries say a token was made, replaced, or revoked, and by whom, never its value.
- **Limits:** 600 requests a minute per organization; 200 new invitations a minute and 2,000 a day (each sends an email, so a stolen token can't make the server a source of spam); and 60 refused tokens a minute per address. Past a limit, 429 with `Retry-After: 60`; Entra retries later. Every 1,024 requests the limiters forget addresses whose limits have filled back up, as the login limiters do, so requests from many addresses can't grow them without bound.
- **The same work either way:** for an organization with no token, the presented token is still hashed and compared, in constant time, with a stand-in as long as a real hash, so timing doesn't say whether an organization has one.
- **Last sync:** the time and address of the last accepted request, written at most once a minute.
- **Only while its maker manages the organization (October 2026):** every request checks that whoever made the token is still a confirmed owner or admin there (and for an MSP's person, still an admin of the MSP), with an account that's on. Otherwise it's refused like any wrong token, and the Provisioning page says the token stopped. See "Offboarding".

### What a token can and can't do

This is the whole of what a stolen token is worth, in its own organization only.

**It can:**
- list the organization's members as SCIM users: their account email, what Entra sent about them (name, emails, externalId, and so on), and whether they're on; and the groups provisioning made, with their members.
- invite any email address as a regular member, which also lets that address make an account on a server where only invited people can sign up (with email, from the invitation's link). An invitation alone gives nothing in the organization: the organization's key comes only from an owner's or admin's app, which lets in someone only the directory invited only after an owner or admin lets them in, or when they join with the install command at the client's verified email domains (see "Getting in").
- turn regular members off and back on, and remove them. It turns back on only people provisioning turned off.
- make, rename, and delete the groups provisioning made, and change which regular members are in them. That changes which shared folders they reach through those groups, which an owner or admin gives each group in PassBundle.

**It can't:**
- read any vault item, key, or other encrypted data. No SCIM endpoint returns any, and SCIM never gets or gives a key.
- let anyone in: give the organization's key, confirm anyone, make or read seals, or read the install command or its join secret.
- make or replace tokens, or change any setting or policy.
- add, change, turn off, or remove owners, admins, custom roles, anyone with access to every item, or an MSP's staff (refused with 403, below).
- give a group access to shared folders, give anyone a role, or touch a group made in PassBundle.
- reach another organization: the token is checked against the URL's organization only.

### Users

| Entra sends | PassBundle does |
|---|---|
| `GET /Users?filter=userName eq "..."` | Finds the member Entra knows by that userName, or a member whose account email it is. |
| `POST /Users` | Invites them as a regular member, or links someone already in the organization. 201, or 409 for a userName already provisioned. |
| `GET /Users/<id>` | The member, on or off. |
| `PATCH /Users/<id>` | Keeps the new attributes; `active` false or true turns them off or on. 200 with the user. |
| `PUT /Users/<id>` | The same, replacing what's kept. |
| `DELETE /Users/<id>` | Removes them from the organization. 204. |

- **Who SCIM sees:** every member of the organization, so Entra matches people who were there before provisioning instead of inviting them twice. A user's id is their membership's id.
- **What's kept:** what Entra sent (name, displayName, emails, title, the enterprise extension, and so on) as it sent it, in `scim_users`, returned as sent. Attributes sent as null are left out. Never kept: a password, roles, entitlements, groups. At most 16 KiB per person.
- **Matching:** filters on userName, externalId, `emails[type eq "work"].value`, and id, with `eq`, `ne`, `co`, `sw`, `ew`, `gt`, `ge`, `lt`, `le`, `pr`, `and`, `or`, `not`, and value paths in brackets. Attribute names and operators in any case; values in any case, except `id` and `externalId`, which RFC 7643 makes case exact. A member Entra hasn't linked shows their account email as their userName.
- **Who's invited:** the work email Entra sends (`emails[type eq "work"]`, which Entra fills from the user's mailbox), else the primary or first email, else the userName when it's an email address (Entra fills userName from the user principal name). Without any, 400 saying so. An account's email never changes through SCIM; a new userName is kept for matching only.
- **Someone already in the organization** (that email's account is a member): a regular member is linked, and their membership isn't changed. Linked to two Entra users at once: 409. Someone provisioning never changes (below) isn't linked: 409, saying so; Entra matches them by their account email as userName instead.
- **A userName is never another member's email:** creating or renaming someone to another member's account email is 409, so the directory can't take the name an owner or anyone else is matched by.
- **Always a regular member:** roles in the request are ignored; making owners, admins, and custom roles stays in PassBundle.
- **The Everyone collection:** people provisioning invites get the organization's Everyone collection, if it has one (the Business preset), as people who join with the install command do.

**Getting in.** Joining means getting the organization's key from an owner's or admin's app, which does it on its own only when something the server can't forge vouches for the person (see "Joins a hacked server can't forge"). An admin's browser seals the invitations they send from the Members page; Entra's requests reach only the server, and nothing in them can be checked by an app, so a hacked server could claim Entra invited an address of its own. So invitations from provisioning carry no seal (an earlier seal for that address is removed), and:
- **With the install command (the MSP way):** someone who joins with the client's command, limited to its email domains, with a verified address there, is let in without a click: the extension's join proof vouches for them, as for anyone who joins with the command. Unlike an admin's invitation, the directory's doesn't exempt the address from the command's domains: otherwise a stolen provisioning token plus a command copied off any machine would let in any address.
- **By the emailed invitation, or by signing up on a server without email:** they accept as anyone invited does (signing up first if they have no account; without email, upstream's rule lets the invited address sign up and joins them at once), then wait to be let in. The Provisioning page lists everyone the directory invited whom nothing vouches for yet; an owner or admin looks at the list, ticks the people they recognize, and Let them in, and their browser seals those invitations exactly as if they'd sent them from the Members page. Their apps then let them in in the background, before or after the person accepts. The Members page's Confirm, with the fingerprint, still works for one person.
- **Who hears about it:** the server treats these as waiting for approval (`confirms_automatically`), so the pending list says so and apps don't try them, and the inviter's email (the billing email, since nobody in PassBundle sent it) is upstream's "accepted your invitation", not "joined".
- **Why not let them in automatically:** sealing the directory's invitations by itself (say, every address at the client's domains) would let a hacked server get the organization's key by making up an address at those domains. An admin looking at the list once, or the install command's secret, is what a hacked server can't fake.

**Turning off and on.**
- `active` false (Entra sends this when someone is turned off, unassigned, or deleted in Entra, and the string "False" without its compliance flag) is the Members page's Revoke: no access, but the membership, its groups, and its folders stay, so turning them back on restores everything, as Microsoft asks of a soft delete. Their own vault is theirs throughout.
- `active` true is Restore, with upstream's checks (single organization, two-step login): refused with 409 and upstream's reason if they fail.
- Provisioning turns back on only people it turned off, and only while that's the last word on them. Someone an owner or admin revoked in PassBundle stays off until an owner or admin turns them on (403), even someone provisioning turned off first, then an admin turned on and off again: an owner's or admin's revoke or restore makes theirs the last word (server security pass, October 2026; before, the directory's earlier turning off still counted, so it could undo an admin's revoke).

**Removing.** `DELETE` (Entra sends it when a user is permanently deleted, 30 days after a soft delete) is the Members page's Remove: the organization's items stay with it, and the person's own vault stays theirs.

**People provisioning never changes:** owners, admins, custom roles, anyone with access to every item, and an MSP's staff. They're listed and matched by their account email, so Entra doesn't invite them again, and they're read-only: every PATCH, PUT, and DELETE for them is refused with 403 and a sentence saying why, nothing Entra sends about them is kept, and group changes leave them out. The one thing noted is whether Entra asked to turn them off, keyed by their own account email, never by a userName the directory chose. When Entra asks to turn one off or remove them, the Provisioning page lists them ("Entra turned these people off; remove them here if they've left"), so someone who left the company doesn't keep an owner's or a technician's access unnoticed.

### Groups

- **Made by Entra:** `POST /Groups` makes an ordinary PassBundle group with no shared folders and no access to every item; an owner or admin gives it folders in PassBundle. Its externalId (Entra's object id) is upstream's group external id.
- **Names are unique** in the organization, in any case, as Entra matches groups by displayName: a name another group has is 409.
- **Only groups provisioning made** are visible to SCIM. Groups made in PassBundle are never read, changed, or removed by it.
- **Members:** `PATCH` adds and removes members in the shapes Entra sends: `add` with `path: "members"` and a list of `{ "value": id }`; `remove` the same way, or with `members[value eq "<id>"]`; `replace` with the whole list. 204, as Microsoft asks. An id that isn't a member of the organization is 400. People provisioning never changes are left out of every change. A regular member the directory puts in a group before they're confirmed gets nothing from it until an owner's or admin's app confirms them and an owner or admin gives the group shared folders.
- **Rename and externalId:** `replace` on `displayName` or `externalId`. A renamed or changed group in PassBundle is overwritten at Entra's next change to it.
- **Remove:** `DELETE` removes the group; its members lose what they reached through it.
- **Reading:** `GET /Groups?filter=displayName eq "..."`, `externalId`, `id`, and member checks (`id eq "..." and members[value eq "..."]`); `excludedAttributes=members` is honored.

### The protocol

- **Discovery:** `ServiceProviderConfig` (PATCH yes; bulk, sort, ETags, and password changes no; filters up to 200 results; the bearer token), `ResourceTypes` (User, Group), and `Schemas` (User, Group, and the enterprise User extension). All need the token.
- **Lists:** a ListResponse, `startIndex` from 1, `count` 100 unless asked for fewer, at most 200, with `totalResults`; `attributes` and `excludedAttributes`.
- **Errors:** RFC 7644's error JSON, with the status as a string and `scimType` where one applies (`invalidFilter`, `invalidSyntax`, `invalidPath`, `invalidValue`, `mutability`, `noTarget`, `uniqueness`), as `application/scim+json`.
- **Entra's quirks:** `op` in any case (Entra sends "Add", "Replace", "Remove"); attribute names in any case; `active` as a boolean or "True"/"False"; replace without a path whose value has paths as keys (`"name.givenName"`, enterprise URNs); `emails[type eq "work"].value` creating the work email when there's none; unknown query parameters such as `aadOptscim062020` ignored; resource names in the path in any case. Bodies up to 1 MiB.

### Events

- **The organization's event log:** invited, revoked, restored, removed, group created, changed, and deleted, and a member's groups changed, with "SCIM" as who acted (upstream's system user 1, which the web vault names) and Entra's address.
- **An MSP's activity log:** a client's token made, replaced, and revoked, and people let in, with who did it.
- **Not logged:** the token, request bodies, and filters.

### Tests

- **Unit (server):** the filter parser and matcher (Entra's queries, the rest of the grammar, case rules, refusals), PATCH on Microsoft's documented request bodies with and without its flag, the token's shape and hash, which memberships provisioning may change, choosing the address to invite, what's kept of a user, paging, and attribute selection.
- **API (`e2e/tests/api/provisioning.spec.ts`):** Microsoft's request shapes replayed in order (Test Connection, create, duplicate, match by userName, externalId, and work email, get, update multi-valued and single-valued, the flag's replace without a path, disable with "False", enable, PUT, delete), the token (shown once, never listed, replaced, revoked, every wrong token the same 401), scope (another organization's token, ids, and groups), people provisioning never changes (owners, admins, a custom role, an MSP's admin and technician), someone already a member, groups (Microsoft's create, find by name, rename, both ways of removing members, groups made in PassBundle out of reach), discovery and paging, the event log, and letting someone in.
- **Mail (`e2e/tests/mail/provisioning.spec.ts`):** the emailed invitation and the owner's "accepted" email, and the install command's domains for the directory's invitations.
- **Not yet, needs a real tenant:** Entra itself. See "Status".

### Status

Built: the server (`src/api/core/keykeeper_scim/`, `keykeeper_provisioning.rs`, `db/models/keykeeper_scim.rs`, a migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL), the admin console's Provisioning page, and the MSP console's per-client status. Checking it against a real Entra tenant waits on the owner's Microsoft 365 tenant (docs/prelaunch.md, "Waiting on you"): Test Connection, Provision on demand, a first cycle with groups, turning someone off, a permanent delete, and a revoked token's quarantine. Not built: OAuth client credentials in place of the long-lived token, limiting the endpoint to Microsoft's published address ranges, Entra's on-premises agent, a "From Microsoft Entra ID" badge on the Members page, and other directories (Okta, Google Workspace, JumpCloud speak SCIM 2.0 too, untested).

## Sign in with Microsoft Entra ID

Each organization, and so each of an MSP's clients, can let its people sign in with their Microsoft 365 work account. Each one connects its own Entra tenant: there's no PassBundle-wide identity provider, and one organization's sign-in never signs anyone in to another's. Single sign-on proves who someone is; their master password still unlocks their vault, so nothing about keys changes (upstream's "SSO with master password"). Built from the OpenID Connect Core 1.0 and Discovery specs, RFC 7636 (PKCE), and Microsoft's public documentation ("OpenID Connect on the Microsoft identity platform", "ID token claims reference", "Optional claims reference", "Signing key rollover", "Register an application"), never from another product's code (CLEAN_ROOM.md). Vaultwarden's own OpenID Connect code (one provider for the whole server, accounts matched by email) stays in the server for merges, but the server refuses `SSO_ENABLED`.

### Setting it up

- **Where:** the organization's Settings, Single sign-on, for its owners and admins, and for an MSP's client its MSP's admins (not technicians), as for provisioning. Anyone else gets "not found", never 403 (upstream's web vault signs out on 403).
- **What it asks for:** turn it on; the SSO identifier people type (3 to 40 lowercase letters, digits, and hyphens, unique on the server); the Directory (tenant) ID and the Application (client) ID, both GUIDs; and the app's client secret. The page shows the redirect URI to paste into Entra (`https://<server>/identity/connect/oidc-signin`), a link that starts the sign-in, the setup steps (docs/sso-entra.md has them in full), the email domains, and the accounts linked so far.
- **Turning it on checks the tenant:** the server fetches the tenant's OpenID configuration and refuses a tenant Microsoft doesn't know, and the personal-accounts tenant (`9188040d-...`).
- **The secret is write-only:** sent once with Save, never in any answer. The page says when it was saved; saving with the field empty keeps it.
- **Changing the tenant** removes the links to the old tenant's people, who can't sign in here anymore, and ends their sessions everywhere (their refresh tokens stop working). It's refused while Require single sign-on is on, since everyone would have to link again at once. Turning single sign-on off ends the sessions of everyone linked.
- **Who isn't linked yet:** the page, and the Require single sign-on policy's dialog, list the members the policy covers (or would) who aren't linked, and say what happens to them when it's on (below).

### How a sign-in works

The server is the OpenID client of the organization's tenant: it holds the app's secret, redeems the code, and checks the ID token. The web vault is the server's client, with its own state and PKCE challenge, as upstream's apps already expect, so upstream's sign-in pages work unchanged.

1. The person types their email and chooses Use single sign-on. If the email's domain is verified (below), the web vault finds the organization's identifier by itself (`POST /api/organizations/domain/sso/verified`); otherwise they type it. `GET /identity/sso/prevalidate` checks it.
2. `GET /identity/connect/authorize` (the web vault and the browser extension, back to the web vault's `sso-connector.html` only; the command-line tool, back to its own listener only, `http://localhost:<port>` for the ports 8065 to 8070 that upstream's tool tries, as a native app's loopback redirect (RFC 8252); no other app; at most as often as pre-login per address). For the command-line tool, authorize first answers with a page of the server's own that asks (which organization, which listener, and to go on only for a sign-in the person just started) and sets a cookie for it (HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, ten minutes); only that page's Continue, a same-origin POST to `/identity/connect/authorize/confirm` with its one-time token (kept in the server's memory, as a hash) and that cookie, goes on, as for the other apps (that page's content security policy lets its form go on to the tenant's sign-in host and the tool's listeners and nowhere else, since browsers hold every redirect after a form to it): the server keeps the app's state, client, redirect URI, and PKCE challenge, makes its own state, nonce, and PKCE verifier (32 random bytes each), sets a cookie tying the answer to this browser (HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, path `/identity/connect/`, ten minutes, and Secure whenever the server's own address is HTTPS, never decided from a request header), and sends the browser to the tenant's authorization endpoint with `response_type=code`, `scope=openid profile email`, `response_mode=query`, the state, nonce, and S256 challenge.
3. Microsoft sends the browser back to `/identity/connect/oidc-signin`. The server takes the sign-in by its state, once, from the browser with the cookie, and right away redeems Microsoft's code at the tenant's token endpoint with the client secret, its PKCE verifier, and the redirect URI, then checks the ID token (below). Whatever that comes to, the browser goes back to the web vault with a one-time code of the server's own and the app's state; the outcome is told when the code is redeemed, so the person sees why.
4. The web vault redeems that code at `/identity/connect/token` (`grant_type=authorization_code`) with its PKCE verifier, the same client, and the same redirect URI. The server decides which account it is (below), runs two-step login as for a password, and answers as a password sign-in does: the account key wrapped by the master key, which the person's master password unwraps. Someone invited who has no account yet is asked to set a master password, upstream's first single sign-on.
5. The session is PassBundle's own, refreshed as a password session is, while the account is still linked in an organization whose single sign-on is on (checked at every refresh). Microsoft's tokens aren't kept.
6. **Linking an account** (upstream's Link SSO, in an organization's menu in the vault, or a linking session, under Require single sign-on below), in four steps:
   1. **A ticket:** `POST /api/accounts/sso/user-identifier`, from a signed-in session with its master password hash (a one-time code only for an account without a master password, since a code proves only the mailbox; never an API key's token, which proves no password), or from a linking session, which already proved the password and two-step login. 32 random bytes, five minutes, once, kept as a hash. The answer sets a cookie (HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, path `/identity/connect/`) that the ticket only works with, so a ticket finished in another browser (say, a coworker's, where Microsoft would sign the coworker in) links nothing and is gone. A linking session's ticket works only for its organization.
   2. **Microsoft:** the web vault passes the ticket to authorize as `user_identifier`, and the server adds `prompt=select_account`, so the person picks the Microsoft account instead of the browser using whichever one is signed in.
   3. **A pending link:** the callback checks the ID token as for any sign-in, and holds the result with the account and the session that asked (a device, or the linking session). The browser goes to the web vault's `/sso-link` page with a one-time code. A link's code is never redeemed as a sign-in.
   4. **Confirmation:** the page shows which Microsoft account (its name and email), which PassBundle account, and which organization (`POST /api/accounts/sso/link/pending`), and the person confirms (`POST /api/accounts/sso/link/confirm`). Both need the session that asked: another account's, or another session of the same account, gets "expired" and doesn't use up the code. Only then is the link made, under the rules below. A linking session ends with it.

   Unlink SSO (`DELETE /api/accounts/sso/<organization>`) undoes a link and signs the account out everywhere. Leaving or being removed from the organization deletes the link too, so sessions it started stop refreshing.

### What's validated

The ID token (`api/core/keykeeper_sso/token.rs`, a pure function with a unit test per check):
- **Signature:** RS256 only (never `none`, never an HMAC keyed with a public key), with a key the tenant publishes at its own `jwks_uri`, by `kid`. An unknown `kid` fetches the keys again, at most every five minutes per tenant, as Microsoft's key rollover guidance says.
- **`iss`** is exactly `https://login.microsoftonline.com/<tenant id>/v2.0`, the tenant's own issuer (the discovery document must name the same issuer, and its endpoints must be on the same host).
- **`tid`** is the organization's tenant, and **`ver`** is `2.0`.
- **`aud`** is exactly the app's client id, one audience; `azp`, if present, agrees.
- **`exp`**, **`nbf`**, and **`iat`** are in range, with five minutes of clock skew, and `iat` no older than ten minutes.
- **`nonce`** is the one this sign-in sent (constant-time compare); a missing one fails.
- **`oid`** is there and is a GUID.

And around it: the state is single use (taken before anything else happens) and expires in ten minutes; the cookie must match (so Microsoft's answer can't be replayed into another browser, and a sign-in started elsewhere can't be finished here); the server's one-time code is single use, expires with the sign-in, and needs the app's PKCE verifier, client, and redirect URI; states, codes, and the cookie are kept only as SHA-256 hashes; the server follows no redirects from Microsoft and reads at most 256 KiB; the request log leaves out the identity routes' queries.

### How accounts link

People are identified by Entra's immutable tenant id and object id (`tid` + `oid`), per organization (`keykeeper_sso_links`), never by email. Entra's `email` claim isn't verified (Microsoft: "never use it for authorization"), and an Entra admin can set a user's mail to anything; matching accounts by it is how the nOAuth class of account takeover happens. The rules (`link.rs`, a pure decision with a unit test per rule):

- **Already linked:** the person signs in to that account, whatever email Entra sends now, while the account is still within the rules below. A spoofed email never moves a link.
- **First sign-in, by email, only for an invitation without an account:** the email Entra sends is only a suggestion, and it links only an invitation whose account has no keys yet (invited by email or by provisioning, no master password set), never an account that has keys: anyone who can write a user's mail in the tenant (User Administrators, Exchange admins, apps with User.ReadWrite.All, on-premises AD through Entra Connect) could otherwise sign in to an owner's account. The email must be plain ASCII (lowercasing other characters can turn one into a letter: U+212A becomes k) and vouched for: Entra's `xms_edov` claim says its domain owner is verified, or, when Entra doesn't say either way, it's at a domain this organization verified with DNS and the person isn't a guest in the tenant (`acct` 1, or an `idp` other than the issuer). `xms_edov` false is never vouched for. The invitation must be this organization's (invited, joined, or confirmed, not turned off).
- **An account with keys links from its own session** (Link SSO, above): its person proved their master password (or their password and two-step login, for a linking session), picked their Microsoft account, and confirmed the link, so the email doesn't matter. The account must be a member of this organization, not turned off, or in no organization at all with an email at one of its verified domains; the Microsoft account must not be linked to another account here. A sign-in by email to such an account is refused and says how to link it.
- **Every link is told:** the account gets an email naming the organization ("If you didn't do this, tell its administrator"), and the event log has the person's first single sign-on (1510).
- **Never:** an account on any MSP's staff, by email or from a session (their account reaches every client, so one client's directory could otherwise sign in to all of them; they sign in with their master password); an account already linked to another Entra person here, or a Microsoft account already linked to another account; a member of another organization who isn't a member of this one; a disabled account. Nobody is made an account: someone with no account and no invitation is told to ask for one.
- **Each refusal says why** on the sign-in page ("isn't a member of Acme Dental", "is already linked to a different Microsoft account") and in the event log.
- **Unlinking:** the settings page lists linked accounts; unlinking one (say, after the person's Entra account was recreated) signs it out everywhere, and it links again the way it did the first time.

### Email domains

- **Proving one:** add the domain, and the page shows a TXT record for the domain itself, `passbundle-verification=<43 random characters>`. Verify asks the DNS (no cache) and verifies the domain when the exact value is there. Domains are exact (a subdomain is its own) and at most 20 per organization.
- **One organization each:** any organization may add a domain, but only one can verify it (a unique index decides a race), and a domain verified elsewhere can't be added.
- **What it does:** typing an email there finds this organization's sign-in (the answer is only its SSO identifier, since anyone can ask; never the organization's name), and emails there are vouched for when an invitation links, for members of the tenant. Removing it stops both; accounts already linked keep signing in.
- **It trusts the configured tenant:** a verified domain vouches for emails at it from whichever tenant the organization's settings name. An admin who points the settings at a tenant they control can make its users' emails at the domain look vouched for; that still reaches only invitations without an account, in this organization, and every link is emailed and logged. Admins are trusted with the organization's settings, as with any SSO.

### Require single sign-on

Upstream's policy (type 4), enforced by the server:
- **Who:** members who joined (accepted or confirmed) as anything but an owner: regular members, custom roles, and the organization's own admins. **Never:** owners, memberships that came through an MSP, and staff of the MSP that manages this organization. Decided per organization: being on some other MSP's staff (or making one) never gets anyone out of it. So an organization always has someone who can sign in with a password and fix its settings. Someone the policy covers who is also on another MSP's staff can't use the client's single sign-on either (it never signs in MSP staff), so they're stuck until an owner makes them an owner there or removes them; the sign-in refusal says so.
- **How:** a password sign-in (including a sign-in approved from another device) that checks out answers `SsoOrganizationIdentifier`, and the apps send the person to that sign-in; a wrong password says nothing about it. A password session ends at its next refresh. A sign-in with another organization's single sign-on doesn't count: it's refused, naming the organization whose sign-in to use (to the person; the event says only "another organization").
- **Members not linked yet aren't locked out:** without a link, single sign-on can't sign in an account that has keys, and a password would only send them there. So their password sign-in goes on through two-step login and then answers `SsoOrganizationIdentifier` with `KeykeeperSsoLinkSession`: a linking session (32 random bytes, kept as a hash, fifteen minutes, one at a time per account), not a login. It isn't a login token, so every request but the link ones refuses it (no sync, no vault, nothing else, and no refresh token); it can get a ticket for that organization's single sign-on and confirm the link, and it ends when the link is made. The web vault keeps it in the tab and takes the person to `/sso-link`: Microsoft, confirm, then single sign-on, then their master password unlocks. A sign-in approved from another device proves no password, so it can't start one. This covers the policy turning on, an unlink while it's on, and anyone who joins later. The other apps (the extension, for now) say to link in the web vault first.
- **API keys:** an API key sign-in (the CLI's `client_credentials`) skips the policy, as upstream's does. With the policy on, the Single sign-on page offers to replace the covered members' API keys (`POST .../sso/rotate-api-keys`; owners' and IT provider staff's stay), so old keys stop working; members get new ones from their account settings after signing in with single sign-on.
- **Prerequisites:** single sign-on on, and the Single organization policy (so a regular member belongs to one organization). Turning off Single organization, or single sign-on, while it's on is refused. It isn't an MSP default policy: each client's sign-in is its own.
- **Account recovery keeps working:** an owner or admin resets a member's master password as before; the member signs in with single sign-on and the new master password unlocks.

### The client secret

The server must use the secret, so it can't be hashed. It's sealed with event streaming's sealed-secrets module ("Sending events to security tools", "Secrets at rest"), so a copy of the database alone doesn't reveal it (`keykeeper_sso/secret.rs`):

- **Its own key:** AES-256-GCM under a key derived from the server's private RSA key as event streaming's is (HMAC-SHA256 over the key's PKCS#1 DER), but over another label, `passbundle sso client secrets v1`. The key lives with the RSA key, never in the database.
