So an event destination's ciphertext never opens as a client secret, and a client secret never opens as a destination's secrets, whatever the associated data says.
- **Bound to its organization:** the associated data is its own, `sso client secret`, a zero byte, and `organization <id>` (the module seals and opens with associated data its caller gives, `seal_aad_with` and `open_aad_with`; event streaming's binding is unchanged). A sealed secret copied to another organization's row doesn't open.
- **Opens only these secrets:** neither key can decrypt a vault, and the server holds no key that can.
- **Changing the tenant or app keeps the secret**, unlike event streaming's Sentinel secret: this one only ever goes to Microsoft's token endpoint for the organization's tenant, on the authority the server's operator configures (`SSO_ENTRA_AUTHORITY`), so another admin can't send it anywhere they can read.
- **If the RSA key changes,** the secret can't be read. Sign-ins are refused, saying an admin needs to enter it again, and the Single sign-on page says so. With Require single sign-on on, owners (exempt) still sign in with their password to fix it.
- **Test vectors:** `docs/vectors/sso-client-secret-v1.json`, made by `make-sso-client-secret-v1.mjs` with Node's crypto alone, from event streaming's vectors' RSA key. They check the key, the associated data, two sealed secrets, that one doesn't open as two other organizations', and both ways across the features: the same secret and associated data sealed with event streaming's key doesn't open as a client secret, and a client secret doesn't open with event streaming's key, as a client secret or as a destination's secrets. The server checks its copy in its unit tests, and Tier 1 checks that the copy is the meta repo's and that the generator still makes the file.

A certificate credential instead of a secret is later.

### Events

In the organization's event log: single sign-on turned on and off (upstream's 1604 and 1605) and its settings changed (1694, ours, naming what changed: identifier, tenant, app, client secret, or API keys replaced), high severity; a domain added, verified, not found, and removed (upstream's 2000 to 2003, with the domain); someone's first sign-in, which links them (upstream's 1510, by email or from a session); every sign-in (1590, ours); every refusal, with why in this server's own words (1591, ours: "ana@acme.test isn't a member", "the ID token was for another tenant", "Microsoft refused, as the browser reported it: access_denied"); and an unlink, by an admin or the person (upstream's 1505). What the browser brings back from Microsoft can be made up by whoever started the sign-in, so the event keeps Microsoft's error only when it looks like an OAuth error code (lowercase letters, digits, and underscores, at most 64; otherwise `unrecognized_error`) and never its description, which only the person sees. An MSP's activity log has the settings, domain, API key, and unlink changes in its clients, with who made them. Never logged: the secret, tokens, codes, tickets, or states.

Event streaming sends them to security tools with these severities: high for single sign-on turned on or off, its settings changed, a domain added, verified, or removed, and a link (1510); medium for a refusal and an unlink; low for a sign-in and a domain not found yet. Each carries its detail (what changed, the domain, the Microsoft account's email, or why someone was refused), never a secret, and a refusal its fixed `reason` code (such as `not_member`, `microsoft_refused`, `another_browser`, `code_redeem_failed`, and one per ID token check: `token_signature`, `token_tenant`, and so on). The changes and links are never dropped from a full queue; refusals, which anyone can make many of by starting sign-ins and sending back bad answers, merge like failed logins, but only with refusals for the same reason. In an MSP's feed, its activity entries are 9180 to 9187, high except unlinks (medium).

### Threat model

- **Another tenant's users** (the nOAuth case): can't sign in, because the issuer and `tid` must be the organization's tenant, and email never identifies anyone.
- **People in the organization's own tenant:** an account that has keys never links by email, so writing a user's mail (which tenant admins, Exchange admins, some apps, and on-premises sync can do) reaches at most an invitation that has no account yet, at an address vouched for, in this organization; the account hears about every link by email. A spoofed email never moves an existing link. The organization's Entra admins can already sign in as any of their users, so for that organization's sign-in they're trusted, as with any SSO; single sign-on still only authenticates, and the master password is needed to read anything.
- **One organization against another, or an MSP's clients against each other:** a client's sign-in never signs in a member of another organization who isn't its member, never MSP staff, and doesn't satisfy another organization's requirement.
- **A stolen browser code or callback URL:** useless without the browser's cookie, the app's PKCE verifier, and within ten minutes, once.
- **The command-line tool's listener:** its answer goes to `http://localhost:<8065 to 8070>` on the computer whose browser signed in, as with upstream's tool and any native app's loopback redirect (RFC 8252), where a program already listening on that port (another person's, on a shared server or jump box) could take the code and, with the verifier of the sign-in it was for, get a session as the person (one that can't decrypt anything without the master password). So a link alone, which anyone can send, never gets as far as Microsoft, which may sign someone in without asking: the web vault's sign-in page never goes on by itself for the tool (not from an identifier or an email in its address, which upstream's do), and the server's own authorize answers the tool with a page that asks, whose Continue works only as a same-origin POST from that page, with its one-time token and a SameSite=Strict cookie another site's page can't make a browser send, once, within ten minutes. A person still has to press Continue twice on pages that say what the sign-in is for. Upstream's tool has the same exposure, without either question.
- **Linking someone else's Microsoft account:** a link ticket works only in the browser it was issued to, Microsoft is asked to let the person pick the account, the page names the Microsoft account before the session that asked confirms it, and the account is emailed. A stolen session alone can't link, even with the mailbox: a ticket needs the master password.
- **Login CSRF and sign-in mix-ups:** the state, the browser cookie, PKCE on both legs, and the one-time code.
- **A copy of the database:** holds no secret usable alone (the client secret is sealed under a key that isn't in it), only hashes of states and codes, and no key that opens a vault.
- **A hacked server:** gains nothing from single sign-on: it already issues sessions, and it still can't decrypt vaults.
- **DNS:** domain checks trust the server's resolver; DNSSEC isn't checked. A forged answer could verify a domain for the wrong organization, which matters only for finding the sign-in and for linking accounts at that domain that are in no organization (who still need a vouched-for email from that organization's tenant). Checked once, not again later.
- **Sessions outlive Entra:** turning someone off in Entra doesn't end a PassBundle session already open; provisioning's revoke does (it revokes their membership), and the next single sign-on is refused. Unlinking, a tenant change, removal from the organization, and turning single sign-on off end sessions; a single sign-on session refreshes only while its account is linked where single sign-on is on. Someone the policy covers who isn't linked yet and is turned off in Entra can't link, so can't sign in.

### Out of scope (for now)

Trusted-device unlock and anything else that changes how keys are held (its own design, with test vectors, first); making accounts on first sign-in without an invitation; single sign-on from the desktop and phone apps (the web vault, the browser extension, and the command-line tool use it; the extension and the tool hand off to the web vault, upstream's way; the extension's handoff is untested); other identity providers (Okta, Google) and SAML; a certificate credential; single sign-out; checking domains again later; an MSP's own staff signing in with single sign-on.

### Tests

- **Unit (server):** every ID token check (wrong issuer, tenant, audience, an extra audience, expired, not yet valid, issue time, a bad signature, a spliced payload, `none` and HMAC, an unknown key, missing and wrong nonce, a token replayed into another sign-in, v1, no object id), `xms_edov` as true, false, or not sent, guests (`acct`, `idp`), non-ASCII emails, an email claim that changes nothing, JWKS parsing; every linking rule (an account with keys never linked by email, an invitation without keys linked by a vouched email, `xms_edov` false over a verified domain, guests, a spoofed email on a linked person, an unvouched email, an invitation linked to someone else, non-members, a member of another organization, MSP staff, turned off, disabled, no account, no email, and from a session: a member, a no-organization account at a verified domain, a Microsoft account taken, a non-member); identifiers, GUIDs, domains, PKCE (RFC 7636's example), and TXT matching; the client secret's sealing, its binding to the organization, its test vectors, and event streaming's ciphertexts and its own not opening as each other's; the events' severities, which are kept, and which merge (refusals only with the same reason); refusal codes, one per ID token check; Microsoft's error kept only when it looks like an error code.
- **API (`e2e/tests/api/sso.spec.ts`), against a stand-in for Entra (`e2e/entra-standin.mjs`, an OpenID provider at Entra's per-tenant paths with keys made at start, and a DNS server):** setup and who may do it, domain verification and discovery, an account with keys refused by email and linked from its session (a ticket needs the master password, not a one-time code, and never takes an API key's token, `prompt=select_account`, the pending link showing the Microsoft account, tickets used once and only in the browser they were issued to, a pending link confirmed only by the session that asked and never redeemed as a sign-in, a Microsoft account taken, unlink by an admin and by the person, sessions ended), invitations linked only by a vouched email (`xms_edov` false, guests, non-ASCII), refusals, MSP staff (never signed in; the managing MSP's exempt from the policy, another MSP's not), each token check end to end, single-use states and codes, PKCE, redirect URI, the command-line tool's listener (the tool only, ports 8065 to 8070 only, its code only for itself at that address), the browser cookie (and its Secure flag), Require single sign-on (its prerequisites, another organization's sign-in refused and not named in this one's log, API keys replaced; members not linked yet get a linking session only after two-step login, which opens nothing else, links only that organization, and ends with the link; an unlink while it's on; the tenant can't change; removal deletes the link and SSO sessions stop refreshing), someone invited without an account, made-up Microsoft errors kept out of the log, and the event log.
- **Mail (`e2e/tests/mail/sso-recovery.spec.ts`):** the email a link sends, and account recovery with single sign-on required.
- **Web vault (`e2e/tests/sso.spec.ts`):** an owner sets it up on the page and verifies a domain; a member links their account with Link SSO (their master password, Microsoft, and their confirmation), then types their email, picks their account, and unlocks with their master password; someone outside the organization is told why; with the policy on (its dialog listing who isn't linked), a member's password sends them to it, one not linked yet links first and then signs in, and the owner's password still works.
- **Not yet, needs a real tenant:** Microsoft itself (docs/sso-entra.md, "Checking it with a real tenant").

### Status

Built: the server (`src/api/core/keykeeper_sso/`, `db/models/keykeeper_sso.rs`, a migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL), the sealed client secret, the admin console's Single sign-on page, the sign-in page saying why a sign-in was refused, the policy's wording, the event log's and MSP activity's sentences, and the events' severities for security tools. Waiting: checking it with a real Microsoft 365 tenant (docs/prelaunch.md, "Waiting on you").

## Two-step login for admins

"Client admins def need SSO or 2FA of some sort" (the owner, October 2026). Whoever runs an organization, or works for its MSP, can change who sees what, recover accounts, and reach every item, so their password alone isn't enough.

### Who
- **An organization's owners, admins, and custom roles that manage members, policies, or account recovery**, once they've joined (accepted or confirmed), where the organization requires it. Not regular members, upstream's managers, custom roles that only see the event log or import and export, nor invited or revoked memberships, unless the organization requires it of everyone (see "Two-step login for everyone"). Upstream's "Require two-step login" still covers regular members, and still exempts owners and admins.
- **Everyone on an MSP's staff**, every role, where the MSP requires it.
- **Everyone an MSP's membership brings into a client**, whatever their role there, where the client requires it: a technician whose custom role reaches every vault item and exports them reaches as much as an admin. So a client can require it of its MSP's people even if the MSP doesn't.
- **Only roles the person accepted (security review, October 2026):** on a server that sends no email, upstream joins an existing account to an organization at once when it's invited (and when a directory provisions it, or an invited address signs up), without asking. Counting such a role would let anyone sign someone out, and hold up their sign-in until they set up two-step login, by inviting them as an admin (or inviting them, then promoting them). So the server records those memberships (`unaccepted_memberships`), and the rule doesn't count one until its person accepts it: the web vault's vault page shows "Accept your role in Acme Dental" for each where the organization requires it (`GET /api/keykeeper/organizations/unaccepted`, `POST /api/keykeeper/organizations/<id>/accept`). Accepting without a method that counts signs them out, as any role that requires it does. Until then the organization's Settings page doesn't list them, and they can't be let in (confirmed), nor a member they haven't accepted be made an owner, admin, or custom role the rule covers, where the organization requires it (November 2026: before this, an invited admin who never accepted could be confirmed and hold the role without two-step login). The pending lists say `autoConfirm: false` for them, confirming them is refused with a message saying they accept first, and a member is invited again in that role instead. With email (the hosted service), people accept an invitation themselves, and nothing changes.
- **A role from before the rule (November 2026):** an admin confirmed before the organization turned the rule on, who never accepted their role, kept it without two-step login (a known gap until this). Now, when the rule comes to cover an organization (its Joining and security page, an MSP's default, Use default), each confirmed member there whose role it covers and who hasn't accepted their membership is revoked until they do (`revoked_until_accepted`), in the organization's event log as revoked by whoever turned the rule on. Their vault page asks them to accept the role, and says their access is on hold until they do; accepting restores them as they were (upstream's checks for restoring someone apply, and the event log says they were restored), and from then on the rule counts them, so without a method that counts they're signed out and set it up at their next sign-in. The person who turns the rule on accepts their own role by doing so. An organization's last confirmed owner isn't revoked (upstream never leaves an organization without one) and keeps the role until they accept it, the one case left open. Restoring such a member where the organization requires the rule, by an owner or admin or by the directory, leaves them revoked until they accept, saying so; a revoke by the directory, or a restore once the rule is off, ends the hold. An owner's or admin's revoke of someone on hold (one at a time or in bulk) makes it an ordinary revoke, with a revoke's usual checks and event (security review, November 2026): their vault page stops offering the role, and accepting it no longer brings them back. Revoked rather than sent back to "Needs confirmation", so they come back with their own acceptance: a client's own owner revoked this way by its MSP's default needs nobody to confirm them again, so an MSP's default can't keep them out. Turning the rule off again restores nobody on its own; an owner or admin can, or the person accepts, and their vault page goes on asking.

### What counts
- An authenticator app (TOTP); a security key or passkey (WebAuthn) with at least one key (removing the last key removes the method, where upstream left it on with none); or Duo with this server's own Duo account (its `DUO_*` settings). That's what this server can use.
- **Duo with someone's own Duo application doesn't count (decided October 2026):** upstream lets each person enter their own Duo keys, and whoever owns that Duo application sets its policy, which can bypass the second step or let a sign-in through without one. This server has no organization-level Duo integration; if one comes, an organization's own would count too. Without the server's own Duo account, Duo isn't offered to someone setting up two-step login for this rule.
- **Email codes don't count (decided October 2026; the owner can change this):** a client's email admins could read a member's codes, the same reason linking to single sign-on never trusts an email. Upstream's YubiKey OTP doesn't count either (it needs Yubico's cloud, which the hosted service doesn't set up). Either is a one-line change (`QUALIFYING_TWO_STEP` in the server's `db/models/keykeeper_two_step.rs`).
- **Once someone it covers has a method that counts,** their sign-in doesn't offer or take email codes, so a mailbox alone never passes for the second step (upstream's recovery code still works). The devices remembered for their two-step login, which may have been remembered with an email code, are forgotten whenever the rule comes to cover them (a promotion, joining, a setting turned on) and when they set up a method that counts, so each proves a method that counts once.
- **Single sign-on:** a sign-in with an organization's own single sign-on meets that organization's requirement for its admins and custom roles. Never another organization's requirement, never for owners (Require single sign-on exempts them so they can always sign in with a password, so their password needs the second step), and never for MSP staff (no client's single sign-on signs them in): for them two-step login is the protection.
- **Single sign-on relies on the client's own MFA:** it counts because the client's Microsoft Entra ID requires MFA for the sign-in (Conditional Access, or security defaults), which is the client's responsibility, as the setup guide (docs/sso-entra.md) says. Entra's v2.0 ID tokens usually leave out `amr`, so in practice the credit rests on that Conditional Access policy; this server can't see whether it ran. When a token does carry `amr` and it has no `mfa`, the sign-in doesn't count, and the person gets a setup session as for a password. For real assurance, an organization could tag the app with a Conditional Access authentication context that requires MFA, and this server could ask for it (`claims` with `acrs`) and count only ID tokens whose `acrs` names it; not built.
- **A session single sign-on started counts for that one organization:** the sign-in records which organization's single sign-on counted (`kk_sso_org` in the access and refresh tokens, kept from refresh to refresh), and it counts only while that organization's single sign-on still signs the account in. Another organization whose single sign-on the account is linked to never counts for it.

### Defaults and settings
- **On** for the Business preset (a company's own organization, "A company's own IT") and in every new MSP's default policies, so Add client turns it on. **Off** for an organization made the upstream way (a family or a group). An organization from before it has no setting and counts as off; an MSP from before it requires it of its staff, but its clients' default is off until an MSP admin turns it on (there were no users to migrate).
- **The organization's setting:** Settings, Joining and security, "Two-step login for admins", for its owners and admins and its MSP's admins (not technicians), as for single sign-on; anyone else gets "not found" (`/api/keykeeper/organizations/<id>/admin-two-step`). Turning it on or off is an event in its log (1695, 1696), high for its security tools, and for an MSP's client goes in the MSP's activity log (`client_admin_two_step_set`).
- **An MSP's clients:** one of the default policies ("Two-step login for admins"), so following, own settings, and Use default work as for the others. Changing the default is logged high (`admin_two_step_default_set`). It isn't an upstream policy: upstream's apps refuse every policy when one has a type they don't know (the SDK's policy filter throws on it), so the MSP's default is kept with the others under type 1000, which the server never sends anyone as a policy, and each client's setting is its own row (`org_admin_two_step`).
- **An MSP's staff:** the MSP's own setting, on by default, in the console's Two-step login section; MSP admins change it, logged high (`staff_two_step_set`).
- **Who isn't set up:** the organization's Joining and security page lists everyone it covers (or would, before it's turned on) without a method that counts, and notes admins linked to its single sign-on, who are covered when they sign in that way. The MSP console lists its staff without one and each client's own admins without one. Each tells the person looking when they're on the list. Giving someone an elevated role without one is allowed; they set it up at their next sign-in.
- **Making a business organization or an MSP without one** would end the maker's session at once, since they're covered from that moment. So New organization (for a business) and the MSP console's Create MSP check first, at the click, and say so instead: "Set up two-step login first", with Set up two-step login and Not now. The way there remembers the form in the tab (never in the address), and Settings, Two-step login shows "Back to ..." with what they'd typed, so they come back to the form as they left it, set up, and stay signed in. A family or a group doesn't need it.

### Signing in
- **A password sign-in** that checks out (the password, then any two-step login the account has, an email code included) by someone it covers who has no method that counts gets a setup session instead of a login: a 400 `invalid_grant`, as single sign-on's linking session, with `KeykeeperTwoStepSetup` (the token) and `KeykeeperTwoStepRequiredBy` (who requires it, by name). A wrong password says nothing about it.
- **The setup session** mirrors the linking session: 32 random bytes, kept as a SHA-256 hash, fifteen minutes, one at a time per account, and no refresh token. It isn't a login token, so every other request refuses it with 401. Only the endpoints that set up two-step login take it: `GET /api/keykeeper/two-step-setup` (what the page shows) and upstream's get-authenticator, authenticator, get-webauthn, get-webauthn-challenge, webauthn, get-duo, and duo, which still check the master password (or a one-time code for an account without one) as they do for a signed-in session. Duo there is only the server's own Duo account. Setting up a method that counts ends it, and that answer carries a new recovery code (`keykeeperRecoveryCode`; any earlier one, say from email codes, stops working), which the session couldn't fetch afterwards. Anything that changes the account's security stamp ends it too: a password change, account recovery, and "Deauthorize sessions".
- **A single sign-on sign-in:** the same, for whatever that organization's single sign-on doesn't meet. Single sign-on proves no password, so the setup page asks for the master password.
- **A sign-in approved from another device** proves no password either, so it gets no setup session: it's refused, saying to sign in with the master password in the web vault. The check sits with the organizations' other rules for such a sign-in (`approved_sign_in_rules`, "Approving a sign-in from another device"), and a session such a sign-in began refreshes only while its person meets the rule, as a password's does.
- **An account without a master password yet** (an invitation's first single sign-on, which goes on to set one) isn't held up. The rule applies from its next sign-in, and its session stops refreshing once it has a password and no method that counts.
- **The web vault** keeps the token in the tab (sessionStorage, nothing else) and shows "Set up two-step login to continue" (`/two-step-setup`): who requires it, then an authenticator app (its QR code and key), a security key or passkey, or Duo. After a password sign-in the page has the master password's server hash in memory, so it doesn't ask again; after a reload or single sign-on, it asks. Once it's set up, the page shows the recovery code, and the person signs in as usual with their new method. What goes wrong there is said in plain words ("Errors in plain words"): a wrong code is "That code didn't work", never upstream's "Invalid TOTP code! Server time: ... IP: ...", and when the page can't load (offline, say) it offers Try again, keeping the master password's hash it already has.
- **The browser extension, the desktop and phone apps, and the CLI** don't set it up: the extension says to set it up in the web vault, in a dialog that opens it; the apps' core turns the answer into its own error (`SetUpTwoStepFirst`), which the sign-in screen shows with the web vault's address, after a password or an email code; the CLI prints the server's sentence, which names the web vault.

### Sessions, API keys, and recovery
- **Sessions end at once:** an access token lasts two hours, long enough for a session that never had a second step to turn the rule off for good, so waiting for its refresh isn't enough. When a change brings someone under the rule without a method that counts (a promotion or new custom role, joining or being restored, joining an MSP's staff or a change to their access there, a setting or default turned on, making a Business organization or an MSP), or takes their last one (turning it off, removing the last security key, the recovery code, the admin panel or account recovery resetting it, Duo replaced with one's own), their security stamp changes: every access token, refresh token, and setup session of theirs ends at once, and the apps hear to sign out. Their next sign-in sets it up. Someone who still meets it (with a method that counts) keeps their sessions; their remembered devices are forgotten. An account without a master password yet is left alone, as at sign-in.
- **And at refresh:** a session also refreshes only while its person meets the rule, whether it began with a password, single sign-on, or a device's approval, so nothing a change misses lasts past the access token.
- **Turning it off takes meeting it:** only someone who meets the rule themselves (with a method that counts, or the single sign-on session that meets it for them) turns off an organization's setting, an MSP's for its staff, or an MSP's default for its clients (or puts clients back on a default that's off). Anyone else is told to set up two-step login first.
- **Making a Business organization or an MSP** makes the person its owner, or its staff: without a method that counts, the web vault's New organization (for a business) and Set up your MSP say first that they'll be signed out once it's made, with a link to set it up in Settings first (it asks the server whether their methods count: `GET /api/keykeeper/two-step-setup` answers a signed-in session too, with `qualifying`). If they go ahead, making it signs them out, and their next sign-in sets it up.
- **API keys (decided October 2026):** an API key sign-in (the CLI's `client_credentials`) skips two-step login, as upstream's does, so it's refused for someone the rule covers who has no method that counts, saying why. Once they set one up, it signs in again, but its token does no admin work for them: upstream's endpoints for an organization's owners, admins, managers, and admitted custom roles, and the MSP console's and organizations' own (`/api/keykeeper/msp...`, `/api/keykeeper/organizations...`), refuse it with 401. Which endpoint it is comes from the route the request reached, never the address as written (Rocket skips empty segments and decodes the rest, so `/api//keykeeper/msp/...` and `/api/keykeeper/%6Dsp/...` reach the same handlers); the request log decides what to hide the same way. The CLI and scripts still read and change the person's own vault with it (sync, items, folders). An API key's token is told apart by its scope (`api`, never `offline_access`). An organization's API key isn't a person, and isn't covered.
- **Recovery:** upstream's two-step recovery code still works: it turns two-step login off, as before, and a covered person's sign-in then goes straight to setting up a new method. Account recovery (an MSP or a client's admins resetting a member's master password) works as before, and the person, if covered, sets up two-step login at their next sign-in. MSP staff are never recovered ("MSP staff and account recovery").

### Tests
- **Unit (server):** which methods count (email and YubiKey OTP don't; a security key method only with a key; Duo only with the server's keys), the token kept only as its hash, single sign-on meeting only its own organization's requirement and never an owner's or MSP staff's, an ID token's `amr` without `mfa`, the admin paths an API key's token can't use, the request log's paths as Rocket routes them, the sentence naming who requires it, the default kind upstream never sees as a policy and in the Business preset, the events' severities (high, kept), and a role from before the rule (revoked when the rule comes, the one who turned it on accepting instead, a restore holding them, accepting restoring them, a directory's revoke ending the hold, an owner's revoke making the hold an ordinary revoke and an admin's of an owner refused).
- **API (`e2e/tests/api/two-step-for-admins.spec.ts`):** every role covered or not (owners, admins, custom roles that manage members, policies, or account recovery; not members, managers, other custom roles, or a revoked admin), and a wrong password saying nothing; the Business preset, an MSP's staff and clients, both settings turned off, Use default, and a default turned off reaching followers; an MSP technician with only every vault item covered by a client's setting; the setup session refused everywhere else (the vault, the profile, other two-step settings, API keys, linking, the organization), not a refresh token, one at a time, still asking the master password, setting up no Duo but the server's, and ended by setting up an authenticator app with a new recovery code, then a normal sign-in with it; the recovery code ending the other sessions; single sign-on meeting only its own organization's requirement for an admin (recorded on the session), not when `amr` says no MFA, never for an owner, its session ending when another organization's applies, and turning the setting off only while it meets it; sessions ending at once when two-step login is turned off, someone is promoted, the setting is turned on, or someone joins an MSP's staff, and going on for someone who still meets it; remembered devices forgotten on a promotion; the events, at a webhook, high; API keys refused until it's set up, then reading the vault but refused admin endpoints, however the address is written (an empty segment, a percent-encoded letter); a device's approval refused, and its session ending on a promotion; on this server without email, an invitation as an admin (or a promotion after one) signing nobody out until the person accepts the role, and an admin confirmed before the setting was turned on being revoked until they accept, then restored by accepting and signed out to set it up (an owner's restore refused meanwhile, the one who turned it on keeping their place, and one an owner revoked in bulk while on hold staying revoked after accepting); and the permission matrix for both settings.
- **Mail (`e2e/tests/mail/admin-two-step.spec.ts`):** an owner whose only second step is an email code is asked for it, and the right code gets a setup session, not a login; once they have an authenticator app, email codes aren't offered or taken; and an MSP resets a client admin's two-step login, which signs them out at once, and their next sign-in sets it up (recovering their master password before they do ends that setup session).
- **Web vault (`e2e/tests/admin-two-step.spec.ts`):** an admin without two-step login, invited on this server without email, accepts the role on their vault page and is signed out; they sign in, are taken to "Set up two-step login to continue", get a wrong code refused in plain words (no server time or address), set up an authenticator app, and sign in with it to their vault; Joining and security lists who isn't set up and turns it off, asking first. `e2e/tests/first-run.spec.ts`: making an MSP and a business without it asks first, Not now makes nothing, and the way back keeps the name and leaves them signed in once it's set up.
- **Extension (`e2e/tests/admin-two-step-extension.spec.ts`):** making a business without two-step login warns first and then signs the person out; the extension's sign-in then says to set it up in the web vault, names who requires it, and opens the web vault.
- **The other end-to-end tests** give the people who run a business or an MSP an authenticator app when they sign up (`e2e/tests/support/two-step.ts`), as such a person would, and answer two-step login with its codes.
- **Apps core:** the answer becomes `SetUpTwoStepFirst` after a password and after an email code (never a wrong code), and its sentence.
- **Not end to end:** setting up a security key or passkey (the test servers' address is an IP, which WebAuthn refuses), removing the last one, or Duo (it needs Duo's own service). They go through upstream's endpoints, which the API test checks take the setup session, and docs/hands-on-testing.md has a real security key.

### Status
Built: the server (`api/core/keykeeper_two_step.rs`, `db/models/keykeeper_two_step.rs`, a migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL, the setup endpoints taking a setup session, the sign-in, refresh, and API key checks, sessions ending when someone comes under the rule, roles the person hasn't accepted, revoked until they accept when the rule comes after them, and the MSP default), the web vault (the setup page, the vault page's "Accept your role", the Settings page's card, the MSP console's section and default policy, the event log's and activity's sentences), the extension's dialog, the CLI's message, and the apps' core. Requiring it of an organization's regular members by this rule, and a nudge for everyone else, are "Two-step login for everyone" below.

## Two-step login for everyone

Two-step login for admins covers the people who run an organization. Some organizations want the same of everyone in it. Upstream's "Require two-step login" policy covers regular members, but it counts email codes, YubiKey OTP, and Duo with someone's own Duo application, and it removes a member without any two-step login from the organization rather than having them set it up. So our rule can reach further: an organization can require it of everyone, with the admin rule's counting and everything the admin rule already does. And for the people no rule covers who have no two-step login at all, a gentle nudge.

### Who
- **Everyone who has joined the organization** (accepted or confirmed), whatever their role, where the organization requires it of everyone: regular members, managers, and every custom role, on top of everyone the admin rule covers. Not invited or revoked memberships, nor a role its person hasn't accepted on a server that sends no email, exactly as for admins.
- **One setting with three levels, not a second setting:** off, admins (everyone "Two-step login for admins" covers), or everyone. Everyone includes the admins, so an organization can't require it of its members but not its admins, and turning off the admin level turns off both. Stored as a second column of the admin rule's row (`org_admin_two_step.everyone`), which counts only while `required` is on.

### What counts
The admin rule's ("What counts" above), unchanged: an authenticator app, a security key or passkey with at least one key, or Duo with this server's own Duo account. Not email codes, not YubiKey OTP, not Duo with someone's own Duo application. A member whose only second step is email codes is covered, and at their next sign-in, after the code, sets up a method that counts; from then on email codes aren't offered or taken at their sign-in. A sign-in with the organization's own single sign-on meets its requirement for everyone but its owners and the people its MSP brings in, as for admins, and only while that single sign-on signs the account in.

### The setting
- **Settings, Joining and security,** in the "Two-step login for admins" card: a second box, "Everyone else too", which needs the first. The same people change it (owners and admins, and its MSP's admins), through the same endpoint (`/api/keykeeper/organizations/<id>/admin-two-step`, `everyone` beside `required`; leaving `everyone` out keeps it as it is), confirmed with the master password or a one-time code, and only someone who meets the rule lowers it (to admins only, or off).
- **Who isn't set up:** below the admins without a method that counts, the card lists the organization's other members without one (`membersNotSetUp`), whether or not it's on for everyone yet, so an owner sees who turning it on would sign out. Members linked to its single sign-on are marked as admins are.
- **Events:** requiring it of everyone is 1697 in the organization's log, and going back to admins only is 1698; off to everyone logs 1695 and 1697, everyone to off logs 1698 and 1696. All high for security tools, as 1695 and 1696 are. For an MSP's client the change also goes in the MSP's activity log (`client_everyone_two_step_set`, on or off, high).
- **An MSP's clients:** the default policy "Two-step login for admins" gets an option, "Everyone else too", kept in that default's data under type 1000 as before (`{"everyone": true}`; without it, the default and a client's own setting read as they always have, `data: null`). Following, own settings, Use default, and Required for every client treat it as any policy's options: a client whose level differs from the default's has its own setting, and requiring the default locks both levels. Changing the option is `admin_two_step_default_set` in the activity log, as turning the default on or off is. Lowering the default (off, or everyone to admins), or using a default that lowers a client's level, takes an MSP admin who meets the rule.
- **The MSP console's two-step section** lists, under each client's own admins without a method that counts, how many of its other members have none, where the client requires it of everyone.

### Defaults
**Off everywhere** (decided November 2026, conservative, for the owner to revisit): the Business preset and a new MSP's defaults still require it of admins only, and an organization made the upstream way requires nothing. Turning it on signs out every member without a method that counts, at once and as they join (an install command's join included), and they can't use the extension or the apps until they set one up in the web vault. That's an organization's choice to make, not one to make for it. Whether the hosted service should turn it on for new business organizations is the owner's call.

### With upstream's "Require two-step login" (decided November 2026)
Both can be on, and each does only what it says.
- **Both on:** upstream's does what it always has: a member with no two-step login at all can't be confirmed, and is revoked from the organization when the policy is turned on or when they remove their last method, and email codes count for it. Ours, on top, has everyone sign in with a method that counts. Anyone who meets ours meets upstream's (an authenticator app, a security key, and Duo all count there), so in practice ours is the stricter. The one exception stays upstream's: someone who signs in with the organization's single sign-on and has no two-step login meets ours there, and upstream's still revokes them.
- **One turned off:** the other stays as it is. Turning ours off (or down to admins) doesn't turn upstream's on or off, and restores nobody upstream's revoked; turning upstream's off doesn't change ours. Neither ever changes the other, and nothing is reverted.
- **Why this is the least surprising:** every setting says in one sentence what it does, and changing one never silently changes another, which is how every other policy here works (see also "Required for every client", where turning a requirement off reverts nothing). Folding upstream's into ours, by turning it off or skipping its revocations while ours is on, would change a policy nobody touched, leave the admin console's Policies page showing something other than what happens, and drop upstream's removal of members who never set anything up, which an organization may want. Turning upstream's on along with ours would revoke members nobody asked about. The cost is some overlap, which the card names in a line: "Require two-step login is a separate policy: it counts email codes, and removes members who have no two-step login from the organization."
- **Upstream's notice stays:** someone waiting to be confirmed into an organization whose upstream policy needs two-step login still sees "<org> requires two-step login before you can get in" ("Joining").

### Everything else is the admin rule's
Nothing new for a member it covers; the code paths are the admin rule's, with the wider level deciding who's covered. The setup session at a password sign-in (and after an email code), "Set up two-step login to continue" in the web vault, the extension's dialog, the apps' `SetUpTwoStepFirst`, and the CLI's message; sessions ending at once when it comes to cover someone without a method that counts (turned on, joining, being restored, a client following an MSP's default) or when their last one goes, and refreshing only while they meet it; remembered devices forgotten when it comes to cover them and when they set one up; API keys refused until it's set up, and their tokens refused for admin endpoints after; a device's approval refused; roles not yet accepted on a server without email not counted until their person accepts them on the vault page ("Accept your role in Acme Dental", for a member too), and confirmed members who never accepted revoked until they do when it comes to cover them (`revoked_until_accepted`, with the same exceptions, the last owner kept, and the same event log entries). The sentence stays "Acme Dental requires two-step login for your role.": a member's role there is member.

### A nudge for everyone else
- **Who sees it:** someone with no two-step login at all (no authenticator app, email codes, security key or passkey, YubiKey, or Duo), whom no rule covers (two-step login for admins or for everyone, or an MSP's for its staff), and whom no organization is waiting on to turn it on for upstream's policy (that has its own notice). The server decides (`GET /api/keykeeper/two-step-nudge`, `{ "show": true }`), so a covered person never sees it, even in a session from before they were covered.
- **Where:** the web vault's vault page, as a one-line banner where upstream's own vault banners go, so it doesn't push the list far down; and the browser extension's vault page in the popup, as a short callout ("Add two-step login") where the other two-step notices are. Both say: "Your password is all that protects your account. Add a second step, such as an authenticator app, so a stolen password isn't enough." With "Set it up" (Settings, Two-step login; the extension opens it in a web vault tab) and "Not now".
- **How often:** whenever the vault page opens while it applies, until the person sets something up or says Not now. Not now hides it for 90 days on every device, then it shows again until they dismiss it again. Setting up any two-step method ends it for good.
- **How dismissal is remembered:** per account, on the server: the time the account last said Not now (`two_step_nudge_dismissals`, one row per account, nothing else; `POST /api/keykeeper/two-step-nudge/dismiss`). So it's synced: dismissing it in the extension hides it in the web vault too. Nothing in the browser's storage.
- **It never blocks anything:** a callout in the page, never a dialog; nothing waits on it; if the server can't be asked (offline, an older server), nothing shows.
- **Not yet:** in the desktop and phone apps, and a nudge from email codes to a stronger method for people no rule covers.

### Tests
- **Unit (server):** the levels (everyone includes admins, turning off the admin level turns off both, `everyone` counting only with `required`), who each level covers (a regular member and a manager only with everyone; never an invited, revoked, or unaccepted membership), a member who never accepted waiting to accept only where everyone is covered, the MSP default's option as an effect (left out, false, and null the same; everyone differing), the events' order and severities (1697 and 1698 high and kept, `client_everyone_two_step_set` high), and the nudge (any method ends it, a covered person never gets it, a dismissal lasting 90 days).
- **API (`e2e/tests/api/two-step-for-everyone.spec.ts`):** an organization that requires it of everyone covers a regular member, a manager, and a custom role that only reads the event log, and lists them as not set up before and after; a member with an authenticator app keeps their session while one without is signed out at once; everyone needs the admin level; going back to admins only, then off, lets them sign in with a password again; the organization's single sign-on meeting it for a member, and a session it started no longer lowering it once that single sign-on is off; the events, high at a webhook; an MSP's default with the option reaching the clients that follow it and not a client with its own setting, and Use default; required for every client locking the option in a client; upstream's policy alongside it (both on, then each turned off, leaving the other as it was); on this server without email, a member's invitation signing nobody out until they accept, and a member confirmed before it was turned on revoked until they accept; and the nudge (shown to someone with no method whom no rule covers, Not now hiding it for the account, a new sign-in included, not shown to someone it covers, to someone with a method, or to someone an organization's upstream policy is waiting on).
- **Mail (`e2e/tests/mail/admin-two-step.spec.ts`):** a regular member whose only second step is an email code, in an organization that requires it of everyone, gives the code and gets a setup session, not a login; with an authenticator app set up, email codes aren't offered.
- **Web vault (`e2e/tests/two-step-everyone.spec.ts`):** Joining and security requires it of everyone, listing the members without it first and asking before it signs them out, and says how it differs from upstream's policy; the member is signed out and taken to set it up at their next sign-in; going back to admins asks first; the nudge shows on the vault page for someone with no two-step login, Not now hides it, and it stays hidden when the vault opens again; it doesn't show for someone with an authenticator app. The website's screenshots (`e2e/site-shots`) say Not now for their people, so it isn't in the pictures.
- **Extension (`e2e/tests/two-step-everyone.spec.ts`):** the nudge in the popup's vault, Set it up opening the web vault's two-step settings, and Not now there hiding it in the web vault too.

### Status
Built (November 2026): the server (the `keykeeper_two_step_everyone` migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL, the level in `keykeeper_two_step.rs`, the MSP default's option in `keykeeper_msp_policies.rs`, and the nudge's endpoints), the web vault (the card's second box and list, the MSP default's option, the console's counts, the event log's and activity's sentences, and the nudge on the vault page), and the extension's nudge.

## Two-step login settings

The web vault's Settings, Two-step login (upstream's dialogs for a security key, an authenticator app, email, YubiKey, and Duo) asks for the master password or a one-time code once, then sends a token instead of the password with the requests that follow: "Read key", "Turn on", "Save", "Remove". Upstream's clients have done this since July 2026 (`userVerificationToken`). Vaultwarden 1.37.4 didn't send one, so we made our own token (November 2026); Vaultwarden 1.37.5 brings upstream's, which replaced ours in the upstream sync of November 2026 (see "Keeping up with upstream").

- **The token (upstream's):** a JWT the server signs with its RSA key, as it signs login tokens, under its own issuer (`<domain>|api.2fa`), so no login check takes one. It names the person, the method, whether the method was on, and that method's state when it was given: the authenticator app's key, the email address, the security keys' and YubiKeys' ids, or Duo's (hidden) keys. It lasts five minutes. A request is refused when any of those differ, so a token from one dialog doesn't work in another, and once the method changes (turned on, a key added) the token stops working. Each request still needs a valid signed-in session, which a new password ends.
- **What it's good for:** only two-step login's own settings. The first answer of a dialog (`get-authenticator`, `get-webauthn`, and the others) still needs the master password or a code, so a token never makes another; every other change that asks for the master password ("Confirming sensitive changes") never takes one. The settings that take it need it: a master password alone no longer turns a method on or off there, as upstream's clients never send one.
- **Ours on top:** the setup session of "Two-step login for admins" still works on the setup endpoints, and a method set up there ends the session with a new recovery code. Every way a method goes (each method's Remove, upstream's own per-method routes now, which replace the shared `/two-factor/disable`) still checks the admin two-step rule afterwards and ends the sessions of someone who no longer has a method that counts. Upstream also forgets remembered devices whenever a method goes.
- **Retired:** our own v1 token and its key, derived from the server's RSA key; its test vectors ("twoStepSettings") left `docs/vectors/server-derived-keys-v1.json`.
- **Tests:** upstream's unit test that a token parses only as its own method's; an API test that turns an authenticator app on and off with a token, and has a made-up token, someone else's, another method's, one for another key, and a token given before the method changed all refused, and everything else refuse it (`e2e/tests/api/two-step-settings.spec.ts`); the setup session's tests (`two-step-for-admins.spec.ts`); and tier 1 adds a security key through the web vault's own dialog (`e2e/tests/connector-origins.spec.ts`).
- **Fixed by the sync:** email's dialog reads the address where upstream's clients read it, and the email, YubiKey, and Duo dialogs' Remove calls routes the server has.

## Two-step login in the apps

Our own apps took only an authenticator app's code or an email code, so someone whose second step is a security key, Duo, or a YubiKey couldn't sign in there at all, and a lost phone left only the web vault (gap audit, November 2026). Now the apps take every method the server offers, as the web vault and the extension do.

**What people see.** The second step opens on the strongest method offered that the device can do: a security key, then Duo, a YubiKey, an authenticator app, and email. "Use another way" lists the others, and a recovery code is always among them (the server takes one whatever it offers). "Don't ask again on this device" and "Start over" are as before.

- **Security key** ("Use security key"; Cancel stops the wait):
  - **Windows:** Windows's own WebAuthn API (webauthn.dll), as browsers use it: Windows shows its dialog (insert, touch, the key's PIN if it has one) for USB, NFC, and Bluetooth keys, and for a phone or Windows Hello where the account added one as its key. Apps can't reach a key's HID device on Windows themselves.
  - **Linux:** keys plugged in, reached directly as browsers do: the hidraw devices whose report descriptor names the FIDO Alliance's usage page (0xF1D0). udev gives the person at the computer access to them (systemd 244 and later); a computer without those rules gets a sentence saying so. No libfido2: the core's own CTAP is small, and the app needs no new library.
  - **Mac:** keys plugged in, through IOKit's HID manager, as browsers do. AuthenticationServices' security key requests need the Associated Domains entitlement for the server's own domain, which needs an Apple developer account and can't name every self-hosted server; IOKit needs neither (the app isn't sandboxed, and a security key isn't a keyboard, so macOS asks nothing).
  - **Android:** a key held to the back of the phone (NFC) or plugged in (USB; Android asks the person's permission for the key each time). Not Credential Manager or Google's FIDO2 API: both need Google Play services, set the client data's origin to the app's signing certificate instead of the server's (so the server would refuse it), and check the server's domain lists the app (`assetlinks.json`), which self-hosted servers don't.
  - **iPhone:** not yet. Apple lets an app reach a key only with entitlements tied to a developer account (NFC's reader for the FIDO applet, or Associated Domains), which PassBundle doesn't have yet. The app says "This device can't use a security key with PassBundle yet. Use another way to sign in, or the web vault." and offers the other ways.
- **Duo** ("Continue with Duo"): the server's Universal Prompt address opens in the browser. Duo sends the browser to the server's Duo page (the web vault's `duo-redirect-connector.html`), which hands the answer to the app as `passbundle://duo-callback?code=...&state=...`, with a "Back to the app" button in case the browser doesn't open the app by itself (passbundle-clients, `duo-redirect.ts`; upstream's links to upstream's apps stay gone, "Connector pages"). On the Mac and iPhones the system's sign-in window (ASWebAuthenticationSession) receives the link itself, so the app takes `passbundle://` links from nowhere else. Android opens a screenless activity with it, which brings the app back to the front. On Windows and Linux, the app makes itself the person's handler for `passbundle://` links when Duo starts (Windows: `HKEY_CURRENT_USER\Software\Classes\passbundle`; Linux: a desktop entry of its own in `~/.local/share/applications` and `xdg-mime`); the OS opens a second copy of the app with the link, which leaves it in the app's folder (readable only by the person, good for Duo's five minutes, read once) for the copy that's signing in, and ends before it shows.
- **YubiKey:** the key types its one-time code into the field (44 characters, checked before sending, so a keyboard layout that moves letters gets a sentence instead of a refusal). On Android the key can also be held to the phone: the app reads the address the key gives over NFC (`https://my.yubico.com/yk/#<code>`) and sends the code.
- **Recovery code:** the field says that signing in with it turns two-step login off for the account (upstream's rule, which the server keeps); once it does, a dialog says so and where to turn it on again. Someone whose role requires two-step login then gets the web vault's setup page, as after a password ("Two-step login for admins").

**Decisions:**
- **The core does the browser's part (W3C WebAuthn, `navigator.credentials.get()`) for its own server only.** The client data's origin is always the server the app signs in to, never anything the server says. The site ID the server asks for must be that server's host or a domain above it that isn't a public suffix, as a browser requires, and never an address. So a hacked or phishing server can only ever get a signature for itself; it can't have the app sign another site's challenge. The AppID (WebAuthn's `appid` extension, for keys added with upstream's older U2F dialog) is used only when it's on the server's own origin, where PassBundle's server puts it.
- **The key's answer is checked before it's sent:** one of the account's credentials, signed for the site ID (or the AppID, saying so), with the person present.
- **CTAP from the FIDO Alliance's specifications:** CTAP2's authenticatorGetAssertion, and U2F's authenticate for keys from before it, over USB HID (CTAPHID: channels, packets, keepalives, CTAPHID_CANCEL when the person cancels) and NFC (the FIDO applet, command chaining, GET RESPONSE). The core asks for a touch only (user verification "discouraged", the server's choice for two-step login), so it never handles a key's PIN; a key that insists on one gets "This security key asks for its PIN, which PassBundle can't send it yet. Use another way to sign in, or the web vault." (Windows asks for the PIN itself.) A key that takes fewer credentials per request is asked one at a time. Several keys plugged in are asked in turn; one without the account's credential answers at once.
- **Duo's address and answer are checked:** the address must be Duo's own authorize page over HTTPS (`api-` and an ID under duosecurity.com or duofederal.com, the web vault's Duo page's rule), and its request (the JWT the server signed for Duo, read but not checked; Duo checks it) must come back to this server. The link back must carry this sign-in's state, so a link from anywhere else, or for another sign-in, finishes nothing and isn't sent.
- **New challenges when needed:** the server makes a new Duo address and security key challenge whenever it says what it offers, and takes one when it's tried. So once one was tried, or it's old (Duo's after a minute, a key's after four), the core asks again: the same request as signing in, without a second step. The slow key derivation still runs once.
- **The wait for a key doesn't hold the app:** the core reads the request, lets go while the key is asked (up to the server's minute), then sends the answer, so other accounts' calls (locking, the screens) don't wait.
- **No change to key derivation or the master password's handling,** so no new test vectors: the second step resends the waiting sign-in's login hash, as before.
- **No new crates.** windows-sys gains its WebAuthn feature; nothing from Bitwarden's SDK or desktop_native.

**Tests:**
- **Core unit tests** (passbundle-apps `core/src/security_key`, `core/src/two_step.rs`): CBOR against RFC 8949's examples, CTAP2's key order, and what a key never sends; CTAP over USB and NFC against a security key in software (`security_key/soft.rs`, in unit tests and builds with the test-hooks feature only, never a release): CTAP2, U2F, the AppID for each, a key that takes one credential at a time, one that wants its PIN, one without the credential, Cancel reaching the key, a timeout, and other channels' packets; WebAuthn's origins, site IDs (public suffixes, addresses, other sites), AppIDs, client data byte for byte, the token's shape, and answers for something else refused; Duo's addresses and answers; YubiKey codes and recovery codes; the second copy's hand-over (once, only the person); the Linux desktop entry's quoting; the security key report descriptor check.
- **The core against a real server** (`core/tests/server.rs`): a recovery code signs in (typed in capitals, in fours) and turns two-step login off, after a wrong one says so; a YubiKey code in the wrong shape is refused before it's sent, and Duo and a key aren't offered to an account without them; and a security key added as the web vault adds one (webauthn-rs checks it) signs in through the real CTAPHID path with the software key, after an answer made for another origin is refused by the server and a new challenge is asked for. WebAuthn takes only a domain, so CI starts a second test server named localhost for it (`tools/test-server.sh`'s fourth argument).
- **The app** (`app/test/two_step_test.dart`): the order, every other way with the recovery code, each method's words and calls, Cancel, a key not touched, a device without security keys, Duo given up and tried again, YubiKey over NFC starting and stopping, the recovery code's warning and dialog, and email. `app/test/two_step_server_test.dart` goes through the real core to a stand-in server: what leaves the core for a YubiKey, a recovery code, and Duo ("code|state"), and what the core refuses without sending (a code that isn't a YubiKey's, a Duo answer for another sign-in).
- **Android** (`SecurityKeysTest.kt`, JVM): the FIDO interface check and a YubiKey's code from its NFC address; the release check keeps what the core calls through JNI.
- **The web vault's Duo page** (passbundle-clients `duo-redirect.spec.ts`): the apps' link, encoded, never naming upstream's apps.
- **By hand** (docs/hands-on-testing.md): a real key on each computer and the Pixel (USB and NFC), Duo with a Duo account, a YubiKey's code, and a recovery code. CI has no key, no Duo account, and no NFC or USB on its emulators.

**Status (November 7, 2026):** built in passbundle-apps and passbundle-clients branches `apps-two-step` (with this section). "Don't ask again on this device" works in the apps since the new-device check (November 2026), which keeps the token the server gives. Security keys on Windows, Linux, the Mac, and Android; not iPhones (above). Duo on every platform. YubiKey codes everywhere, and over NFC on Android. Recovery codes everywhere. Not yet:
- A key's PIN outside Windows (CTAP's PIN protocol), for keys that insist on one even for a touch.
- iPhones' security keys and YubiKey codes over NFC, with an Apple developer account.
- An installer that registers `passbundle://` links on Windows and Linux ahead of time (today the app does it for the person when Duo starts).

## New-device check

In upstream's format the master password alone signs an account in on any device, unless the person has two-step login. 1Password stops that with a Secret Key a new device must also have, which is the onboarding step we don't want. The owner chose a middle path (November 2026), and two-step login stays optional for people no rule covers:

1. **A new-device check, on by default** for every account without two-step login (the owner's decision). When an account signs in on a device it has never used, the sign-in waits until the person approves it from a device already signed in to that account, or, when there's no such device, with a code sent by email.
2. **Number matching** (the owner's decision, as in Microsoft Authenticator). The new device shows a number, and the approving device must type it. A tap alone never approves, so nobody approves a sign-in that isn't theirs by reflex.
3. **Where it comes from** (the owner's decision). The approval prompt, the new-device email, and the event log say roughly where the sign-in comes from (country, and city when known), with the device and browser, so "a sign-in from another country" can be denied.

No key moves. The new device already has the master password; the check only decides whether the server finishes its sign-in. Nothing here changes encryption, keys, or the KDF, so there are no test vectors. "Sign in with a device" ("Approving a sign-in from another device"), which does hand over the user key, is a separate flow and stays as it is.

### Who is asked, and when
- **A device it has never used:** a password sign-in from a device identifier that has never finished signing in to this account (upstream keeps devices by identifier and account, and marks one that hasn't finished a sign-in as new), or from one that can't show its device token (below). A device that has, and shows its token, is never asked again: signing out and in, a new master password, and "Deauthorize sessions" end sessions, not devices.
- **The device token (our decision, security):** device identifiers aren't secrets ("Login limits"): every sign-in carries one, and a shared computer, a proxy's log, or malware can give one away. A check that trusted the identifier alone would let anyone with the password and one of the person's identifiers skip it. So a device that gets in to an account without two-step login (through the check, or as the account's first) gets a token of its own (32 random bytes, kept on the server only as its SHA-256, by account and device; a device that has to pass the check again gets a new one), in upstream's `TwoFactorToken` answer. Upstream's web vault, extension, and CLI already keep that answer for the email and send it back at the next sign-in as two-step login's "remember" token (provider 5), so they need no change for it; our apps keep it in the OS's secret store beside the account's refresh token, by server and email, and keep it at sign-out (`docs/core.md`, "Signing in"). An identifier without its token, or with another device's, is a new device. Devices from before the check have no token, so each is asked once (there are no users yet). Two-step login's own remembered devices are a different thing, and work as upstream's.
- **The account's first sign-in isn't asked:** when no device has ever finished signing in to the account (it was just made, or its invitation was just accepted), there's no device to approve from, and nothing in it yet. Every later new device is asked.
- **Two-step login skips it (the owner's decision):** an account with any two-step method on (an authenticator app, email codes, a security key or passkey, a YubiKey, or Duo) proves its second step on every new device already, so it isn't asked. A sign-in with a two-step recovery code isn't asked either: the code is a second secret too. The device token isn't issued to such an account; its sign-ins carry two-step login's own token when the person asks to be remembered.
- **Single sign-on isn't asked:** the organization's identity provider signs the person in, with its own rules for MFA and devices (its Conditional Access), and the master password only unlocks. A password sign-in by someone with single sign-on linked, where it isn't required, is asked as any password sign-in.
- **API keys aren't asked** (the CLI's `client_credentials`): the API key is a second secret the master password doesn't give, and only a signed-in session shows it, after the master password again. Upstream's new-device email still goes out. The CLI with a password is asked: it shows the number and waits, or takes an emailed code. The CLI never approves.
- **Refreshing a session is never asked:** that device signed in already.
- **Sign in with a device** now asks only from a device that has finished a sign-in to the account. Before, any device the account had a row for could ask, and a password sign-in held by this check makes such a row, so a held device could have turned to the one-click approval in a browser instead. `GET /devices/knowndevice` answers the same way.
- **Admins and MSP staff whom two-step login is required of, without a method that counts yet:** asked, before the setup session ("Two-step login for admins"), so a password alone on a new device can't set up an authenticator app and take over the account; the email code isn't offered to them (below). With a method that counts, they have two-step login, so they aren't asked.
- **Where it sits in the sign-in:** after the password, upstream's checks (a disabled account, an unverified email), and two-step login, and before the single sign-on linking session and the two-step setup session, so neither can be reached with a password alone on a new device. A wrong password says nothing about the check.
- **The server's setting:** `NEW_DEVICE_CHECK`, on by default. There's no setting for an account or an organization: two-step login is how a person replaces it, and an organization can't require what everyone already has. An operator can turn it off for the whole server (the test servers do, except the ones that test it).

### Approving
- **The answer to a held sign-in** is a 400 `invalid_grant` with upstream's message for new device verification ("new device verification required"), so upstream's clients keep the sign-in waiting as they do for upstream's emailed check, plus `KeykeeperNewDeviceCheck`: its id, a token for this device (43 characters; kept as its SHA-256), the number, when it ends, whether any device can approve, and whether a code by email can be sent now, later, or not at all.
- **The number:** two digits, 10 to 99, from the OS's random source, shown only on the new device, never in any list or answer the account's other devices get. Why two (our decision): each check takes one try, and a wrong number denies it, so length doesn't stand against guessing; the number's job is to make the person look at the new device, and two digits read at a glance and type quickly on a phone. A random guess matches one time in 90. Three digits would make that one in 900 and protect against nothing else (someone talked into it reads any length aloud); it's one constant (`NUMBER_DIGITS`).
- **Signing in again from the same device** while its check waits (a reload, the extension's popup closing, the CLI run again) finds the same check, with the same number and a new token; the old token stops working. The web vault's and extension's waiting page does that when it has lost its check.
- **The approving device** lists the account's waiting checks (`GET /api/keykeeper/new-device-checks`, every 10 seconds while the vault is unlocked and on screen) and shows each in a prompt: "Approve this sign-in?", the device ("Web vault in Firefox", "Extension in Chrome", "PassBundle on Windows", "CLI on Linux"), where it comes from ("Berlin, Germany", or "Unknown location"), its address, when it started, and how long is left. Then a field for "the number on the new device", Approve, Deny, and Not now. It says to approve only a sign-in the person started themselves, on a device in front of them.
- **Typing it:** Approve sends the number (`POST /api/keykeeper/new-device-checks/<id>/approve`, `{ "number": "47" }`). The right number approves the check; the new device, which asks every 3 seconds (`POST /api/keykeeper/new-device-check/status` with its id and token, never in the address), sees it and sends its sign-in again with the token (`newDeviceOtp`, upstream's field), from the same device identifier and type, and is signed in. A request without a number, or with anything but two digits, is refused and changes nothing, so no client can approve, or deny, with a tap.
- **A wrong number denies it** at once (our decision): one try, so typing numbers until one fits isn't possible. The new device sees "denied", the person's email gets "A sign-in on a new device was denied" with the device, where it came from, and why (a wrong number, or Deny), and the event log has it. Starting the sign-in again makes a new check with a new number.
- **Deny** (`POST /api/keykeeper/new-device-checks/<id>/deny`) does the same without a number. Not now leaves it waiting; the prompt comes back after 2 minutes if it's still there.
- **Who approves (our decision):** a signed-in session of that account, from one of its devices other than the new one: a session that began with the master password, the organization's single sign-on, or a device's approval. Not an API key's, and not a setup or linking session, which aren't login tokens. The web vault, the extension, and our apps ask only while the vault is unlocked. The server can't see whether a vault is unlocked, and doesn't need to: someone who holds a session token and the master password has the vault already, and the number is shown only on the new device.
- **How long:** 10 minutes from the first sign-in attempt. The waiting page keeps the web vault's and the extension's sign-in in memory that long (upstream keeps it 5 minutes). After that, it's gone, and checking it, approving it, or using its token answers 404 the same as one that never was.
- **Limits** (not tested, as no login limit is): one check per device at a time, at most 3 waiting per account (a fourth sign-in gets 429), 5 new checks an hour from one device, the account's login limits for every sign-in, the address's limit for the status calls, and for email codes, 3 sends and 5 tries per check.
- **Events,** in the account's organizations' logs: 1090 a check started (low), 1091 approved (with how: from a device, an email code, or waiting), 1092 denied (high, with why), 1093 an emailed code asked for later (high). The sign-in itself is upstream's 1000.

### The email code (weaker)
- **When no device can approve:** a device can approve when it's one of the account's devices of a kind that has the prompt (the web vault, the extension, or our desktop and phone apps; not the CLI), has finished a sign-in, and was used in the last 30 days (90 for a phone), how long its session lasts unused. With none, the waiting page offers "Email me a code" straight away (the owner's rule): 8 digits, good for the check's 10 minutes, kept as a hash, sent to the account's email, with the device and where it comes from.
- **It's weaker, and says so:** a code by email is the master password plus the mailbox, and a reused password often opens both, which is the most common way in. Approving from a device is the master password plus something the person holds. The waiting page puts the number first and the email second, and the DESIGN and the email say which is stronger. Two-step login is stronger than either.
- **When devices can approve but the person can't reach them** (a broken phone, a lost laptop, all of them signed in for 90 more days): "Can't use your other devices?" offers a code by email after 24 hours (our decision). The account's email and devices hear at once ("Someone asked to sign in on a new device with an emailed code instead"), and any of the account's devices can deny it meanwhile. After 24 hours, signing in again on that device offers to send the code. Without this, a person whose only device broke would be locked out for up to 90 days; sending the code at once would make the check no stronger than email for anyone. One such request waits per account.
- **Admins and MSP staff whom two-step login is required of:** no email code, at once or later (the owner's suggestion, and the admin rule's own: email codes don't count for them, since an organization's email admins can read a member's mailbox). They approve from a device, or sign in on a device they've used before, which isn't asked, and set up two-step login there; after that, new devices take their two-step login instead. One who can reach none of their devices waits for an owner (or their MSP) to change their role or the rule.
- **A server that sends no email:** with no device that can approve, the sign-in goes through, as before this check, and its event says so; a self-hosted server without email has no other way to let someone back in. With devices, "Can't use your other devices?" lets the sign-in through after the same 24 hours, without a code.

### Where a sign-in comes from
- **No outside service, ever:** no call to anyone per sign-in. The server looks the address up in an IP-to-location database file on its own disk (`IP_LOCATION_DATABASE`, default `data/ip-location.mmdb`). Without the file, no location is shown anywhere, and nothing else changes.
- **The database (our pick, license checked November 8, 2026):** DB-IP's "IP to City Lite" (or "IP to Country Lite", for country only), free, monthly, in the MaxMind DB format, under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License (CC BY 4.0). DB-IP's download page asks for credit, with a link to db-ip.com, on pages that show its results ("IP Geolocation by DB-IP"). Any database in that format with GeoLite2's layout (`country.iso_code`, `country.names.en`, `city.names.en`) works, under its own license.
- **The attribution:** next to every location shown (the prompt, the event log, the emails), from the server (`IP_LOCATION_ATTRIBUTION` and `IP_LOCATION_ATTRIBUTION_URL`, DB-IP's by default, so a server using another database credits that one), and in the legal notices: the website's Terms ("Notices") and passbundle-server's README.
- **Getting the file:** it's 8 MB (country) to about 120 MB compressed (city), changes every month, and isn't ours to put in a repo or image, so neither has it. `tools/update-ip-location.sh` in passbundle-server downloads this month's City Lite from DB-IP (or last month's, early in a month), checks it's a MaxMind DB file, and moves it into place in one step; the hosted service runs it monthly from cron, and self-hosters can do the same (Docker: run it on the host against the data folder). The server notices a new file within a minute (its modified time), and reads it in place: a lookup reads only the few 4 KB pages it needs, never the whole file into memory. The server's reader is its own (`keykeeper_location.rs`, the MaxMind DB format's published specification), so no new crate.
- **Approximate only:** "City, Country" when the database knows the city, "Country" when it knows only that, in English. Never a region, coordinates, a postal code, or a network's owner.
- **Never stored:** the location is worked out when it's shown, from the address the check or the event already keeps; nothing more than the event log already keeps (the address) is written anywhere.
- **Unknown:** private, local, and VPN addresses show as "Unknown location": IPv4 private (10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16), loopback, link-local, carrier-grade NAT and Tailscale (100.64/10), 0/8, broadcast and multicast; IPv6 loopback, unspecified, link-local, unique local (fc00::/7, where WireGuard and Tailscale put their networks), and multicast; an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address is looked up as its IPv4 address. A VPN service's exit address shows where its server is: a free database can't tell those apart.

### The apps
- **Being the new device:** our apps sign in with the password grant, so the server holds their first sign-in on a new device too. Their sign-in shows the number, waits (asking every 3 seconds), offers the email code as the waiting page does, and finishes with the token, the same calls as the web vault's. The check's token stays in the core; the screens get the number and the states.
