- **Approving:** the apps are often the device already signed in, so their sign-in requests (the prompt over the vault, and Settings, "Sign-in requests") list the account's checks beside "Sign in with a device" requests, with a field for the number instead of the phrase, while the vault is unlocked. Approving sends no key, so it doesn't ask for biometrics or the master password first: the number, shown only on the new device, is the proof that the person is at both.
- **The device token** also makes two-step login's "Don't ask again on this device" work in the apps: the core now keeps whatever `TwoFactorToken` a sign-in gives, and drops it when the server asks for two-step login instead.
- Details: `docs/core.md` ("Signing in", "What's kept where", "Approving a sign-in") and `docs/phones.md`.

### Tests
- **Server unit tests** (`keykeeper_new_device.rs`, `keykeeper_location.rs`): the number's range, a wrong number and a missing one, the token kept only as its hash, which devices can approve (kinds, finished, 30 and 90 days, not the asking one), when email is offered (now, later, never for someone two-step login is required of), the 24 hours, expiry, the location database reader on a tiny database the test makes (a city, a country only, IPv6, an address it doesn't have, private ranges), and that the module makes no network call (its only I/O is the database file).
- **API** (`e2e/tests/api/new-device-check.spec.ts`, on a test server of its own with the check on, email, and a tiny location database from `e2e/fixtures/location/make.mjs`): a new device waits; the first sign-in isn't asked; a known device with its token isn't asked, and the same identifier without the token is; the right number approves and the new device signs in with its token, once; a wrong number denies, with an email; a request without a number can't approve (or deny); another account, the new device itself, an API key's session, and no session can't list or answer it; expiry (the same way); the same device signing in again gets the same check; an account with two-step login isn't asked; an API key isn't asked; the email code when no device can approve, and not when one can; "later" waiting its 24 hours (the test makes the check older in the test server's SQLite database, as the device approval tests do: the server's clock can't be moved); no email for an admin two-step login is required of; the location from the test database in the list, the email, and the event log, and "Unknown location" for a private address.
- **Web vault and extension** (`e2e/tests/new-device-check.spec.ts`): signing in on a new browser shows the number; the web vault signed in elsewhere prompts, takes the number, and the new one signs in; a wrong number shows denied on both; the email code from the mail catcher; the extension as the new device and as the approver.
- **CLI** (`e2e/tests/cli/new-device-check.spec.ts`): `login` prints the number, waits, and signs in once approved.
- **Clients' unit tests** (passbundle-clients): the check's shapes and calls (`libs/common/src/auth/keykeeper/new-device-check.spec.ts`: only two digits answer, the token never in an address, the list never has the number) and the CLI's wait (`apps/cli/src/keykeeper/new-device.spec.ts`).
- **The apps** (passbundle-apps): core unit tests (the held answer, the token kept out of the screens, the email states, only two digits sent, the device token sent and dropped); the core against a real server with the check on (`PB_TEST_SERVER_NEW_DEVICE`: waiting, approving with the number from another session, finishing, the device token, signing in again, a wrong number, Deny, a locked vault answering nothing); and the app's tests (`app/test/new_device_test.dart`, `app/test/sign_in_requests_test.dart`).
- **No outside calls:** the test servers have no network beyond 127.0.0.1 (tier 1's rule), and the API test checks the location comes back with the fixture's names, which only the file has.

### Status
Built (November 2026): the server (`keykeeper_new_device.rs`, `keykeeper_location.rs`, the `keykeeper_new_device_check` migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL, the emails, `tools/update-ip-location.sh`), the web vault's and the extension's waiting page and prompt, the event log's locations, the CLI's wait, and the apps on both sides.

### Not yet
- Push to phones while PassBundle is closed, so a phone prompts without being opened (our own push service).
- Telling a VPN service's exit from a home address, which needs a commercial database.
- Location in the event streaming feed, and in the prompts for "Sign in with a device" (upstream's dialog has a place for it, but no room for the database's credit, which its license asks for next to it).
- The apps' email code against a real server (the apps' test server sends no email; their unit tests cover it, and the API tests cover the server's side).

## Browser import

Two ways in: guided import now, in the web vault and the extension, and automatic import later, through the desktop agent once it's signed. Both are optional.

**Guided import (built).** The person exports their passwords with the browser's own Export, and we take it from there.

- **Where:** the web vault (Tools, Import from your browser, with a pointer on the regular Import page, and the import step right after signing up) and the extension (Vault settings, Import from your browser, in its own window, since a file picker closes the popup). One component for both (`libs/importer/src/components/keykeeper/`).
- **Which browsers:** Chrome, Edge, Brave, and Firefox on Windows and Mac, and Safari (the Passwords app) on a Mac. It picks the person's browser and computer from the user agent, and they can pick another.
- **The steps:** that browser's export, step by step, with the confirm its computer asks for (Windows Hello, Touch ID, or the computer's password) and the file name it saves. The extension can open a Chromium browser's passwords page for them; a web page can't, so the steps say what to type.
- **The import:** upstream's importer for that browser's file, into the person's own vault. A login already there (same site, username, and password) or repeated in the file is left out, and one without a password is skipped. A login whose password differs from one they have for the same site and username is imported into a folder, "Check these: from <browser>", for them to compare. Items whose passwords the person can't see aren't compared at all (security review, November 2026): "already there" or "a different password" would confirm or rule out a guess at one. A file that isn't that browser's export is refused by name, and an export with no passwords in it (only its column names) says so. Where an organization's policy keeps items out of Personal (upstream's "Enforce organization data ownership"), it says so first, and to ask an admin for a shared folder they can add to and import there with Import and export, rather than failing at the end with upstream's "Due to an Enterprise Policy ..."; a refusal from the server is said in plain words ("Errors in plain words").
- **Afterwards:** a summary of what happened, a reminder to delete the export (it has every password in plain text), with how on that computer, and how to turn off the browser's own password saving. On managed computers the install script turns it off by policy.
- **Tests:** unit tests for detection, the steps, the sorting (with upstream's Chrome importer on a real export), an empty export, the policy, and a refused import; a web vault test (Chrome and Firefox exports, a file that isn't one, an empty one, the folder to check) and an extension test.

**Automatic import (later).** Runs after first sign-in through the desktop agent, which ships after code signing.

- Sources: Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera, and Vivaldi through the upstream GPL importer (`apps/desktop/desktop_native/chromium_importer`). Firefox is ours to write.
- Dedupe: identical URL, username, and password merge into one item. Same URL and username with different passwords: the most recently changed wins and the others go to the review queue.
- Skip "never save" entries and entries with empty passwords.
- Ends with a summary and a review queue that users clear whenever they like.

### Keeping up with upstream

- **The bases:** the server follows Vaultwarden's release tags (`passbundle-server/.keykeeper-base`) and the clients follow the web vault Vaultwarden supports (`passbundle-clients/.keykeeper-base`), not Bitwarden's newest: the server must understand the clients. The two move together.
- **Our own UI, upstream's security** (the owner's call, October 22, 2026): the web vault's and the extension's screens are ours to change as far as PassBundle needs, to look and work like its own product (familiar to Bitwarden and 1Password users, not a copy of either), and they don't have to stay mergeable with upstream. What has to stay close to upstream is the security core: the crypto, sign-in and keys, sync, the vault format, and the server. Upstream's fixes there get ported; a change of theirs to screens we've replaced can be dropped.
- **The weekly watch** (`.github/workflows/upstream-watch.yml`, `tools/upstream-watch/`) keeps one issue, "Upstream and dependency watch", open while something needs doing and closes it when nothing does. It raises a Vaultwarden release newer than our server base (flagging security fixes), Vaultwarden shipping a newer web vault than our clients base, and known vulnerabilities in what we ship: `cargo audit` of the server and the desktop agent, and `npm audit` of the clients' production dependencies.
- **Accepted advisories:** advisories for npm packages that never reach the web vault or the extension (the command-line tool's, and build tooling) are listed with a reason in `tools/upstream-watch/accepted.json`, by advisory and package (as cargo's are by advisory, crate, and version), so a new advisory for a listed package is raised until it's looked at; the watch notes the accepted ones instead of raising them. The command-line tool's were looked at again when it first shipped (October 27, 2026): none of their advisories reaches how it uses them, and the reasons say why for each.
- **Security fixes ahead of upstream:** when a fix is in a patch release of a dependency we ship and upstream hasn't taken it yet, we take it ourselves, as with Angular 21.2.25 in the clients (two cross-site scripting advisories; still ahead of upstream's web-v2026.10.1, which ships 21.2.17) and rustls 0.23.46 for the server's outgoing connections (four advisories; Vaultwarden 1.37.5 has it, so ours is gone), and drop our change at the next base move that has it. Advisories that don't apply to how we use a crate (`rsa`, which has no fix yet, or `crypto-bigint` 0.5.5, below) are accepted in the same file, by advisory and version, with a reason.
- **How a sync is done:** a merge of upstream's release tag into the base branch (`git merge <tag>`, the tag fetched from upstream: Vaultwarden's `1.37.5`, Bitwarden's `web-v2026.10.1`), with every PassBundle change kept and moved where upstream moved the code, `.keykeeper-base` set to the tag, and the PR merged as a merge commit (not squashed), so the next sync's merge base is this tag. In the clients: `bitwarden_license/` is deleted again without opening it (`git rm -r bitwarden_license`), and so are upstream's project files that come back (issue templates, workflows under `.github/`, `.claude/`); the lockfile is upstream's with only our pins changed (Angular, node-forge, no commercial SDK); and our `lint-staged.config.mjs` keeps formatting only for `apps/desktop/desktop_native`, without upstream's clippy, cargo-sort, and cargo-udeps checks, which compile Bitwarden's SDK crates for a desktop app we don't build (keep that when resolving a conflict there). Upstream files moved out of `bitwarden_license/` into the GPL tree come in like any upstream file once they no longer name the Bitwarden License (checked by name with `git grep -l`): web-v2026.10.1's browser Health tab did, hidden unless the server turns on its feature flag.
- **The November 2026 sync** (Vaultwarden 1.37.4 to 1.37.5, web vault web-v2026.8.0 to web-v2026.10.1): 1.37.5's security fixes are the website icons and emergency access advisories (GHSA-x9cq-f669-vrr4 and GHSA-9pjj-f9g5-3x4c, medium, private until their CVEs), escaped request values in logs, and the new per-method two-step login routes ("Two-step login settings"). Its Rocket 0.5.2 moves the server off rustls 0.21, so rustls-webpki 0.101.7 (RUSTSEC-2026-0382 and the three accepted before) is gone. `crypto-bigint` 0.5.5 (RUSTSEC-2026-0387, fixed only in 0.7.6) stays: it comes with `jsonwebtoken` 11.1.0's `rust_crypto` backend, through `p256` and `p384` 0.13, and no jsonwebtoken release moves them; the server never uses those curves (it signs and checks RS256 and HS512 only, and its SSO checks RS256 first), and its advisory is about timing in secret-key operations, which the server has none of with those crates. It's accepted with that reason until jsonwebtoken moves. Upstream's new Fill Assist rules feed (`FILL_ASSIST_RULES_URL`, by default Bitwarden's) is set only in the environment, and the clients fetch it only with the `fill-assist-targeting-rules` feature flag, which is off unless the operator lists it in `EXPERIMENTAL_CLIENT_FEATURE_FLAGS`. Upstream's wording moved from New to Add (the vault's Add button, "Add login", "Add folder"), and its Invite member dialog picks the role from a list; we take both. Changing an item's shared folders from the admin console goes only through upstream's SDK now (`saveCollectionsWithServerAdmin`), which we can't read, so a browser test (`e2e/tests/admin-console-folders.spec.ts`) checks the result: an admin's change leaves every encrypted value as stored, the owner opens the same password and one-time code, someone whose access hides passwords still can't see them, and someone who can edit the item without seeing its password can't change its folders.

## The look

PassBundle has its own look, not Bitwarden's with a new color: the design language below, the same in the web vault and the browser extension, so the two feel like one product, and in the apps next. Familiar to people who know Bitwarden or 1Password, but our own.

- **The design language.** Color says what a thing is; depth says what you can touch. One typeface, Google Sans Flex.
  - **Ground and depth:** a calm ground (`#EEF2F0`; dark mode's is graphite, below) and three depths on it: resting (lists, panels, the web vault's menu), raised (what's suggested or selected, the item open beside the list), and floating (menus, dialogs). Things rise as they become more temporary.
  - **Every category has a color, on a flat tile** (the owner's call, November 6, 2026; passbundle.com already draws its tiles this way, `site/static/styles.css`, "Tiles"): a rounded square tinted with the category's jewel tone, the tone at 11% over whatever the tile sits on, with the glyph drawn in the tone itself; in dark mode a lighter, softer tone for both, at 14%. No white glyph, no sheen, no inner shading, and no drop shadow: only the logo's tile is glossy (see "The logo"). The tones, light and dark: login sapphire `#2448B8` and `#93ADFF`, secure note topaz `#A9600C` and `#F2B766`, card amethyst `#6B30B8` and `#C6A3FF`, identity turquoise `#08788A` and `#62D3E3`, SSH key onyx `#353B4A` and `#C3C9D6`, bank account emerald `#0E7A47` and `#6FDBA0`, and for templates Wi-Fi aquamarine `#1C7FB8` and `#7CC4EF`, server garnet `#A8291F` and `#FF9D92`, and license ruby `#B01E54` and `#FF92B8`; "All" is the logo's emerald `#0E7A5A` and `#5FD6AE`. The web vault's menu adds Favorites in gold `#946A0C` and `#ECC25E` (a shade deeper than the star's `#B98310`, whose glyph falls under 3:1 on its tint) and Health in a bank's emerald. The glyph keeps 3:1 or more on its tile, composited over every surface a tile sits on (the ground, cards, the picked entry, chips, menus, and dark mode's washes), in both themes (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/categories.ts` in the clients and `app/lib/theme.dart` in the apps, each with a test). The same tile is in the menu, the list, the New picker, an item's top, the Health reports, the extension's chips and New menu, the fill menu inside websites, and the apps.
  - **Items show their site:** the site's own icon on a white tile when the server has one; otherwise the item's first letter on a tint of the login color (`#E3E9F8` with `#1D3C99`, dark `#1C2A4A` with `#9DB4F2`), so a list is never a column of identical keys. The server's stand-in for a site without an icon (Vaultwarden's 19 by 19 pixel globe) counts as none. Other items show their category's tile.
  - **Buttons:** rounded rectangles (10px), not pills. Primary is the brand's green with white text (6:1), dark mode's is emerald `#6FD4B4` with dark text `#06231B` (9:1); secondary is the field color with a hairline.
- **Dark mode: graphite glass.** Neutral, a little glassy, never green, and the same on every platform:
  - **Ground:** `#111318` with faint jewel washes, sapphire at the top left, amethyst at the top right, and emerald along the bottom (`radial-gradient(75% 55% at 0% 0%, rgba(36,72,184,.24), transparent 72%), radial-gradient(60% 40% at 100% 10%, rgba(107,48,184,.14), transparent 70%), radial-gradient(80% 50% at 50% 100%, rgba(14,122,90,.12), transparent 70%)`). The sapphire has a little more presence than the others; the mockup's amethyst and emerald (0.22, 0.18) are toned down, so they read as a hint at a full page and secondary text keeps 4.5:1 where they're brightest.
  - **Cards and panels:** translucent, `rgba(255,255,255,.055)` (raised `.085`) with a 1px `rgba(255,255,255,.11)` edge; fields `rgba(255,255,255,.06)`, lines `.09`. No backdrop blur anywhere: it draws differently from browser to browser and costs performance. Menus, which float over content that isn't dimmed, are solid `#1C1F25`, so nothing shows through them. Dialogs float over the page dimmed nearly to the ground (85%): they're the same translucent card (`.07`) laid over the ground, so the dimmed page never shows through, with a stronger edge (`.16`) and a shadow, and lift off it. Upstream's own surfaces get solid equivalents, so nothing of theirs turns see-through by accident.
  - **Text:** `#EEF0F3`, labels `#C9CED6`, secondary and placeholders `#A9AFB8` (row subtitles, the menu's headings and counts), where-it-lives chips `#D0D4DB` on a `.10` fill with a `.12` edge, and error text and required marks `#FF8A85`. Emerald `#6FD4B4` is only the accent: primary buttons with dark text, focus rings, selection, and links, never the ground. The grays upstream draws from are neutral graphite in dark mode, where light mode's lean toward the brand's green.
  - **One token block:** every dark-mode color is in one block of `--pb-*` tokens in `brand/look.css` (`:root.theme_dark`), with upstream's tokens mapped onto them, so the owner can swap the set. Two alternates, as token sets: **plain graphite**, `--pb-ground: #121417`, `--pb-wash: radial-gradient(80% 50% at 50% 0%, rgba(14,122,90,.12), transparent 70%)` (a faint emerald glow at the top only), `--pb-surface`, `--pb-raised`, and `--pb-field: #1A1D21`, `--pb-edge` and `--pb-line: #262A30`; and **true black**, `--pb-ground: #000000`, `--pb-wash: none`, `--pb-surface`, `--pb-raised`, and `--pb-field: #111214`, `--pb-edge` and `--pb-line: #1E2023`. Graphite glass ships.
  - **Inside websites:** the fill menu and the save bar can't read the page's tokens, so they carry graphite's solid colors in their own two palettes (`apps/browser/src/autofill/shared/styles/variables.scss` and `content/components/constants/styles.ts`): `#1C1F25` over `#111318`, the same text colors, and emerald buttons with dark text.
- **The web vault** (`html.pb-web`, set in `index.html` so the first paint has it) has the design language in every theme: Settings, Appearance, Theme offers System default (light or dark like the device), Light, and Dark.
  - **The menu** is a white card (dark: translucent) in the corner, resting on the ground: the logo's glossy tile and two-tone name at the top, the search, then All items, Favorites, and Health with their 28px tiles, Health's count of items to fix as a topaz pill; Categories, each with its tile and count; Folders by owner, Personal and each organization in bold over their folders, then Archive and Trash; a line; and the tools (Generator, Send, Import and export, the MSP console, Settings) in the label color. The entry showing has the selection's tint; entries start 10px into their pill, folders 20px further in each level, and a group's arrow sits at the pill's end. The admin console's and the provider portal's menus are the same card. See "The left menu".
  - **The vault's list:** the page's title and Add; the getting-started checklist, while it shows, as one quiet tinted card with a small title and secondary buttons; then the list as rows on the ground: each row's 40px tile (the site's icon, the letter, or the category's tile), its name, its username or what it is, where it lives as a quiet chip (Personal or the organization), and Options. The whole row opens the item. The row whose item is open is raised, with the selection's ring (it's the item in the address, `itemId`). Rows are 64px.
  - **The item opens in a side panel** to the right of the list instead of a dialog over it (upstream's drawer, `VaultItemDialogComponent.openDrawer`; where there's no room for both, as on a phone, it takes the page): the item's big tile, its name, and "Login in Personal · Work" on top, with Edit and Close in the corner; its fields on one inset card, a label over each value, with tinted copy buttons and the two-step code's ring in the brand's green; the website as a link; the password's health as a quiet line with a dot in the strength's color (green strong, amber good, red weak), as in the extension; and its history, quietly. Archive, print, and delete stay at the bottom. Picking another row shows that item in the panel. Edit works in place in the panel, and while the item there has unsaved edits, anything that would close it (Close, Escape, another row, another item from the address) asks "Discard edits" first; Back to editing keeps them. A new item, and an item opened to edit from a row's Options, open in a dialog as before, which closes the panel first, unless its item has unsaved edits: then the panel stays under the dialog, each with a form of its own, and the address names the panel's item again once the dialog closes. The page keeps the panel and a dialog apart and reads whether the panel's item is being edited from the panel, not the address. Another page closes the panel. A link to an item to view (Back from another page, the MSP console's links) opens it in the panel with the list loading beside it. To assistive technology the panel is still a dialog named by its title ("View Login"), without the dimmed page. The admin console's vault keeps its dialogs.
  - **Health:** each report is a resting card with its own flat tile (`apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/health/report-tiles.ts`, by the report's route: exposed garnet, reused topaz, weak ruby, unsecure websites aquamarine, inactive two-step login turquoise, data breach amethyst, passkeys sapphire, member access emerald, expiring items onyx) in place of upstream's illustration on a block of the brand's color; the reports list their items with item tiles.
  - **Everything else** follows from the tokens and a few rules for upstream's components: pages' titles at 24px, cards and tables as resting cards, Send, the Generator, Import and export, Settings, the admin console's pages, and the MSP console. The Danger zone's buttons are tinted red with a red edge, not solid; the dialog that asks to confirm keeps a solid red button for the last step. The New picker shows each kind's and template's tile. Sign-in, unlock, and the other pages before the vault: the logo in the corner and the form on a raised card on the ground.
- **The extension's popup** has it in every theme (`brand/look.css`, under `html.pb-popup`, and small components of ours): Settings, Appearance, Theme offers System (light or dark like the device), Light, and Dark.
  - **Sign-in** asks "Where's your vault?" first: PassBundle or Self-hosted, a two-way switch (Self-hosted, as the apps say it, rather than "My own server", which an employee's company server isn't), with the server's address right under it for one's own ("Just the address. We add https:// for you."). It sets the same environment upstream's region menu and self-hosted dialog do, through the dialog's own rules: no scheme means https://, only https:// is accepted, and an address that isn't just a server (a user name or password before the host, as in "passbundle.com@evil.example", or a query or fragment) is refused, in the dialog too. Sign-in never goes to a server other than the one shown: while Self-hosted is picked and its address is blank or refused, Continue, the passkey, and single sign-on are off (Enter too), going on first saves an address typed and not saved yet, and the switch and the field follow the saved environment whenever it changes. An address is saved only once a server answers there (November 2026): the field asks it for its settings (`GET <address>/api/config`, which every server the extension signs in to answers without signing in; nothing but the request is sent, no credentials or cookies), and says under the field what's wrong: nothing answered ("Check it for typos, and that you're online"), it isn't a server to sign in to (a website, a typo's parked domain), it had a problem, or it didn't answer in 15 seconds. Leaving the field and Continue share one check, and an address typed over while it ran isn't saved. Before, a typo was saved as it was and showed up after the master password as "An unexpected error has occurred." (`libs/angular/src/auth/keykeeper/server-check.ts`). The password step names the server the password goes to ("alex@contoso.example · vault.example.com", the identity server's host as the URL parser reads it, so an internationalized name shows in punycode). An environment with custom addresses per service (from the dialog or a managed policy) says so, with a link to the dialog, which stays on the other sign-in pages too. Then email, Continue, Sign in with a passkey, and single sign-on as a quiet link. Under it: "New here? Your admin sends the invite.", or a Create account link on a server that lets people sign up.
  - **Items** (the first tab): the search and Add (+) on top, then the category chips (All and each kind of item there is, each with its tiny tile; they replace upstream's Type filter), and the folder filter beside them (a folder button opening every place an item lives, grouped by owner, with counts; the place picked shows as a chip under the chips; see "Folders and permissions"), which shows only when there's more than Personal to pick. "For contoso.example" lists what matches the site in the current tab, raised, with a Fill button (upstream's "Item suggestions" on a site on the autofill blocklist); Favorites and All items are one resting card each, each row its tile, name, username, and a copy button, with More showing under the pointer or focus. The tabs at the bottom are upstream's, restyled: Vault, Generator, Send, Settings, drawn as a padlock, a die, a paper plane, and a gear.
  - **An item:** back, the favorite star (saved from a fresh copy of the item, as the list's More menu does), and Edit on top; the item's big tile, its name, and "Login in Personal" or "Login in Contoso IT" (the template's name for an item made from one, then its folders); Fill on this page; its fields on one card, each with a tinted copy button; its website; and a quiet line on its password's health ("Password strong · changed 3 weeks ago", zxcvbn's score as in the health report). Its history card links to Version history, a page of its own ("Item history"). Archive and delete stay at the bottom.
  - **The fill menu** inside websites shows the same tiles.
- **Identity in every theme:** Google Sans Flex (SIL Open Font License, self-hosted; its license is in `brand/assets/fonts`), the brand palette, a gray scale tinted toward the brand's green in light mode and neutral graphite in dark mode, instead of upstream's slate blue, and the illustrations' colors. Inter, upstream's font, still draws scripts Google Sans Flex's files don't cover (Cyrillic, Greek, Vietnamese).
- **Type:** Google Sans Flex everywhere: the web vault, the extension (its popup, and the autofill menu and save bar it draws inside websites), the apps, and passbundle.com. One variable file per script (Latin, Latin Extended) with the weight axis, made from the google/fonts release by `brand/build-fonts.sh`; the brand step adds the font to the component library's shared `theme.css`, which every surface loads. Never from Google's servers: the content security policy allows fonts only from the site itself, and the website promises no third-party requests.
- **The logo:** the one glossy tile, a jewel (a rounded square in emerald `#0E7A5A`, with a soft highlight, a light top edge, a shaded bottom edge, and a soft shadow on light backgrounds) holding the lock in white, then the name in lower case, two-tone, in Google Sans Flex with -0.02em tracking: "pass" at weight 700 and "bundle" at 450, `#1B3A33` and `#0E7A5A` on light backgrounds, `#E7EFEC` and `#6FD4B4` on dark ones (tokens in `brand/look.css`, so the menu's logo follows the theme). One line everywhere it appears: the corner of every product's navigation (the web vault, the admin console, the provider portal), the sign-in pages, the extension's, and passbundle.com's header and footer; the navigation closed to icons shows the tile alone. Which product you're in is in the logo link's label and the product switcher. The name stays "PassBundle" in text and accessible names; only the drawn wordmark is lower case. `brand/wordmark.py` (run by `brand/render-assets.sh`) draws the letters as outlines, shaped at each weight of the variable font, not as SVG text, which would show in whatever font the viewer has; the website, which serves the font itself, sets the name as text. The gloss is the logo's alone: the logo in the web vault's menu and on the sign-in pages, the extension's, the apps' (`LogoTile`), the favicon, the apps' launcher icons, and the extension's toolbar icon keep it; every other tile is flat (see "Every category has a color"). passbundle.com's header draws it glossy too.
- **Readable everywhere:** every text and background pair meets WCAG AA (4.5:1, or 3:1 for large text), checked from screenshots in light and dark: for each text, its color over the page drawn without text, so translucent cards over the washes count as they're composited. Placeholders use the muted gray.
- **Artwork:** every illustration and tab icon upstream draws in Bitwarden's style (55 of them) is redrawn from the logo's shapes, the rounded bands and the shackle, by `brand/artwork/draw.mjs`. The brand step puts each in place of upstream's drawing by its name, and fails if upstream renames one. Drawings use the theme's color classes, so they follow light and dark. Duo's and Yubico's logos stay theirs; the product logos are swapped by the brand step as before.
- **How:** `brand/look.css` is added to the component library's theme at build time. It targets upstream's components (`bit-*`, `popup-*`) rather than its utility classes, so changes inside them don't break it. The web vault's page says `pb-web` and the popup's `pb-popup` from the start; the theming is upstream's again (System follows the device), with no Glass and nothing else on the page. The palette is the `--pb-*` tokens, one block for light and one for dark, with upstream's tokens (`--color-*`) mapped onto them, so upstream's components follow and ours read the tokens directly. Component changes for the layout are small and marked `// Keykeeper:`: an icon slot in upstream's nav item (for the tiles), the item tile in the vault's and the reports' rows, the vault page opening an item to view in the drawer, and the item dialog in a drawer showing the item's top and Edit up top.
- **Not glass:** nothing uses backdrop blur. A desktop window, when there is one, can use the operating system's own translucency (macOS vibrancy, Windows Mica).
- **Tests:** unit tests for the theming (System follows the device, no Glass), the categories (each kind's and template's, and each tile's glyph at 3:1 on its tint over every surface it sits on, light and dark, Favorites' and Health's too), the tiles (site icon, letter, the server's stand-in, category, a size of their own), the chips, the sign-in's "Where's your vault?", the item's top (its heading one level under the page's in the web vault) and its password's health line (its strength's dot), the fill menu's tiles, the web vault's menu (its tiles in place of icons, the owners), the open row's mark, the New picker's tiles, the Health reports' tiles (one for every report, the glyph at 3:1 on its tint), the item dialog (a form id of its own; in the panel, asking before unsaved edits go), and the vault page's side panel (opened to view, another row taking its place, the address kept or cleared, closed before a dialog or kept under it with unsaved edits, an item from a link opened once and beside the loaded list); end-to-end tests that the web vault starts light or dark like the device and never Glass, has the calm ground and the menu as a white card, graphite with washes and a translucent menu in dark mode, offers System default, Light, and Dark and keeps the choice across a reload, uses Google Sans Flex, shows none of upstream's drawings, shows the tiles in the menu and the list, and opens an item in the side panel beside the list (another row replacing it, Edit in place, Close, another page closing it), keeps an item's unsaved edits in the panel while a new item is saved in a dialog, asks before they go, and opens a link to an item beside the loaded list; and ones that the popup won't go on with a blank or refused server address, or one no server answers at (a typo, a site that isn't a server), saying so under the field (and names the server on the password step), signs in to one's own server typed without https://, has the calm ground light and graphite dark with a translucent card, filters by the chips, shows a login's letter and a card's tile, and stars an item from its page.

## The left menu

The web vault has one menu on the left, like 1Password's sidebar, instead of upstream's short nav plus a separate filter panel beside the item list. Top to bottom: a search box ("Search all items"), All items, Favorites, Health (the Reports page), then Categories, and Folders (with a New folder button beside the heading that asks where the folder goes: Personal or an organization the person can make folders in; then Personal with its folders under it, each organization with its shared folders under it, then Archive and Trash; see "Folders and permissions"); a line; then the tools: Generator, Send, Import and export, the MSP console for MSP staff, and Settings. The list gets the page's full width.

- **Same filtering as before:** the menu is upstream's filter panel underneath (`apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/vault-menu/`): the same sections and actions, on the same filter in the address (`type`, `folderId`, `collectionId`, `organizationId`, `search`), so picking an entry shows what picking it in the panel did. Personal and each organization are a scope, as vaults were: a category picked inside one shows only its items, the scope outlined and the entry filled. A personal folder shows everything its owner filed there, whatever the scope; "No folder", under Personal, shows Personal's items that aren't in a folder. All items clears everything.
- **The title says what's picked:** the vault page's title is the name of the entry showing (All items, a category, Personal, an organization, a folder, Favorites, Archive, or Trash), where upstream said "All vaults" or "<organization> vault". Inside an organization, the header's breadcrumb and heading are the organization's name alone.
- **Counts:** each entry shows how many items the list will show once it's picked, as things are now (so inside Personal or an organization, its categories count its items). Health shows how many items to fix instead, as an amber pill (hidden at none): items with a weak, reused, or exposed password, or that have expired or expire within 30 days, each item once, by the reports' own rules, so it matches what Health lists (see "Exposed passwords" and "Expiration dates"). "Expiring soon" sits under Favorites while anything is expiring (or while it's picked), with its count. It's worked out after the vault is unlocked and has shown (3 seconds, then 2 seconds after the items stop changing), a few passwords at a time so the page stays responsive: weak and reused first, then exposed once the breach check answers. If the check can't be made, it counts weak and reused and the pill shows a sign that it couldn't check (even with nothing else to fix). If the person turned off "Check my passwords against known breaches after unlocking" (Settings, Appearance), it counts weak and reused and nothing is sent. Empty categories stay hidden, as before; "No folder" shows only next to real folders.
- **On every page:** off the vault (Send, Settings), the menu is the same, nothing in it is picked, and picking an entry opens the vault with it.
- **Phone width:** below the width where upstream's icon rail fits, there was no way to open the menu; page headers there get a Menu button that opens it over the page, and picking an entry or another page closes it. The list fades out at its bottom edge instead of cutting an entry in half, and "More from PassBundle" is a heading like the others. The header's product switcher steps aside there (the menu has it). Real phones get the desktop layout, zoomed, as upstream sets it.
- **The admin console** and organization vault pages keep upstream's nav and filter panel, in the same card.
- **The look** of the menu (the card, the tiles for All items, Favorites, Health, and the categories, the folders' owners in bold) is in "The look".
- **Tests:** unit tests for the menu (order, counts, scopes, picks through the routed filter, search, the phone behavior, the title it gives the page) and the layout's order; tier 1 filters a vault through the menu (categories, a folder made from it, All items, search, the title following each pick), checks the tools' order and picking from another page, and the Menu button and checklist (all of it showing) at a phone's width.

## Words

The same words in the web vault, the extension, the apps, and the site (audit, November 2026):

- **Sign in and sign out**, never log in or log out. Upstream's English strings that said it are reworded in passbundle-clients' `brand/wording.en.json`, about 100 of them: the sign-in page's title and button, Sign in with a passkey, Sign in with a device, sign-in requests and their approvals, the timeout action, the account menu, and the messages that sign people out. "Login" stays the kind of item, and "two-step login" stays the feature's name. Strings that upstream's newer English dropped are left out, so the brand step doesn't fail when the clients' base moves. The CLI and the site's header still say "log in"; they're their own change.
- **The product's name comes from brand.json**, never typed into our pages. The brand step adds it to the English messages as `keykeeperProductName`, which our pages use (single sign-on, provisioning, and the MSP console's client actions).
- **And the rest:** US English ("canceled"); "two-step login", not "2FA"; "the web vault" (see "Where 'vault' stays"); "Personal" or "your items" where a place is meant; "Self-hosted" on the extension's sign-in, as the apps say it; "your IT admin", not "your IT"; and no "us" on a server that may not be ours. Upstream's FIXMEs no longer sit in our own files: the left menu says why it keeps upstream's change detection, and our CLI specs are type checked like the rest.
- **Tests:** the brand step fails when a reworded key is gone upstream; tier 1 clicks and reads the new words (Sign in, Sign out, Sign in with a passkey, Sign in with a device, Self-hosted).

## UX we commit to

Fixes for things that annoy users of Bitwarden and 1Password today.

- **One unlock:** unlocking the desktop agent unlocks the browser extension, and the other way around, over the existing authenticated native-messaging channel. Locking either one locks both.
- **Biometrics by default:** Touch ID on Mac and Windows Hello on Windows are offered at first unlock, with no separate "browser integration" setup. Admins control biometrics and password requirements in the portal.
- **Survives profile migrations:** tools like ForensiT Profile Wizard change the Windows user's SID, so locally protected data (DPAPI, Windows Hello keys) stops decrypting. Upstream's extension then works once and needs a reinstall. Ours detects local state it can't decrypt, clears it, and asks the user to sign in again. Never a broken extension.
- **Custom fields are one tap away** in every item editor (web, extension, desktop, mobile), not buried in a menu.
- **Context-aware new items:** creating an item while viewing a folder or collection puts it there.
- **Onboarding day works:** upstream rate-limits logins per IP (a burst of 10, then one per minute) and signup, pre-login, and hint requests per IP (a shared burst of 50, then one per minute). A whole office behind one NAT address would get stuck after ten to twenty-five people. Built: the strict limit is per account now, and an address's limits fit an office (see "Login limits"). Later: allowances for an org's known addresses and enrolled devices.
- **No nags:** upstream's web vault interrupts every fresh login with an extension page, an "Are you sure?" dialog, and a welcome tour. All three go. Extension setup happens through onboarding instead.
- **New item picker (built, web vault):** Add opens "What are you saving?": Login, Secure note, Card, and Identity as big tiles, and "More categories" for the other item types and every template (built-in, your own, your organizations', your MSP's), with a search and links to make a template. Folder and Collection are buttons at the bottom. Like 1Password's, so what people save most is one click and the rest is one more (`libs/vault/src/keykeeper/new-item-dialog.component.ts`). The admin console keeps upstream's menu for now.
- **Only categories with items** show in the left menu (Favorites too, once something is one); the one being viewed stays while it's open.

## Errors in plain words

When something goes wrong, our pages say what happened in plain words and what to do next, never the raw error. The audit of November 2026 found "Error: ...", "TypeError: Failed to fetch", an empty toast when a proxy answered a 502 with a web page, and upstream's "Invalid TOTP code! Server time: ... IP: ..." on "Set up two-step login to continue".

- **One helper** (`plainErrorMessage` in `libs/common/src/platform/keykeeper/plain-errors.ts`) for every page and notice of ours in the web vault and the extension, in place of the 19 copied handlers: the MSP console (its sections, and why it couldn't load), an organization's settings cards (joining, install command, single sign-on, provisioning, script access, two-step login for admins), New organization, the two-step setup page, the single sign-on link page, the install token prompt, the extension's join notices and site problem report, the background MSP work's reports, and item history.
  - **No answer** (offline, a name that doesn't resolve, a refused connection, a rejected certificate): "Couldn't reach the server. Check your internet connection, then try again." No answer in time: "The server took too long to answer. Try again in a minute."
  - **The server's own problems** (500 and up, a proxy's 502 or 504 included) and too many tries (429) get our words, whatever came with them.
  - **Otherwise the server's sentence:** our endpoints' refusals are already plain (a 400 with a plain message; see "Running the server safely"). Upstream's that aren't plain, or say too much, are reworded from one table: a wrong or malformed authenticator code (with what to check, and without the server's time and the person's address), a bad setup key, a wrong master password, a security key the server can't set up, an item Personal can't hold under an organization's policy, a stale copy of an item, and a short "... not found".
  - **Never the server's insides:** a sentence with an IP address, the server's time, a timestamp, a web page, database or crash text, or a dump of data, or one over 400 characters, never shows; ours for its status does (401 sign in again, 403 ask an admin, 404 reload the page, anything else try again).
  - **Our code's own errors** carry their sentence (without a leading "Error: "). JavaScript's own (TypeError, SyntaxError, and the like: a bug, or an answer that couldn't be read) never reach people as they are, nor a browser's DOMException (a security key cancelled or timed out reads "Your browser stopped it, or it took too long. Try again.").
- **Never more than before:** it only removes or rewords what an error said. It isn't used for signing in, where upstream answers a wrong email and a wrong password alike, so nobody learns which accounts exist: its pages come after a sign-in, or in the two-step setup and single sign-on linking sessions a sign-in started. It makes no requests of its own, so it can't add to upstream's sign-out on any 401 or 403 (see "Running the server safely"); it only words what shows before that.
- **Decided (November 2026):** the rewording is on the client side; the server's upstream text (`api/core/two_factor/authenticator.rs`) stays as Vaultwarden has it, so moving the server's base doesn't conflict. The sentences are English, in one table, like the rest of our pages' text, for when our strings are translated.
- **Next steps where there were none:** the two-step setup page offers Try again when it can't load, and says when a security key is already set up; item history shows the server's reason for a refused restore, delete, or clear; the browser import says first when an organization's policy keeps items out of Personal, and that an export with no passwords has none (see "Browser import"); a missing organization key says it's waiting for an owner or admin to confirm you, which happens on its own once one of them has the web vault or the extension open and unlocked (for MSP staff in a client, one of its admins or another of the MSP's), where it said "Reload and try again"; a template update that didn't reach every client lists what didn't work and says to use Update, where it said "Not yet:"; and Activity reads an event it has no sentence for yet as words ("Client seat report in Acme Dental (by ana@northwind.example)"), never its code. "Where's your vault?" checks the address before saving it (see "The look").
- **Tests:** jest specs for the helper (each kind of error, each reworded sentence, the insides it hides, a next step in each of our sentences), the server check and the sign-in's use of it, the two-step setup page (a wrong code, a proxy's page, offline, Try again keeping the password's hash, an ended session), item history in the extension and the web vault, the browser import, first run, and Activity; tier 1: the extension's sign-in refuses a typo and a site that isn't a server under the field (`extension-look.spec.ts`), the setup page words a wrong code plainly (`admin-two-step.spec.ts`), and the browser import says an export with no passwords has none (`browser-import.spec.ts`).
- **Status:** built in the web vault and the extension. The apps' core errors are their own work (in `passbundle-apps`).

## Item templates

Like 1Password's item types (API key, server, Wi-Fi, software license, passport, and so on), plus custom templates that people, families, and MSPs define.

- **Built-in templates** beyond upstream's types (login, card, identity, secure note, SSH key, bank account, passport, driver's license): API key, server, database, Wi-Fi network, router, software license, domain, certificate, email account, membership, and later crypto wallet. Each is an upstream base type (login or secure note) with predefined, typed fields.
- **Typed fields:** text, hidden, date, URL, email, phone, number, yes/no, one-time code. Upstream only knows text, hidden, yes/no, and linked, so values are stored as ordinary custom fields and the template supplies each field's type, which drives the editor (date picker, URL check) and display.
- **Storage, with no server changes:** an item records its template in a text field `keykeeper.template` (template id, name, and version), so its template shows even to people who can't see the template itself. A custom template is itself an item: a secure note with a text field `keykeeper.template-definition` (an id, whether new items are logins or notes, and a version). The note's name is the template's name, and its custom fields are the template's fields, with any values as defaults. So people create, edit, share, and delete templates with the ordinary item editor, and everything stays end-to-end encrypted and syncs like any item. Other clients show a template as a plain note.
- **Where custom templates live:** personal templates in the personal vault. Family and business templates in an organization collection, which reaches everyone who can see that collection. An MSP pushes a template to many client orgs at once from the MSP console, which writes the same template item, same id, into each client's templates collection.
- **Hidden defaults are secrets:** a hidden field's default is a secret like any other. Someone who can't see the template note's passwords gets the field, empty, in the web vault and the extension as in the apps (`customTemplate`, `core/src/templates.rs`), so a template can't copy a secret into a new item of their own. Nobody can name a custom field `keykeeper.…` in the editor, as a new field or a rename: such a field would turn into Keykeeper's own data, a template definition for instance, and leave the editor.
- **Creating items:** "New item from a template" lists each organization's templates, then the person's own, then built-in. An item from an organization's template still goes where the person is adding it (the open folder, organization, or collection), never into that organization by default, so a personal item can't land in a shared collection by surprise.
- **Built:** ten built-in templates (API key, server, database, Wi-Fi network, router, software license, domain, certificate, email account, membership); upstream already has passport, driver's license, and bank account types. Custom templates for logins or notes, made from "Create template" in the same picker or with "Save as template" from a login or note (the web vault's item menu and the extension's; it opens a new template with the item's name and its custom fields' labels and kinds, never their values, unsaved), with defaults, a hint in the editor, a description in the item view, and a "Template" badge in lists. "From template" in the web vault's New menu and the browser extension's (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/templates.ts`, `template-chooser.component.ts`). New items keep hidden fields editable (upstream locked them when there was no original item). Tier 1 covers built-in and custom templates in the web vault and the extension.
- **MSP push to many orgs:** built; see "MSP templates".
- **Not built yet:** giving every member of an organization its own templates collection automatically (an MSP's Templates collection already reaches every member of each client, read-only: "MSP templates"); typed fields beyond text, hidden, and yes/no (date, URL, email, phone, number, one-time code) and a date field that sets the expiration; a Templates view, so someone's own templates can leave "All items" too (an MSP's copies already stay in their collection); "From template" in a desktop vault window, if we build one; and an organization template test (needs org creation in the test rig).

## Expiration dates

Any item can expire: API keys, certificates, licenses, domains, passports, cards.

- **Set it anywhere:** an "Expires" date on every item, plus template date fields marked "is the expiration date" (an API key's expiry, a passport's). Cards use their existing expiry month and year automatically.
- **Stored with the item:** a text field `keykeeper.expires` (ISO date), encrypted and synced, no server changes. A text field, not hidden: a date isn't secret, and list views only carry text fields' values, so lists can show badges without decrypting every item.
- **Built:** the "Expires" date in the item editor (web, extension, desktop share it), an Expiration section in the item view, badges in the web vault list and the extension's item list for items expired or expiring within 30 days, and card expiry in the item view. All of PassBundle's own fields stay hidden in views and editors and survive edits (`libs/vault/src/keykeeper/`). An "Expiring items" report on the web vault's Reports page lists everything expired or expiring within 30 days, cards included, expired first, then soonest. The extension checks every six hours while unlocked and shows a system notification at 30, 7, and 1 day before and once expired, never twice for the same step; a new date starts over (`apps/browser/src/keykeeper/expiration-reminders.ts`). One notification says how many items ("2 items expire soon"), never which: the system shows it on the lock screen and keeps it in its notification history, outside the vault. What it already showed is kept per account and cleared at logout. Tier 1 covers each.
- **Built (November 2026):** card expiry badges in lists, which turned out to carry the card already (the web vault and the extension decrypt whole items for their lists); the "Expiring soon" filter in the web vault's menu (`type=expiring`, `libs/vault/src/models/filter-function.ts`), the extension's chips, and our apps' menu; the count beside Health counting expiring items (`health-count.service.ts`), and an organization's Expiring items report in its admin console (the organization's items as its other reports load them, `pages/organizations/keykeeper-expiring-items-report.component.ts`); "Remind me" in every editor (`libs/vault/src/keykeeper/expiration-field.component.ts`, and our apps' editor), the item view saying the days when they aren't the default, our own export's check bringing `keykeeper.remind` back only well formed (rewritten as the editor stores it), the extension's reminders following each item's days (`expiration-reminders.ts`), and reminders in our apps and the agent's tray (`core/src/expiration.rs`, `agent/src/reminders.rs`).
- **Not built yet:** a system notification from the agent (Windows needs an app identity from the installer, and macOS a notification permission for the signed app), and reminders on phones while the app is closed (they need the operating system's scheduled notifications or our own push).
- **See it (decided November 2026):** "Expires in 12 days" and "Expired" badges in every list, cards by their expiry month included: the web vault's, the extension's, and our apps'. An "Expiring soon" filter shows everything expired or expiring within 30 days: in the web vault's menu under Favorites (`type=expiring` in the address), as a chip in the extension's list, and in our apps' menu, each shown while something is expiring (or while it's picked), with its count. Health counts them too: the count beside Health includes items expired or expiring within 30 days, each item once, whatever else it has to fix; our apps' Health lists them ("Expires in 5 days"); and an organization's Reports in the admin console has Expiring items for the organization's items, as a person's Reports has for theirs.
- **Reminder days, per item (decided November 2026):** every editor has "Remind me" under "Expires": "30, 7, and 1 day before" (the default), "90, 30, 7, and 1 day before", "7 and 1 day before", or "Don't remind me". It's stored only when it isn't the default, as a text field `keykeeper.remind` beside the date: whole days before the date, comma-separated ("90,30,7,1"), each from 1 to 365, or `none`. Every app reads any list of that form (in any order, repeats once), so a list the editor doesn't offer still works, and anything else counts as the default. Every list but `none` also reminds once the item has expired. Like the date, it isn't secret, stays hidden as a custom field, survives edits, and comes in from our own export only when it's well formed.
- **Reminders, on the device:** the extension and our apps check while unlocked and remind at each of the item's days (30, 7, and 1 by default) and once it has expired, each step once for a date; a new date or new days go on from where the item now stands. A reminder says how many items, never which. The extension shows a system notification. Our apps (`core/src/expiration.rs`, the same rules) check after unlocking and every six hours while unlocked, and say it at the top of the vault with Show, which opens Expiring soon; what was shown is kept per account and forgotten at sign-out. On a computer, the app also tells the agent how many items, and the agent's tray menu says it ("2 items expire soon", which opens the app) until the app finds nothing due; the agent gets two numbers and makes its own words, so nothing else on the computer can put text in its menu. Nothing leaves the device.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the dates, statuses, and labels, `keykeeper.remind` read in any order and anything else counting as the default, stored only when it isn't, the reminder step for each item's days, going on from where an item stands when its days change, and never with `none` (`expiration.spec.ts`, `expiration-reminders.spec.ts`); the Expiring soon filter (the menu's entry, its count in the place showing, picking it like a category; the extension's chip, one chip at a time, and its filter, cards by their month); the count beside Health with expiring items, each once; and our own export's check. End to end (`e2e/tests/expiration.spec.ts`, `expiration-extension.spec.ts`): Expiring soon in the web vault's menu lists the expired note, the note due in 5 days, and the card expiring this month, with the card's badge in the list and Health's count of 3, and nothing later or undated; Remind me set, shown, kept, set to Don't remind me, and back to the default; an organization's Expiring items report; the extension's Expiring soon chip and a card's badge in its list; and an item whose days are 7 and 1, and one that says not to remind, reminding only when they should. Our apps' own tests are in passbundle-apps (`core/tests/expiration.rs`, `core/tests/agent.rs`, `app/test/expiration_test.dart`).
- **Reminders by email (open):** the server can't read items, so emailed reminders would mean storing an unencrypted reminder date with an opaque item id ("an item in your vault expires on Oct 30", no name). Opt-in per user or org, or skip it.
- **MSP view (built):** the console lists everything expired or expiring within 30 days across all clients, soonest first, with the client and a link that opens the item in the client's vault. It's computed in the MSP staff's browser from items they can decrypt, alongside the per-client counts, so the server never sees it.

## Item history

Every item keeps its past versions, as in 1Password: each edit keeps what the item was before, and anyone who can open the item can see its earlier versions and restore one. Upstream only keeps a login's last five passwords.

- **Kept by the server, readable only by apps:** when an edit replaces an existing item's contents (upstream's item update from the vault or the admin console, and the share endpoints, one item or several), the server first copies the item as it is stored: its encrypted fields exactly as they are (type, name, notes, custom fields, the type's data, password history, re-prompt, and its item key), when that version was saved and who saved it, and when and by whom it was replaced. Who saved an item is recorded from the moment it's made, so the first version has an author too. The version, the edit, and who saved it are written in one transaction. The server can't read any of it and never gets a key.
- **Not kept:** new items, imports, the same contents encrypted with a rotated key, and changes outside the encrypted fields (folder, favorite, collections, archive, trash). The web vault saves a favorite on its own (upstream saves the whole item again).
- **Every save, not every change:** apps encrypt the whole item again on each save, with fresh IVs, so the server can't tell a new password from a save that changed nothing. It keeps a version for every edit unless the encrypted fields are byte for byte what's stored. The web vault decrypts the versions and lists one of each run that looks the same: the oldest, so "Saved by" names who wrote those contents.
- **What an item keeps:** one version a day (UTC), the item as it was before that day's first edit, today's included; and the newest 20 others, every one; at most 100 versions. Each kind has a budget of its own: the days share 4 MB, and the newest 20 another 4 MB. A burst of saves, accidental or scripted, however big, can push out only the newest, never the item as it was before them that day or any day before: those make room only for later days, one a day. (Until the security pass of October 2026, one 4 MB budget counted newest first, so someone who could edit an item, but not delete it or see its history, could wipe its history with a few big saves.) Merging a person's quick edits into one version instead would drop a real intermediate state (a password changed twice in a minute), and a plain count would let 50 saves flush everything older.
- **Each organization's history is capped** (`ORG_ITEM_HISTORY_LIMIT`, 1 GB by default, encrypted): past it, an edit keeps a version only if it's 64 KB or less. Real items are a few KB, so filling the cap with big saves can't stop a normal item's history, and the server's log says when a big version wasn't kept. Personal items have no such cap.
- **Size limits:** an item is at most 512 KB encrypted, not counting attachments, which are stored apart (a login is a few KB, and a note at most 10,000 characters); bigger saves are refused. Password history keeps the newest 100 entries (upstream's apps keep 5). So an item's history response is at most 100 versions and about 8 MB.
- **Who sees it:** who can open the item and has view item history in one of its shared folders (see "Folders and permissions"; every preset has it). The versions' contents need see passwords in that same folder. `GET /api/keykeeper/ciphers/<id>/history` makes the same check as reading the item, plus that one: its owner, members with one of its collections (directly or through a group), and owners, admins, and members with access to all items. Anyone else gets 404, the same as for a missing item, including members who are invited, accepted but not confirmed, or revoked, and members taken out of the item's collection. Someone given access later sees the history from before, as they see the item. The web vault says so above the list: everyone who can open the item can see its earlier versions, including old passwords.
- **Members who can view an item but not its passwords** get when and by whom only: no version's encrypted fields. The web vault lists the versions without View or Restore.
- **Who saved each version** shows by name and email to that person, and to those who can list the organization's members, by the same rule as `GET /organizations/<id>/users`: managers and above with access to all items (owners and admins have it), and custom roles that manage members or account recovery. Others see "Saved by another member". A deleted account's versions stay, unnamed.
- **Clearing it:** people with view item history and delete in the same one of its shared folders (or access to all items) can clear its history, or delete one version from its view (`DELETE /api/keykeeper/ciphers/<id>/history`, `.../history/<version>`). When the server refuses (a 400 with its reason, such as who may clear an item's history), the web vault and the extension show that reason in plain words, not "An error has occurred." (the web vault's version dialog stays open). Both go in the organization's event log for its items (types 1190 and 1191, shown as "Cleared the earlier versions of item …" and "Deleted an earlier version of item …") and in the server's log. The item and who saved it stay.
- **Restoring:** the web vault and the extension decrypt the chosen version and save it as a normal edit of the item, sent with the revision it replaces (`lastKnownRevisionDate`), so it can't overwrite an edit made since. The server's rules for edits apply (a stale copy is refused, read-only members can't), the version it replaces goes into history like any edit, and upstream's password history keeps the replaced password. Attachments, folder, favorite, and collections aren't part of versions and stay as they are, and so do PassBundle's own fields (`keykeeper.*`: fill rules, the expiration date, template data): restoring someone's words never brings back a fill rule or a template marker that was since taken away. An app decrypts a version only when the server sent it as this item's (its id) in this item's organization or vault: ids aren't encrypted, so a hacked server could otherwise send another organization's item as a version, and restoring it would copy it across organizations (security pass, October 2026). A passkey the item still has (by credential id) stays as it is now, with its raised sign-in counter, and a version without one of the item's passkeys says so first ("restoring removes it, and you can't sign in with it after", with Restore anyway), as the apps do. Members who can edit an item but not see its passwords can't restore, which would put back secrets they can't see. There's no server-side restore: writing old ciphertext back blindly could pair the item with an item key or attachment keys it no longer has.
- **Item keys:** a version keeps the item key it was encrypted with, wrapped as it was by the account's or the organization's key, so it still opens after the item gets a new item key.
- **When history goes:** with the item when it's deleted for good (from the trash, emptying the trash, purging a vault, deleting an organization or an account): deleting an item deletes its history, and the database's cascade does too (SQLite connections turn foreign keys on). Moving a personal item into an organization deletes its history: those versions are encrypted with the person's own key, which nobody else there can open, and the organization shouldn't get them. Changing an account's key deletes its personal items' history once the new key is saved, since nothing could open it anymore, as with synced settings. Organization items keep theirs: they're under the organization's key.
- **Built (October 23, 2026):** the server (`keykeeper_cipher_versions`, `keykeeper_cipher_saves`, `/api/keykeeper/ciphers/<id>/history`) and the web vault: "Version history" in the item view, newest first, with who saved each; View shows a version next to the item now, changed fields marked and hidden values masked, to restore or delete it; Clear history (`libs/vault/src/keykeeper/item-versions*.ts`). **Our desktop and phone apps (October 23, 2026)** list the versions, show one beside the item now, restore it the same way, as an edit, and delete one or clear them where the server allows (docs/core.md, "Version history"). Restoring there keeps each passkey the item still has, with its raised counter, and says first which passkey a version without it would take away, and takes PassBundle's own fields from the item as it is now, as the web vault does (until November 2026 the apps brought back the version's). **The browser extension (October 26, 2026):** the item page's history card has Version history, for people with view item history in one of the item's shared folders (and always for their own items). It opens a page of its own: the item's tile and name, who can see its earlier versions, and the versions newest first as a card of rows, each with when it was saved, who saved it, and how many fields changed. A version opens beside the item now, a row per field with what changed marked in topaz, hidden values masked until Show hidden values; then Restore this version, and Delete this version. Clear history is at the bottom of the list. Restore, Delete, and Clear show only where the server allows them: Delete and Clear need view item history and delete in the same shared folder, as the web vault now checks too. Someone who can't see the item's passwords gets when and by whom only, and the extension never decrypts a version for them, whatever the server sends. An item that asks for the master password asks again before its history opens, as before the item itself opens. Loading, restoring, and deleting are the web vault's code (`libs/vault/src/keykeeper/item-history.service.ts`); the page is `apps/browser/src/vault/popup/components/vault/keykeeper-item-history/`.
- **Tests:** server unit tests (what a version keeps, what each kind of save does, sizes, what an item keeps); API tests on SQLite and PostgreSQL (versions for edits through every endpoint and none for the rest, authors, bursts, item keys, size limits, who can read and clear with a permission matrix, hidden passwords, membership states, collections and groups, custom roles and MSP technicians, names, moving into an organization, deletion checked in the database, key rotation); jest specs for comparing versions, the list, restoring (passkeys kept, the revision sent, the warning), clearing, favorites, who may clear (both capabilities in one folder), and the extension's page (the master password first, hidden passwords, a refused restore, delete, and clear, each with the server's reason), and the web vault's (a refused clear, restore, and delete); a browser test that changes a login's password twice, compares the first version with the item, restores it, finds three versions, and clears them; and the same in the extension's popup (`extension-history.spec.ts`), plus a member there who sees when and by whom only, without Clear, and one who can clear where they have view item history and delete (`extension-folder-permissions.spec.ts`).

## Importing from other password managers

Upstream gives us about 70 importers. They include 1Password, LastPass (with direct import), Dashlane, Keeper, Proton Pass, Enpass, KeePass, and the browsers. Our job is making them painless.

- **Right after signing up (built):** before a new account's vault opens, the web vault offers to bring passwords over from where they are now, as big tiles: the browser and Apple Passwords (the guided browser import; on a computer that isn't a Mac, Apple Passwords gives the steps on the Mac and takes the file here), 1Password, Bitwarden, LastPass, and anything else upstream reads. Each password manager gets the steps for its export, and the importer already set to it (1Password's recommends 1PUX, with the steps for a CSV a link away, read by our own "1Password 8 (csv)" importer); after importing, a reminder to delete the export. "Skip for now" is always there. It's offered once, in the tab that signed up, and not on a phone's browser, once the vault has items, or where an organization's policy keeps items out of Personal (an invited member's, say), where the import would put them (`apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/import/setup-import.component.ts` and its route guard). Tests: unit tests for when it's offered; tier 1 checks the offer and Skip at sign-up, and imports a Bitwarden export through it.
- **Apple Passwords:** now, from a Mac, the Passwords app's CSV export, through the guided import (the Safari guide) and the first-run import step; an iPhone-only person needs a Mac to export today. Later, with iOS and macOS 26, Apple's credential exchange (the FIDO Alliance's Credential Exchange format): Passwords hands logins and passkeys straight to another app on the device, with no file. Our iPhone app, an AutoFill provider already, takes it in, encrypts on the device, and saves to the vault; the Mac app the same way. It needs the iPhone app installed, so it waits on the Apple Developer account. Android's Credential Manager is adding the same exchange; the Android app takes it when it's there.
- **Detect and guide:** the desktop agent detects installed password managers and walks the user through that product's export with exact steps. Then it imports and securely deletes the export file.
- **Direct import** where the source allows it, as upstream already does for LastPass.
- **Business:** the admin bulk-imports shared vaults into collections, mapping source folders and shared folders. Each member's private items go through the same guided import during their onboarding.
- **Bitwarden (built and tested):** its JSON export imports with every item, folder, TOTP secret, custom field, note, and password history intact. A password-protected export moves to another account with no plaintext in the file. Both are tier 1 tests. Our own exports use the same format, so the importer calls it "PassBundle or Bitwarden (json)" (and "(csv)").
- **Our own fields never come in from a file (security review, November 2026):** PassBundle keeps its data on items in hidden custom fields named `keykeeper.*` (fill rules, template markers, expiration dates), and the apps trust them: a fill rule fills, and a template definition that says it's an MSP's copy hides the item from vault lists while it still fills. A crafted export could carry such fields, so every import goes through one check before it's saved (`libs/importer/src/services/keykeeper/reserved-fields.ts`). From another password manager or a browser, a field with one of our names (any case, any spaces around) keeps its value under a visible name, "Imported keykeeper.fill". From our own .json export (plain or password-protected), our fields come back only when they're well formed, rewritten from what was read: a fill rule as a hidden field with only a rule's parts, one expiration date, one template marker, and one template definition, never as an MSP's copy (its `from` is dropped). Anything else under our names comes in visibly too. Unit tests import a planted field through each affected importer (1Password 8 CSV and 1PUX, KeePass 2 XML, LastPass, Proton Pass, and Bitwarden's format), and tier 1 imports planted files through the import page and reads the vault back.
- **Files upstream's SDK reads (KeePass's .kdbx), checked after it saves them (November 2026):** the SDK reads the file, encrypts the items, and sends them to the server itself, and gives back only how many of each kind it imported, so the check above can't run before they're saved. It runs right after, inside the same call, which every SDK importer goes through (`importWithSdk` in `import.service.ts`; `libs/importer/src/services/keykeeper/sdk-import-check.ts`), so one added later is checked too. Before the SDK runs, the destination's items are read from the server: Personal or a shared folder by a full sync; an organization from its admin console as that page reads them (all its items for someone who can edit every item, otherwise the ones they're given), since such an import can leave items in no shared folder, which a sync doesn't show. If they can't be read, nothing is imported. After, they're read again; the items there now that weren't before are the import's, and each with a field named like ours gets the same treatment as from any other password manager ("Imported keykeeper.fill"), saved as its importer (through the admin endpoint from the admin console). An item that can't be saved that way goes to the trash, so nothing with a planted rule is left to fill; the import then says which items, and names them if even the trash fails. Seeing fewer new items than the SDK counted means some couldn't be checked, and the import says so; seeing more after a first read that found nothing means that read can't be trusted (every item there would look new), so nothing is changed and the import says so. Limits: for the few seconds between the SDK saving the items and the check saving them again, another of the person's devices can sync them as they came (a planted rule there fills only that crafted item's own values, on its own site), and closing the page in those seconds leaves them as they came; and an item someone else adds to the same place during the import is checked as the import's, so its own rule would show as a visible "Imported" field. Unit tests cover what's checked and saved, the destinations, the trash, and the failures (`sdk-import-check.spec.ts`), and that an SDK importer added later goes through the check (`import.service.spec.ts`); tier 1 imports a planted .kdbx file (written by `e2e/fixtures/imports/kdbx.mjs`, KDBX 4 with AES-KDF and a protected rule) through the import page and reads the vault back.
- **Importers that say what they did (November 2026):** Dashlane's CSV files are told apart by their columns, not their order or the first row, so a payments file with a title column is cards, not personal info, and a file that's none of Dashlane's is refused rather than imported as empty items; a payment that isn't a card or bank account is a secure note. A CSV with a quote that's never closed (which swallows every row after it into one value) is refused. KeePass's Recycle Bin is left out with a count the import summary shows ("Left out 1 item in KeePass's Recycle Bin."), and a KeePass one-time code comes over only with 4 to 10 digits and a period of 1 to 300 seconds, otherwise as the fields it was. LastPass phone numbers are read without a pattern that took seconds on a long value, and card brands are known only by their names (a "constructor" brand was an object's own property).
- **Tests (built):** upstream importer specs, plus one fake vault written out the way each app exports it: Chrome, Edge, Brave, Firefox, Apple Passwords, 1Password (1PUX and CSV), Bitwarden, LastPass, Dashlane, KeePass 2, and Proton Pass (`e2e/fixtures/imports/`, made by a seeded generator and committed, so they can be tried by hand). Tier 1 imports each file into a new account through the screen people use, reads the vault back with its own export, and compares it item by item with what should be there, including what each importer leaves out (listed in that folder's README). Bugs and gaps these found are fixed in our importers with unit tests: KeePass's TOTP fields and extra websites, 1Password 8's CSV, LastPass card brands, an identity for each person in Dashlane's personal info, and Proton Pass addresses, among others.

## Generator settings

The setting people reach for is the generator's: how long, which kinds of characters, words or characters. Upstream keeps it on each device, forgets it at logout, and its apps disagree on the default. Ours is set once and is the setting everywhere.

- **One default, everywhere:** 20 characters with upper and lower case, digits, and symbols, leaving out look-alikes (0 O, 1 l I); passphrases of 6 words. The web vault and the extension used upstream's 14 with no symbols; they now use the apps'.
