- **Follows the account:** the generator's settings are saved to the server in a small document encrypted with the account's user key, and every app reads and writes the same one: the web vault and the extension (`libs/tools/generator/core/src/keykeeper/`), and the apps' core (`core/src/synced_settings.rs`). A change in any of them is the setting in the others at their next sync; saving tells the account's other devices to sync, which reads the settings again without downloading the vault. Signing out no longer resets them.
- **What's stored:** `{"type": "passbundle.settings", "rev": …, "generator": {…}}`, the generator's part named as the apps' core names its options (`kind`, `length`, `uppercase`, `lowercase`, `digits`, `symbols`, `avoidAmbiguous`, `minDigits`, `minSymbols`, `words`, `separator`, `capitalize`, `includeNumber`). Each app takes what it understands, held to its own generator's limits, and keeps the rest of the document, including generator settings other apps add, when it saves. Its `health` part holds the breach check's switch (`checkAfterUnlock`, see "Exposed passwords"). The web vault syncs each part on its own (`KeykeeperSyncedPart` in `settings-sync.ts`): a change made here to one part that the server hasn't got goes back, and every other part is taken from the server's copy, so changing the switch doesn't send this browser's generator settings over another device's newer ones. The extension and our apps keep the `health` part as it is when they save.
- **The server can't read it** (`GET`/`PUT /api/keykeeper/settings`): it checks only that it's a type 2 string, at most 16 KB, per account, never another's. Changing the account's key forgets it, since no app could read it anymore; the next app to save writes it anew. An organization's password generator policy still applies on top, as before.
- **Conflicts:** last save wins. A change on this device that isn't saved yet (offline, or made just before closing) wins over the server's copy at the next sync; each app keeps what the server had, as far as it knows, across sign-outs to tell the two apart. A document that isn't PassBundle's settings (something else encrypted with the account's key) is replaced with this device's.
- **A server handing back an old copy** (security review, October 2026): each save numbers the document (`rev`: after every number the app has seen, and the time in milliseconds), and an app refuses a copy numbered lower than one it has seen, saving its own again. A server could otherwise bring back settings someone chose before, say a short one for a picky site, without their noticing. What it can't do is make settings up: they're encrypted and authenticated with the account's key.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the mapping, limits, numbering, and each sync case (the server has none, has some, has an older copy, has something else; a change here first, during a sync, before closing; after signing out and in; other apps' settings kept); the core's against a real server, from one device to another and back; API tests (per account, only encrypted, no vault download, forgotten when the key changes); and an end-to-end test that a length chosen in the web vault is the extension's.

## Screenshots

PassBundle's windows stay out of screenshots, screen recordings, and screen sharing on macOS and Windows, as the phone apps already keep the vault out of them (Android's `FLAG_SECURE`; docs/phones.md, "Locking"). A screen shared in a meeting, or a recording tool, doesn't pick up a vault.

- **How:** on macOS `NSWindow.sharingType = .none`; on Windows `SetWindowDisplayAffinity(WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE)` on Windows 10 2004 and later, and before that `WDA_MONITOR`, which shows the window black in captures. Each runner sets it as the window is made, before anything is drawn (`MainFlutterWindow.swift`, `flutter_window.cpp` in passbundle-apps).
- **"Allow screenshots of PassBundle"** in Settings, off by default and kept on the device (`screenshots.json` in the app's folder), for someone who wants PassBundle in a screenshot, a recording, or a screen they share. Remote support tools capture the screen too, so a technician helping someone remotely sees PassBundle's window only once that person allows screenshots.
- **Linux** has no way for a window to keep itself out (on X11 any program can read the screen; on Wayland it's the compositor's to decide), so the switch doesn't show there.
- **What it isn't:** a guarantee. It keeps the window out of captures that go through the OS's own capture, as screenshots, recording, and screen sharing apps do; a program that reads the screen some other way, or a camera, still can.
- **Tests:** the window tests check the window is kept out by default and taken once allowed (Windows' display affinity, the Mac window server's sharing state), and the app's tests check the switch. CI's screenshots come from builds with the core's test hooks, which allow capture with `PB_ALLOW_SCREENSHOTS=1`; a release's core can't read it, and the release jobs check the name isn't in it.

## Several accounts in the apps

People with a work account and a personal one want both in one app, the way 1Password shows every account together. They can't merge them: the Business preset's single-organization policy keeps a work account to one company, and account recovery lets the company's admins reset its master password, so personal passwords belong in an account of their own. The browser extension (upstream's) already switches between up to five accounts. The apps now do too.

**Status (November 7, 2026):** part 1 merged (passbundle-apps#64): several accounts signed in side by side, switching, adding, and signing out of one. Part 2 built in passbundle-apps' `all-accounts` branch: an "All accounts" place listing every unlocked account's items, each labeled with its account's name and color, searched together; Android's autofill and passkeys offering items from every unlocked account, labeled; and new items asking which account.

- **Up to five accounts** on a device, as the extension allows. The account menu, from the email at the foot of the menu on a computer (and on a phone, Settings' "Accounts" and the vault header's account button), lists them (the one shown marked, a locked one saying so), switches to another, adds one, and signs out of the one shown. A locked account's screen has "Switch account" too.
- **Signing out says which:** it applies to the account shown, and every way to it names that account ("Sign out of ana@example.test"): the account menu, a phone's Settings, and a computer's Settings, whose Account group has it too (Settings closes first). The question names the account's vault, and with others signed in says they stay. The lock screen's Sign out asks the same.
- **Adding one** is signing in, as before: the same server choice (PassBundle's, an organization's managed server, or "Self-hosted?"), two-step login, and making an account. Signing in to an account that's here already (the same server and email) signs it in again in its own place, as "Sign in again" does. At five, adding waits for signing out of one.
- **Each account is its own:** its vault, keys, session, settings (when to lock, confirming before approving a sign-in, the generator's), lock state and timer, biometric unlock, its organizations' policies, and sign-in requests. Switching shows another account as it is, unlocked or locked; the one shown before stays as it was. Lock locks the one shown. Signing out of one leaves the others.
- **What's the device's:** how the app looks (website icons), whether screenshots are allowed, updates, browsers trusted for autofill, and the offers to turn on biometric unlock and autofill ("Not now" holds for every account).

### All accounts

- **Names and colors.** With more than one account signed in, each account's Settings has "This account": a name (Work, Personal; at most 40 characters, empty for its email) and a color from the flat tile tones the vault draws its kinds in (sapphire, emerald, amethyst, topaz, ruby, turquoise, garnet, aquamarine, onyx, gold). A new account gets the first color no other account here has. They're kept in `accounts.json` on this device, never sent anywhere: a label for the person, not a setting of the account's. The account menu, the sidebar's foot, and every label show them.
- **"All accounts"**, the first place in the menu on a computer (and a card above the favorites on a phone) with more than one account signed in: every unlocked account's items in one list, by name, each with a pill in its account's color and name. Its search searches every unlocked account. A locked account's items aren't there, and go from the list as it locks. "All items" and the rest of the menu stay the account shown's.
- **Picking an item from another account opens it in its own account:** the screens switch to that account's vault, still in "All accounts", with the item open. Everything done with it (revealing, copying, editing, history, attachments) is that account's, as with any item, so no call ever acts on one account's item from another's vault.
- **A new item asks which account** when more than one is unlocked (the one shown first): New, and a sign-in Android's autofill offers to save. Choosing another account opens its vault and the New picker (or the editor, filled in) there. A new password for a login that's in another account opens that account's editor for it.
- **Android's autofill** offers the matching logins of every unlocked account, the one shown first, each with its account's name or email beside its username ("ana@example.test · Work"), and fills a login only from its own account (`Core.value(account, …)`, checked again against the list when picked). With no account unlocked, "Unlock PassBundle" unlocks the one shown, as before.
- **Android's passkeys** list every unlocked account's passkeys for the site, each labeled the same way, and sign with the passkey's own account. Saving a new passkey asks which account first, when more than one is unlocked; then "Save a passkey in PassBundle?" names the site's ID, then the account.
- **Icons** in "All accounts" come from each item's own account's server, kept by account and website: a personal server is never asked about a work login's site, as before.
- **What crosses, then:** only what the person sees and picks. The list holds what each unlocked account's own list shows (names, usernames, websites); picking one goes to its account; nothing of one account is written into another, and no key or secret leaves its account.

### Storage

In the app's folder (`docs/core.md`, "What's kept where"), as before 0700 with each file 0600, written whole:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The accounts, in the order they were added; the one shown last; this install's device identifier; the server and email last signed in to or out of (for the sign-in screen) | `accounts.json`. Nothing secret. |
| Each account's server, email, KDF settings, kept sync, and choices | `accounts/<id>/` (`settings.json`, `sync.json`, `preferences.json`, `generator.json`, `generator-synced.json`, `approvals.json`), one folder each |
| What's the device's | the app's folder itself, as before (`appearance.json`, `screenshots.json`, the updater's) |
| Each account's refresh token | the OS's secret store, one entry per account, named by its server and email as before (Android: `tokens/<hash of both>.sealed`) |

- **`<id>`** is made on the device: 32 random hex digits that say nothing about the account. The core builds a path from an id only when it's one of these, and only inside `accounts/`.
- **One device identifier per install,** the same for every account, as upstream's apps have it. An account signed out and in again is the same device to its server (no new-device email, and a remembered two-step login stays remembered), and the server keeps devices by device and account (Vaultwarden's two-part key), so two accounts on one server don't collide. The cost: two accounts on the same server look like one device to that server's operator. Two servers each see only their own account. Decided against: an identifier per account, which would make every sign-out and sign-in a new device.
- **Recovering from a stop part way:** at start, a folder with a kept vault that `accounts.json` doesn't list (a sign-in that finished as the app stopped) is kept; a folder without one (an account signed out of, or a sign-in given up) is deleted. `accounts.json` holds the order and the account shown; the folders are what count.
- **Carrying over an install from before:** its one account's files in the app's folder move into an account folder of their own (`settings.json` last), and it's the account shown. One that was signed out leaves only its server and email for the sign-in screen. On a computer, biometric unlock carries over as it is: the desktop agent keeps it by the account's id on its server. On a phone, the one key kept before goes (the Keystore key and sealed file, or the Keychain item), and the app offers biometric unlock again after the next unlock with the master password. There are no users yet, so that's the simple way.

### What's in memory, when

- **Every account signed in** has its own `App` in the core (`core/src/accounts.rs`): its server, email, KDF settings, choices, and, from its kept sync, its organizations' policies. No key while it's locked.
- **An unlocked account** holds its own keys (user key, private key, organization keys), its decrypted items, its session (access and refresh token), its websites' icons, an item's history, its listed sign-in requests, and its last confirmation, as one account did before. With two accounts unlocked, two sets of keys are in memory; locking one wipes its own (`zeroize`) and leaves the other's.
- **Nothing decrypted is written,** as before.
- **The screens** show one account at a time, and every call about an account names it (below).

### Locking

- **Each account locks by its own choices and its own organizations' policy:** its wait, "Lock when the computer sleeps" (or the phone locks), "Immediately", and a policy's longest wait, lock on system lock, or sign out. The app asks the core every two seconds while any account is unlocked, and the core checks each.
- **Using the app** starts the shown account's wait again; the others keep theirs, as the extension counts activity for its active account. On a computer the OS's idle time counts as before, so working in other apps keeps every account's wait from running out.
- **Leaving the screen, the computer sleeping, a phone's screen going off,** and on Android autofill or a passkey handing back its answer: each account locks if its own choice or policy says so.
- **A policy's "sign out"** signs out of that account only, and takes it off the device.
- **Switching** never unlocks anything: an account that's locked shows its lock screen.

### Biometric keys, per account

- **On a computer,** the desktop agent keeps each account's key by the account's id on its server, as it does for the browser extension: each account turns it on for itself (one Windows Hello or Touch ID prompt each), unlocks with its own, and signing out of one removes only its own. The extension and the app still share one enrollment for the same account.
- **On Android,** each account has its own Keystore key (`passbundle-biometric-<id>`: AES-256-GCM, strong biometrics for every use, voided when a fingerprint or face is added, in StrongBox where there is one) and its own sealed user key (`biometric-<id>.sealed` in the app's files).
- **On iOS,** each account has its own Keychain item (`user-key-<id>`), readable only after the current Face ID or Touch ID, this device only.
- **The account is named in every call** between the core and Kotlin or Swift: sealing takes that account's user key, and the key a prompt opens goes back to that account's unlock (or its approval), never another's. A key that doesn't open that account's vault turns off that account's biometric unlock only.

### What can never cross between accounts

- **A key:** one account's keys open only its own vault. No call hands one account's key to another, and the core opens a vault only with its own account's master password or its own biometric key.
- **An item:** lists, search, revealing, copying, editing, saving, trashing, attachments, history, passkeys, and autofill are each account's own. Every call across the bridge carries the id of the account the screens showed when it was made (`Core.account` in `app/lib/core.dart`), so a slow call, a save from an editor, or an icon's fetch lands on that account even if the screens switched meanwhile. Switching closes whatever was open over the vault, as locking does. A call naming an account that isn't here sees an app with nothing in it, which keeps nothing.
- **A server:** each account talks only to its own server, with its own session. Websites' icons for an account's logins come from that account's server, so a personal server never learns the sites of the work account's logins.
- **A policy:** an organization's policies (locking, the password generator, "Remove Unlock with PIN") apply to the account in it only.
- **A sign-in request:** while a vault is shown, the app asks for every unlocked account's requests, and each is listed, confirmed (that account's biometric unlock or master password), and approved for its own account, with that account's key, wrapped to the request's public key as before. The prompt names the account it's for, and Settings' list names another account's. A listing asked for before a switch is dropped.
- **The same account twice:** a new account the server says is one here already (the same id on its server, under another email or server) is refused, with nothing kept of it: the two would share the desktop agent's key. Sign out of the first one, then add it.
- **An account's id, pinned:** once an account's vault is on the device, a sync that answers with another account's vault (another id) is refused, with nothing of it kept and the session dropped. A server can't turn one account here into another, so the desktop agent's key for one account can't be asked for, or replaced, through another, even by a hostile server. This holds with one account too: before, a server could have answered a session with another account's vault.

### Not in this

- **The browser extension filling from several accounts at once.** Upstream's design fills from the active account only; ours keeps that for now. Later.
- **Sign-in requests for a locked account:** they show once it's unlocked, as with one account. While no vault is shown (the account shown is locked), none are asked for.
- **Unlocking a locked account from Android's autofill list** while another is unlocked: the list offers the unlocked accounts' logins; the app unlocks the others.
- **iOS's AutoFill extension** (not in the app yet) will list every unlocked account's the same way.

### Tests

- **Core unit tests** (`core/src/accounts.rs`, on two vaults with keys of their own): two accounts unlock with their own master passwords only, show only their own items, and one's user key doesn't open the other; locking one leaves the other; each keeps its own choices in its own folder and locks on its own time (one minute and fifteen, made-up readings) and by its own sleep choice; signing out of one removes its folder and leaves the other unlocked and shown; a policy's "sign out" takes only its account off; carrying over an install from before (files moved, the device's left, the same choices and device identifier, done once) and one that was signed out; a sign-in that finished as the app stopped kept, one given up deleted; switching kept across a restart; at five, a new sign-in refused while one that's here still signs in again; the same server and email, as typed, routed to the account; a new account with an id already here dropped with nothing left; giving up adding one; a dropped sign-in's refresh token forgotten at start, and only its own; the device's own choices shared; a sync for another account than the one kept refused (`core/src/app.rs`).
- **The core against a real server** (`core/tests/server.rs`): a work and a personal account signed in side by side, each its own session and items, one install's device identifier, locking one while the other syncs, signing in to the first again keeping its place, signing out of one forgetting only its refresh token, and a restart opening the one left, locked.
- **Through a real agent** (`core/tests/agent.rs`): each account turns on Windows Hello or Touch ID for itself, unlocks with its own, and signing out of one turns off only its own.
- **Core unit tests for "All accounts"** (`core/src/accounts.rs`): names and colors are each account's own, checked (length, control characters, only the tones there are), kept across a restart, a new account given a free color, and a signed-out one's forgotten; autofill's logins for a site come from every unlocked account, the one shown first, each with its account; searching every account finds each item with its account, by name; a locked account offers and lists nothing.
- **The app** (`app/test/accounts_test.dart`): "All accounts" lists both accounts' items, each labeled, searches them together, and drops a locked one's as it locks; picking the other account's item opens it in that account; a new item asks which account and is made in the one chosen, and asks nothing with one unlocked; naming and coloring an account in its Settings, shown in the menu and leaving the other's; a phone's "All accounts" card. (`app/android/app/src/test`): autofill's second line with the account's name.
- **The app** (`app/test/sign_in_requests_test.dart`): another account's sign-in request names that account, and is confirmed and answered for it. (`app/test/accounts_test.dart`): the account menu switches between two accounts, each unlocked and locked on its own and showing only its own items; the lock screen's "Switch account"; adding goes to the sign-in screen and back to the account as it was; signing out of one leaves the other; at five, adding can't be chosen; a phone's Settings and the vault header's account button; an install from before carried over. Every other app test opens its sample as an install from before, so each goes through the carry-over too.
- **Android and iOS** (`app/integration_test/phone_test.dart`, `autofill_setup_test.dart`): biometric unlock sealed under the account's own id, removed with the account, and autofill and passkeys filling from the account they name.
- No key derivation or encryption changed, so no new test vectors.

## Staying unlocked across a reload

Upstream's web vault locks on every reload, whatever the vault timeout: it keeps the user key only in the page's memory. The owner found that wearing ("I press refresh and type my master password again"), and chose (2026-10-26) to keep a tab unlocked across a reload, within its timeout, with a key the server holds. A security review on 2026-10-27 tightened it (reopened tabs, Back, its own setting, policies, limits), and a second review tightened it again (the leaving beacon, "On browser refresh" under policies, other tabs).

**What upstream does** (checked against our build, 2026-10-26, before building this):

- The user key is `USER_KEY` in `CRYPTO_MEMORY` (`libs/common/src/key-management/state-definitions.ts`): on the web, the page's memory, plus the SDK's copy in WebAssembly memory. A reload, closing the tab, locking, and signing out all clear it.
- The only key upstream ever keeps is the "auto" key for a timeout of Never, in `SECURE_STORAGE`, which production web builds keep in memory too, and which offer Never only in development builds.
- The tab's sessionStorage (the web's "disk": per tab, kept across a reload, not shared with a new tab, gone when the tab closes, though the browser brings it back with a reopened tab or a restored session) holds the access and refresh tokens (with the Lock action) and the user key encrypted with the master key, so a reload shows the unlock screen. With Sign out, the tokens are in memory too, so a reload signs out.
- So the Business preset's Vault lock wasn't the cause: someone with no policy locked on every reload too. Our own end-to-end tests had relied on it since 2026-10-14.

**What ours does:**

- **At a real unlock** (signing in, the master password, any way but this one): the tab makes a random key R (`PureCrypto.make_aes256_cbc_hmac_key`, as upstream makes keys), wraps the user key with it using upstream's key wrap (`EncryptService.wrapSymmetricKey`, an EncString of type 2), and keeps only that wrapped key and a random tab id (16 bytes) in its own sessionStorage. It sends R and the tab id to the server over the signed-in session (`POST /api/keykeeper/reload-key`). The server answers with the tab's reload cookie (below).
- **Only a reload restores.** At startup, before anything reads the key, a page whose navigation type is "reload" (`performance.getEntriesByType("navigation")`) asks for R with its tab id (`POST /api/keykeeper/reload-key/release`, the browser adding the tab's cookie), unwraps the user key, and unlocks with it the way upstream unlocks with a key it already has (`UnlockService.unlockWithDecryptedUserKey`, which replaced `KeyService.setUserKey` in web-v2026.10.1): the key goes into memory, the Never timeout's copy is kept as upstream keeps it, and upstream's unlock listeners run, as for any unlock. A closed tab reopened, Back to the page, a new load in the same tab, a duplicate of it, or anything else stays locked, forgets its record, and asks the server to forget R at once; where the navigation's entry is missing, nothing restores. A page that comes back from the back-forward cache, with the key still in its memory, locks as it shows (as a lapsed timeout locks, upstream's `LockSource.VaultTimeout`) (in our tests Chrome loads the vault again instead of keeping it in that cache). A key unlocked from a reload key never makes a new R: only the next real unlock does. The page then syncs, as it does after any unlock.
- **As a page goes** (`pagehide`: closed, navigated away, or reloading), it sends a beacon (`POST /api/keykeeper/reload-key/leaving`) with only its tab id and its page id, a random 16 bytes new at each load; its cookie is the proof, so the beacon needs no access token, and it can only shorten an entry. From then on the server keeps the entry 15 seconds, for the reload that follows to ask; asking ends the leaving, and records the asking page's id. The server takes a leaving only from the page that kept the entry or asked for it last, so a beacon from the page before a reload, arriving late, can't end the reloaded page's entry; and a second leaving keeps the first one's time, so repeating it can't push the end back. What's guaranteed: once a closed tab's beacon reaches the server, nothing can restore that tab 15 seconds later. A beacon that never arrives (the browser crashed or dropped it) leaves the entry until 2 minutes after the tab's last check-in.
- **The person's own setting:** "Stay unlocked when I reload", on Settings, Security, Session timeout, under the timeout, on unless they turn it off, kept per account in this browser (`KEYKEEPER_STAY_UNLOCKED_ON_RELOAD`, localStorage). Its hint names shared computers: off, every reload asks. Turning it off forgets R at once, in every open tab of the account: the others hear of it from the browser's `storage` event, and forget theirs too. It's off, with a note saying why, while the person's own stored timeout is "On browser refresh", and a policy that writes its minutes over "On browser refresh" turns it off, with a note, until they turn it back on.
- **Only with a timeout in minutes and the Lock action.** "On browser refresh" and Sign out keep upstream's behavior, and so do Vault lock policies of "immediately", upstream's "on app restart", signing out at the timeout, or `webReload: false`: no R is made, and a reload asks. Changing to one of these forgets R at once, in this tab and the account's other open tabs. Nothing rewrites the person's timeout to make room for a reload key, and "On browser refresh" keeps meaning a lock at every reload: where a policy shows it as minutes, or writes minutes over it, "Stay unlocked when I reload" goes off (below).
- **While open**, the tab checks in every 30 seconds (`POST /api/keykeeper/reload-key/check-in`) with how long since the person last used it (upstream's own activity record) and their timeout now. Browsers run a hidden tab's timers about once a minute, inside the server's 2 minutes.
- **Forgetting:** locking (upstream's lock waits for it before it reloads the page) and signing out (the web vault forgets before it clears the session, from the account menu's Sign out or any other) ask the server to forget R (`POST /api/keykeeper/reload-key/forget`), with the access token refreshed first if it ran out, as a `keepalive` request that outlives the page. A forget that doesn't get through is tried once more a check-in later if the page is still open, and the page's leaving says so too.
- **The server keeps R in its process memory only**: never in the database, a file, or a log. Each entry belongs to one account, one device (the session its access token is for), and one tab id, and only a request with all three, and the tab's cookie, gets R back. Anything else gets the same "Not found". The entry ends:
  - when the tab hasn't been used for its timeout, as the tab reports it, capped by the strictest Vault lock policy of every organization the account has joined: a longest wait's minutes, or "when the computer locks"'s `webMinutes` (15 when it's missing). For reload keys only, this binds owners and MSP staff too, where the policy's other effects exempt owners as upstream does: refusing a reload key only costs a master password prompt, so nobody can be locked out, and our apps' lock policy already binds admins and MSP staff;
  - 2 minutes after the tab stops checking in: the browser closed or crashed, or the computer slept;
  - 15 seconds after the tab's page left, unless a reload asked;
  - when the tab forgets it (above);
  - when a policy now refuses reload keys, at the next check-in or reload;
  - when the account's security stamp changes (a new master password, key rotation, "Deauthorize sessions", an admin's deauthorization, a KDF or email change), or its devices are removed (deleting the account does);
  - when the server restarts, so the next reload asks.
- **The reload cookie.** When the server keeps R, it sets a cookie for the tab: a random 256-bit value, named `__Secure-pb-reload-<tab id>`, HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict, with no Expires or Max-Age (a session cookie), and a path of `/api/keykeeper/reload-key` under the server's own path, so it goes only to the reload endpoints. The server keeps only its SHA-256 with the entry. Getting R back, checking in, and leaving need the cookie; a missing or wrong one gets the same "Not found" as no entry, and changes nothing. Forgetting R expires the cookie with it; an entry that ended on its own expires its cookie the next time the tab asks, and keeping a new R expires the cookies of tabs nothing is kept for any more, so they don't pile up.
  - Why `__Secure-` and not `__Host-`: `__Host-` would also pin the cookie to the host, but only with a path of `/`, which would send it with every request. `__Secure-` makes the browser take it only with Secure from an https page, and the narrow path keeps it off everything else. Each tab has its own name, so tabs signed in apart in one browser don't replace each other's cookie.
  - Why a cookie: page scripts can't read an HttpOnly cookie, and Chrome and Edge keep cookies encrypted at rest with the operating system's own key (DPAPI, with app-bound encryption in Chrome, on Windows; the Keychain on macOS; the desktop keyring on Linux, where there is one). sessionStorage gets none of that: Chrome writes it to the profile as it is. So the two halves a reload needs live in different places with different protection.
  - Same origin: the web vault always calls the API at its own origin (`WebEnvironmentService` sets the base to `window.location.origin`, and the server serves both), so the cookie is first-party and goes with the reload calls, the keepalive forget, and the leaving beacon, without anything special. A web vault built to call an API on another site wouldn't get the cookie back (SameSite=Strict), and its reloads would ask for the master password, as upstream's do.
- **Under "when the computer locks"** the web vault makes the timeout a number of minutes, at most `webMinutes`, where the timeout is read, and doesn't write it into the person's stored choice, so leaving the organization leaves their choice as it was. That holds while Settings is open too: the page shows the timeout it reads whenever that changes, and a value it shows that way is never saved (fixed November 2026: upstream's timeout field passed such a value back as a change, so a policy arriving while the page was open saved its minutes over a stored "On browser refresh"). Settings offers the waits up to it and Custom. "On browser refresh" isn't offered: it never locks while the page stays open, so it would turn the idle lock off. A stored "On browser refresh" counts as `webMinutes`, with "Stay unlocked when I reload" off, so it still locks at every reload. Under a longest wait, upstream writes the policy's minutes over a stored "On browser refresh", and the web vault then turns the setting off for that account, with a note saying why (`docs/core.md`, "Locking by policy").
- **Limits:** 20 tabs per account (a new one replaces the stalest), and a tab id already in use by another account or device is refused. Each tab's calls are limited to 20 at once, then one every 3 seconds, and each account's, all its tabs together, to 60 at once, then two a second, so a technician with many tabs open checking in twice a minute never meets them. The leaving beacon has a limit of its own per address, 300 at once, then five a second, which counts only beacons that match no entry, so an office closing many tabs at once never meets it and never uses up the server's limit for requests without a session (signing in, among others). Entries are kept by tab id, so a beacon finds its entry without looking through the others. Expired entries are wiped every 30 seconds; every call checks its own entry's expiry first.
- **What's wiped:** R is held in a heap buffer of its own, decoded straight into one from the request and wiped when its entry ends or is replaced, so the map moves only a pointer when it grows. The request body that carried R is wiped once read. Not wiped by us: the base64 text of R in the response that hands it back, the TLS library's buffers, and the bytes a reallocation inside serde may have copied; they're freed, and stay in freed memory until reused.
- **Core dumps:** the server turns them off at startup (`RLIMIT_CORE` 0) and, on Linux, marks itself not dumpable (`PR_SET_DUMPABLE` 0), which also keeps other processes of the same user from reading its memory through ptrace or /proc. `ALLOW_CORE_DUMPS=true` keeps them, to debug a crash; it's read once the `ENV_FILE` is loaded, so it can be set there or in the process environment. Swap is the other way memory reaches a disk: hosts should run without swap, or with it encrypted.
- **Files:** server `src/api/core/keykeeper_reload_keys.rs` (with the hooks in `User::reset_security_stamp` and `Device::delete_all_by_user`, and `no_core_dumps` in `main.rs`); web vault `apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/reload-key/`, started from `InitService`, with the setting's component on the session timeout page and the forget in `AppComponent.logOut`; the setting's state, which upstream's timeout code turns off when it replaces "On browser refresh", in `libs/common/src/key-management/session-timeout/keykeeper-reload-setting.ts`; the reload key's state is `KEYKEEPER_RELOAD_KEY_DISK`, which must stay the tab's sessionStorage.

**Threat model.** What each kind of attacker can do with a reload key, against what upstream's web vault gives them. Today, without reload keys, the tab's storage holds the access and refresh tokens and the user key encrypted with the master key: whoever copies it can call the API as the person and download the encrypted vault, but needs the master password to open it.

- **A copy of the tab's sessionStorage alone** (Chrome writes it to the profile on disk for session restore, so a backup, a sync tool, or malware that reads files but not memory can take it): the tokens, the tab id, and the wrapped user key, but not the cookie. Asking for R without the cookie gets "Not found", so the copy opens nothing more than it does today. (Our own web vault, finding no cookie on a reload, also asks the server to forget R, which ends the original tab's reload key; another client needn't.)
- **The cookie alone:** useless. It's a random value that opens nothing without the tab's tokens, its id, and the wrapped user key, which stay in sessionStorage. With it alone, someone can only say the tab is leaving, which shortens the entry.
- **Both, while the entry lasts:** the user key. That needs the sessionStorage copy and the cookie decrypted, which on Windows and macOS means the operating system's key for the browser: malware running as the person that can also get the browser's cookie key (Chrome's app-bound encryption on Windows makes that harder still for software that isn't Chrome), or someone with the person's unlocked session. Such an attacker could usually read the browser's memory, and so the key of an unlocked vault, anyway. Once the entry ends (the timeout, 15 seconds after the tab's leaving reaches the server, or 2 minutes after its last check-in), both together open nothing.
- **Session cookies on disk:** Chrome keeps session cookies in memory, and writes them to the profile only with "Continue where you left off" on; a copy of a profile without that setting doesn't hold the cookie at all.
- **Firefox and Safari** keep cookies on disk without that encryption (Firefox in plain SQLite), so there the cookie only adds what HttpOnly gives: page scripts can't read it, and it's in a different file. A copy of the whole profile is then enough while the entry lasts.
- **A browser extension with the cookies permission** can read HttpOnly cookies, and one that can also read the page has the tab's storage: both halves. An extension that can read the page could already read an unlocked vault. Choosing extensions is the person's, or their IT's (DESIGN.md, "Browser extension management").
- **A breached server alone** has R and the cookie's hash, but not the wrapped key, which stays in the tab: nothing. It gains nothing new anyway: the web vault already trusts the server for its code, so a breached server could serve a web vault that sends it the key at the next unlock.
- **A breached server and a copy of the tab's sessionStorage** together have R and the wrapped key: the user key, cookie or not, for as long as the server kept that R. The same breached server could already have taken the key through its code.
- **A crash dump of the server** would hold every live R; dumps are off (above), and an R alone opens nothing without its tab's storage.
- **Script injected into the web vault's page** (cross-site scripting, or an extension that can read the page) can't read the cookie, but it can read the user key from the unlocked page's memory, and make same-origin requests that carry the cookie. Unchanged from today.
- **Same-site siblings and the origins CORS allows:** SameSite=Strict is about the site, so a page on a sibling host of the same site (another subdomain) can make the browser send the cookie with a request to the reload endpoints; it can't read the answer (the server's CORS allows only its own origin, the Safari extension's and the desktop app's origins, and an SSO provider's), and asking for R also needs the tab's access token, which it doesn't have. `__Secure-` doesn't stop a sibling from setting a cookie of the same name for the whole site; it can't know the right value, so the worst it does is make a reload ask. Hosts should keep sibling subdomains of the vault's site trusted, or give the vault a site of its own.
- **The device id isn't a secret:** it's in the browser's localStorage and goes with sign-ins. Binding an entry to the device means binding it to that device's session: the access token's device claim, which the server signs.
- **The time limit** is the server's own clock counting from the tab's report of how long it's been unused, under the policy's cap. The server trusts that report: whoever can send it has the tab's storage and its cookie. A tab whose clock moves forward reports more and ends sooner. A tab that sends nothing is ended by the server's own clock alone.
- **A closed tab reopened, or Back to the page:** the server ends the entry 15 seconds after the page left, so after that nothing can restore it. Within those 15 seconds, the page restores only if the browser calls its load a reload. In our tests (Chrome), Back reports "back_forward" and a tab opened again "navigate", and both stay locked. Playwright can't drive "Reopen closed tab", and MDN notes that browsers may report a reopened or duplicated tab as "back_forward" or copy the original tab's type; so a tab reopened within 15 seconds of closing, in a browser that copies "reload", would come back unlocked. That's the gap left: 15 seconds, for the same browser. Firefox wasn't automated here.
- **A browser restart** that restores its tabs: the entry ended 15 seconds after its page left as the browser closed, or at most 2 minutes after its last check-in if the browser crashed or didn't let the page say so. A restart quicker than that, after a crash, could restore it if the browser called the restored load a reload.
- **Shared computers:** a vault left open in a tab is open to the next person, as upstream's is, until the timeout. What a reload key adds is only a reload of that same open tab within the timeout; a closed tab, a reopened one, Back, or a restarted browser all ask. Where people share computers, turn "Stay unlocked when I reload" off, or the organization's policy turns it off for everyone (`webReload: false`), and a short timeout matters more than ever.
- **Duplicated tabs:** duplicating a tab copies its sessionStorage, and cookies belong to the browser, so both have the same tab id and cookie. A duplicate that isn't reported as a reload asks, and ends the original's entry at once, so the original asks at its next reload too. One reported as a reload shares the original's entry, and locking either ends it for both; the beacons of whichever page didn't ask last are ignored, so closing one leaves the other's reload working. A window the web vault opens itself asks ("navigate"), and ends the entry the same way.

**The wrap's test vectors** are `docs/vectors/reload-key-v1.json`, made by `make-reload-key-v1.mjs` with Node's own crypto: R, a user key, and the EncString, with refusals (another R, a changed MAC, a changed ciphertext). Tier 1 opens them with upstream's own crypto (`check-reload-key-vectors.mjs`), and the web vault's unit test opens them through upstream's `EncryptService` from a copy (`apps/web/src/app/keykeeper/reload-key/reload-key-v1.json`; Tier 1 checks the copy matches).

**Tests:**

- The server's unit tests: who gets R back, the 2 minutes, leaving and the reload that follows, a repeated leaving that can't push the end back, a late leaving from the page before a reload that can't end the reloaded one's, another account that can't take a tab id in use, the timeout, the tab's report, forgetting, the timer's sweep, the tab limit, the policy cap and what refuses (signing out, `webReload: false`), the strictest of several policies, reading the body and decoding R, the cookie (only the tab's own opens it), and the cookie's attributes.
- API tests (`e2e/tests/api/reload-key.spec.ts`): the same over HTTP; the cookie's attributes as sent; R refused without the cookie or with a wrong one; forgetting and ending expiring it; refusals that never echo R; a new master password, key rotation, "Deauthorize sessions", and deleting the account forgetting it; the policy cap for members and owners; "immediately", signing out, and `webReload: false` refusing it; the strictest policy of two organizations; the server's own clock ending an entry with nothing from the tab; a page leaving, and a leaving from another page ignored; the limits per tab and for many tabs; and a flood of leaving beacons that leaves signing in from the same address alone.
- The web vault's unit tests: the wrap against the vectors, when it applies (the setting, the policies, a stored "On browser refresh"), restoring only a reload and forgetting on the server otherwise, the leaving beacon and its page id, locking a page back from the cache, the setting and its notes, another tab turning it off or choosing "On browser refresh", forgetting with a fresh token and once more, and each step (`reload-key.spec.ts`, `reload-setting.component.spec.ts`); the mapping under "when the computer locks", which offers only minutes, counts "On browser refresh" as the cap, and keeps the stored choice, and a longest wait replacing "On browser refresh" turning the setting off (`keykeeper-system-lock.spec.ts`, the settings services' specs).
- End-to-end tests (`e2e/tests/reload-key.spec.ts`): a reload stays unlocked with no prompt and the tab's storage never holds R; the cookie is HttpOnly, Secure, Strict, and for the session, page scripts can't see it, and without it a reload asks; another browser with a copy of the tab's storage but not its cookie gets nothing, even reloading; a closed tab reopened is locked and the server let R go; Back asks, with the back-forward cache off and on; locking asks and expires the cookie; the account menu's Sign out forgets R first; "Stay unlocked when I reload" off makes every reload ask; a new browser asks; "On browser refresh" keeps asking, and under the Business preset, where Settings no longer offers it, a stored one still asks, with the setting off and a note; Sign out signs out at a reload; a member under the Business preset signs in, reloads, and stays unlocked, with Settings explaining the cap; with the web vault's wait at 1 minute and the clock moved, it locks; a policy with `webReload: false` turns it off, Settings saying why; and a server that restarts asks at the next reload.

**Status:** built; reviewed twice, the second review's fixes waiting for the reviewer.

## Health (better than Watchtower)

Reports are only useful if they lead to a fix.

- **Org password rules:** admins set minimum length, character sets, or passphrases. Every generator enforces them, on every device.
- **Site-aware generation:** generated passwords also follow each site's own rules, using `apple/password-manager-resources` data, so they are never rejected.
  - **Built:** the extension's suggested password in the menu inside a new-password field; "Generate password" from the right-click menu and the keyboard shortcut, for the page in front; and the item editor's generator in the web vault and the extension, for the item's first website, where the generator shows the password that follows the rules, so what someone copies from it is what's saved. A password the generator made that the site's rules accept is kept; otherwise one is made to the rules, from a secure random source, never with a space, keeping the generated one's length and kinds of characters (uppercase, lowercase, digits, symbols) wherever the rules allow. So the generator's settings and an organization's password generator policy still count, and where they conflict with the site's rules, the site's win, since a password the site rejects is no use. The rules are Apple's password rules language, read by our own parser (`libs/common/src/tools/keykeeper/password-rules.ts`), from Apple's MIT-licensed list of 458 sites, vendored at a pinned commit by `scripts/keykeeper/update-site-data.mjs` with Apple's license next to it. A site's entry covers its subdomains.
  - **Tests:** unit tests for the language (Apple's custom set quirks included), every listed site read and a password made for it at three lengths that follows its rules, and the wiring; an extension test where the suggested password on a sign-up page is the usual 20 characters on a site without rules and follows airfrance.com's (8 to 12, a fixed set of symbols), served locally under that name; and a web vault test where the item editor's generator, for an item at www.airfrance.com, shows and uses a password that follows them.
  - **Keeping current:** the weekly upstream watch (`tools/upstream-watch`) flags when Apple's data has changed since the commit the clients take it from, with the command that takes the newer one. The Generator page on its own has no site, so it's unchanged.
- **One-click fix:** open the site's change-password page (`/.well-known/change-password` where available), generate a compliant password, and save it with history.
  - **Built:** "Change password" (on an at-risk item's view, and the extension's at-risk prompts) opens the site's own change-password page. The apps first check Apple's MIT-licensed list of 805 sites' change-password pages on the device (`libs/common/src/tools/keykeeper/change-password-urls.ts`, vendored with the password rules by `scripts/keykeeper/update-site-data.mjs`). Otherwise they ask the server, as upstream's apps do (`/icons/change-password-uri`), and ours answers with the site's standard address (`/.well-known/change-password`) when the site supports it by the W3C check: that address answers with success and a path no site has doesn't. The server asks sites only the way it downloads icons (not when icon downloads are off or go to an outside service, never a blocked address, through the same client, and at most twice per site a day), only on ports 443 and 80, and only for people who show website icons, as upstream's apps already decide. Anyone may ask, as for icons, so it's limited per address and across the server, and people asking about one site at once share one lookup (see "What the server fetches"). With neither, the site itself opens.
  - **Tests:** unit tests for the list lookup and the apps' order (no request when the list has the site), the server's check and which websites it may reach; an API test that the lookup answers and never reaches a site with icon downloads off.
- **Privacy-preserving admin view:** org-owned items are checked by an admin's client. For private items, each member's client reports only counts (weak, reused, breached), never which items. See "Members' own passwords" below.

### Exposed passwords

Have I Been Pwned's Pwned Passwords says whether a password is in a known breach without being given the password (k-anonymity). Every check in the web vault and the extension goes through one client (`libs/common/src/dirt/keykeeper/pwned-passwords.ts`), which upstream's AuditService now uses, so the reports, the MSP console, the count beside Health, the item editor's Check button, and the master password check at sign-up all behave the same.

- **What leaves the browser:** for each password, only the first 5 hex characters of its SHA-1 hash (20 of its 160 bits), as `GET https://api.pwnedpasswords.com/range/<prefix>` with `Add-Padding: true`, without cookies or other credentials, without a referrer, kept out of the browser's cache, and refusing redirects. The service answers with every suffix it knows under that prefix (hundreds, plus decoys seen 0 times, so the answer's size says nothing), and the browser makes the match itself. Never the password, its full hash, or which item it's in. So an automatic check (the count beside Health, the MSP console) sends the service the set of 5-character prefixes of the vault's different passwords, in random order rather than the vault's. Like any website, the service (behind Cloudflare) sees the browser's IP address, when it asked, and the `Origin` header, which is the web vault's address. The web vault's content security policy (the server's header) already allows that one address.
- **Only the service's answer counts:** a 200 with at least 100 lines, each a 35-character uppercase suffix and a count of up to 10 digits, and at most 1 MB. Anything else (an empty or short 200, a 204, another status, a captive portal's page, a filtering proxy or inspecting firewall's answer) is a failed check, never "not exposed", and is never remembered.
- **Who decides:** Settings, Appearance has "Check my passwords against known breaches after unlocking", per account, on by default. It follows the account (November 2026): it's kept in the account's settings document (`"health": {"checkAfterUnlock": false}`, encrypted with the account's key; see "Generator settings"), so turning it off in one browser turns it off in the account's other web vaults at their next sync, and other open tabs of the web vault follow a change at once. Each browser keeps its own copy too, so the setting applies before the first sync and on a server without the settings document. Conflicts are as for the generator's settings: last save wins. An end-to-end test turns it off in one browser and finds it off in a new one, then back on there and on again in the first after a reload (`e2e/tests/exposed-passwords.spec.ts`). Off, the web vault sends nothing to the breach service on its own: the count beside Health counts weak and reused only, and the MSP console's Exposed column says "Not checked" beside a Check exposed passwords button. Passwords are checked only when the person opens the exposed passwords report, presses that button, or presses Check in the item editor. For MSP staff, the automatic count covers every client item they can edit, since a person's Health does. Separately, creating or changing a master password checks it the same way while the form's "Check known data breaches for this password" is ticked, as it is at first.
- **How often:** 4 requests at a time at most, starting at least 25 ms apart; a 429 or 5xx is asked once more after a short wait (Retry-After, at most 5 seconds). Within a run, each different password is one request, and passwords whose hashes start alike share one.
- **What's kept:** the client keeps nothing once a check is done: no password, hash, or answer, the master password included, in the web vault, the extension, and the desktop app alike. The web vault remembers what its automatic checks found by item id and revision date, never by password or hash, so the MSP console working its counts out again after an action doesn't ask again, and an edited item is asked about anew; failures are never remembered, and it's all forgotten when the vault locks or signs out (the web vault reloads then too). Nothing is kept across sessions: doing that would mean encrypting results with the account's key, a new use of a key that would need its own design and test vectors.
- **Calling a check off:** each run carries a signal. The MSP console calls its run off when it works the counts out again or the person leaves it, and the count beside Health when the items change or it's no longer shown. A request every waiting check has called off is dropped from the queue, or stopped if it's out. Automatic checks also read the setting again just before each request goes out, so turning it off (in any tab) while a run is queued sends nothing more.
- **When the service can't be reached** (offline, a firewall, a captive portal, an error, or no answer within 10 seconds): the check fails instead of answering "not exposed". The checks already waiting behind a failure fail at once, so a firewalled network doesn't wait out a timeout per password; checks asked for later try again. The exposed report says how many items couldn't be checked (and never says "Good news" then), the MSP console says "Couldn't check" with Try again and how many items, the count beside Health keeps weak and reused and shows a sign that it couldn't check (shown even when there's nothing else to fix, so a failed check never looks clean), the item editor says it couldn't check, and sign-up goes on without it, as before. What couldn't be checked is counted in items everywhere.
- **The same counts everywhere:** which passwords count, and what's weak, reused, and exposed, are upstream's report rules kept in one place (`libs/common/src/dirt/keykeeper/password-health.ts`) that the weak, reused, and exposed reports (a person's and each organization's), the MSP console, and the count beside Health all use. Counted: logins with a password the person may see, outside the trash, and in a person's own Health only items they can edit. Weak is a strength score of 2 or less out of 4, with the username's parts as hints. Reused is a password another counted item also uses (within one client, in the MSP console). Exposed is seen in at least one breach.
- **In the MSP console:** an Exposed column per client beside Weak and Reused. Weak and reused show first; exposed follows, with progress across every client ("Checking passwords against known breaches: 12 of 40"). For a client whose items the person can see, a count with something in it opens the client's list of items to fix by name (never their passwords), the most exposed first, with what's wrong (Exposed N times, Weak, Reused), how many items couldn't be checked ("Nothing weak or reused; 2 items couldn't be checked for breaches" rather than "Nothing to fix"), and Open: in the client's admin console for its owners and admins, otherwise in the person's own vault, since a custom role can't load the admin console's vault. The console counts the items the person's own vault holds, as its expiring list does; an organization's own reports count every item its admin console lists.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the client (only 5-character prefixes sent, padding asked for, no redirects, nothing else in the request; matching with either line ending and padding entries; every answer that isn't the service's refused and asked again next time: empty, whitespace, 204, other statuses, too few lines, lowercase, an over-long count, a short suffix, over 1 MB by header or by stream; one retry after a 429 or 5xx with Retry-After held to its cap, none when offline; nothing kept; the queue failing behind a failure; timeouts; at most 4 at once and spaced; calling off a queued run sends nothing more, a shared request goes on while any check wants it, and a setting turned off after queueing stops the rest; random order) and for the web vault's memory by item version (never failures, forgotten on lock), the shared rules, the MSP counts and details, the count beside Health (a sign when it couldn't check, calling off on recount, the setting read before each request), the setting following another tab, and the item editor's message; and an end-to-end test where Playwright stands in for the service (the test browser reaches no outside host): each client's counts, the console asking nothing more when it works them out again, the list of what to fix and Open, "Couldn't check" in items when firewalled and Try again, the count beside Health and its sign when firewalled, the exposed passwords report, and every request checked to be a padded GET for a 5-character prefix and nothing more; and, with the setting off, no request at all after unlocking (the count is weak and reused, the console says "Not checked") until the console's button or the report asks, and turning it back on in another tab reaches the open one.

### Members' own passwords (the admin view)

An organization's owners and admins see how healthy their people's own passwords are without seeing them: each member's apps count the logins in the member's Personal items and send the server only the numbers. The organization's own items are already checked where its admins can decrypt them, in the admin console's reports, by the same rules ("The same counts everywhere" above). Decided November 2026.

- **What's reported:** four whole numbers about the logins in the person's Personal items (not an organization's, not in the trash) whose passwords they may see, by the rules Health uses: how many are counted (`logins`), how many have a weak password (`weak`), how many share a password with another of these logins (`reused`), and how many are in a known breach (`exposed`). Exposed is sent only when every one of them was checked; otherwise it's left out, never guessed. A report is `{"logins": 40, "weak": 3, "reused": 4, "exposed": 1}` and nothing else: never an item's name, id, website, username, folder, password, hash, or which logins have which problem.
- **Who reports:** the web vault, once the count beside Health is worked out after unlocking (exposed only while "Check my passwords against known breaches after unlocking" is on and every check answered); the browser extension, after unlocking, with weak and reused (it doesn't check breaches on its own); and our apps, after each sync, with weak and reused. Only for someone who is a confirmed member of an organization, once per unlock and again when the numbers change, and never while the server doesn't have the endpoint (an older or other server answers 404, and the app stops asking).
- **How it's kept:** one row per account (`keykeeper_member_health`): the numbers, when they were reported, and when exposed was last checked. A report replaces the numbers; one without exposed keeps the last exposed count and when it was checked, so the extension reporting after the web vault doesn't lose the web vault's breach check. These are counts, not vault contents: nothing in them says which item or what password, so the server keeps them as they are, not encrypted, which lets it show them to admins without any key. No history; deleting the account deletes them.
- **Who reads it:** the organization's owners and admins (an MSP's people among them where that's their role in the client), in the admin console's Reports: "Members' own passwords", one row per confirmed member with the numbers and when they were reported, or "Not reported yet". Left out: anyone invited and not confirmed, revoked members, and an MSP's people in its clients (a client sees nothing about a technician's own items). Custom roles don't see it, since reports permissions for custom roles aren't built, and nobody else does: the endpoint answers 404 to everyone who isn't an owner or admin, as anything a page loads must, so a refusal never signs anyone out. Leaving or being removed from an organization ends its view at once, since the list is worked out from who's a member when it's asked for.
- **The person knows:** the web vault's Health page says, for a confirmed member of an organization, that the owners and admins of their organizations see these numbers and never which items (it doesn't name the organizations: an MSP's clients don't see its people's numbers, and the page doesn't know which organizations are an MSP's clients). There's no switch to stop it: an organization that asks its people to keep work passwords in PassBundle needs to know whether their own passwords are a risk, and the numbers say nothing more than that.
- **What it can't stop:** someone's own app could send made-up numbers about their own items; the server checks only that each is a whole number, `logins` at most 100,000, and the others at most `logins`, and answers 400 with a plain message otherwise. A hacked server could show admins wrong numbers, and learns nothing from them it couldn't from the vault's size.
- **The API:** `PUT /api/keykeeper/own-health` with the numbers, by the account itself; `GET /api/keykeeper/organizations/<id>/member-health` for its owners and admins: `{memberId, logins, weak, reused, exposed, exposedCheckedAt, reportedAt}` for each confirmed member, by membership id only (the page names people from the member list it already loads). The server's code is its own (`keykeeper_member_health.rs`), outside upstream's.
- **Status:** built (November 2026): the server (`keykeeper_member_health.rs` and its migration for SQLite and PostgreSQL), the web vault (the numbers worked out with the count beside Health, `health-count.service.ts` and `libs/common/src/dirt/keykeeper/own-health.ts`; the report, `keykeeper/health/member-health-report.component.ts`; the line on a member's Health page), the extension (`KeykeeperOwnHealthWatch`, after unlocking and when items change), and our apps' core (after each sync).
- **Tests:** server unit tests (whole numbers in range, a report without exposed keeping the last check, an MSP's people told apart on a real database); API tests (`e2e/tests/api/member-health.spec.ts`: a member's numbers reach the owner and an admin by membership id, with no names; a member, a custom role, someone outside, another organization's owner, and an organization that doesn't exist get 404; invited and revoked members and an MSP's people in a client left out; bad numbers refused with a plain 400; exposed kept; deleting the account deletes them); jest for the counts (Personal only, the trash and an organization's items left out, exposed only when every one was checked, numbers and nothing else), sending (once until they change or the next unlock, nothing for someone who isn't a confirmed member, stopping at a server without it), the extension's watch, and the report's rows; and end to end (`e2e/tests/member-health.spec.ts`): a member's web vault counts four logins (one weak, two sharing a password, one breached, by a stand-in for the breach service) and the owner's report shows 4, 1, 2, and 1 and no item's name, the member's Health page says who sees the numbers, and a plain member gets neither the report nor anything that signs them out. Our apps' test is in passbundle-apps (`core/tests/server.rs`).
- **Not yet:** a switch for an organization that doesn't want the numbers, and the MSP console showing them per client.

### Domain breach report (waiting on the owner)

1Password's domain breach report (Teams and Business) lists breaches that include email addresses at a company's domain; Bitwarden's data breach report checks one address. Both come from Have I Been Pwned's breach API, which, unlike Pwned Passwords, needs a paid API key (a subscription with a request limit), and searching a whole domain also needs the domain verified with Have I Been Pwned. So it isn't built. Decisions for the owner: whether to buy a key for the hosted service, and which plan; that the key stays on the server (`HIBP_API_KEY`), never in the apps; and whether each MSP verifies its clients' domains with Have I Been Pwned, or we do it for them. Upstream's per-person data breach report already works on a server with a key; without one, as now, it offers a link to check by hand.

## Legacy contacts

Built on the inherited emergency access feature (web vault and server). Bitwarden puts it behind Premium. We include it in every plan.

- Solo and Family onboarding suggests naming a legacy contact.
- A request starts a waiting period (default 7 days). The owner gets email and push reminders and can deny the request at any time. With no response, access is granted.
- The access level is either view or takeover.
- A printable instructions sheet helps the family find the account.
- **Open:** letting owners exclude items or folders. Upstream grants the whole vault.

## Account keys (open)

- **Upstream model:** the master password and email go through a KDF (PBKDF2 by default, or Argon2id) to make the master key, which unlocks the account key stored on the server. The server never sees the master password. If the database is stolen, each vault can be attacked offline, and only the master password's strength protects it.
- **1Password model:** the master password is combined with a 128-bit Secret Key that never leaves the user's devices, so a stolen database is useless. The cost is the Secret Key step in onboarding, which is exactly the friction we want to remove.
- **Proposal:**
  1. Version 1 keeps the upstream model with stronger defaults: Argon2id, plus master-password strength and breach checks at signup. Changing the key hierarchy is the riskiest change possible.
  2. Business orgs get trusted-device unlock, where a device key works like a Secret Key the user never sees.
  3. After an external crypto review: an optional account key for Solo and Family, added to the KDF input and kept on devices and in the recovery kit. New devices get it from an existing device by QR code, never by typing or downloading.

## Browser password manager

Disable it, don't purge it.

- **Browser level:** policies `PasswordManagerEnabled`, `AutofillCreditCardEnabled`, and `AutofillAddressEnabled` in Chrome and Edge, plus the Firefox equivalents.
- **Profile level:** the extension sets the same switches through the browser's privacy API, only in profiles where it is active.
- **Open:** does the policy stop already-saved passwords from filling? Sources disagree. Tier 2 settles it.

## Passkeys

- Inherited from upstream GPL code: passkeys in the browser extension, the Windows 11 plugin authenticator and macOS AutoFill provider on desktop. Upstream's mobile apps have them too, but our phone apps are our own.
- Our job: make sure the server stores and syncs passkey items correctly (verify against Vaultwarden), then cover registration and sign-in in tiers 1 and 2.
- **Status, the extension:** tier 1 covers it (`e2e/tests/extension-passkeys.spec.ts`). A site makes a passkey through the extension, saved as a new login, and signs in with it; a second browser signed in to the same account gets it from the server and signs in with it too. The test site checks each answer as a site would. It found that the extension's passkey window never opened when Chrome refused its place (partly off the screen); every popout now falls back to the browser's place. Not yet: tier 2 (a real browser and a real site).
- **Our apps (Android built, iPhone next):** `docs/core.md` "Passkeys" and `docs/phones.md` "Passkeys". The core signs and makes ES256 passkeys in upstream's format, the private key never leaving it (`core/src/passkey.rs`). Android 14 and later get them through Credential Manager, with the site's Digital Asset Links checked for apps; that's built, and the emulator test makes a passkey and signs in with it. iOS 17 and later get them through the AutoFill extension, which isn't merged yet (see "Clients", "iOS"). Vectors fix the bytes (RFC 6979), and a real-server test runs passkeys both ways between the core and our extension (`e2e/tests/passkeys-interop.spec.ts`). Not yet: passkeys in the desktop apps (the extension has them in browsers), and on phones, replacing an account's old passkey and choosing an existing login when saving one.
- **AAGUID:** our apps get PassBundle's own (`09c5ee5f-0845-48e6-aef4-c7c443317de5`). The browser extension still makes passkeys with upstream's, so sites that name a passkey's maker by its AAGUID name upstream's product for them. Moving the extension to ours, and listing ours where sites look it up, is the owner's call.

## Connector pages

The web vault serves small pages for steps that run outside the app: the security key step of two-step login (`webauthn-connector.html`, in a frame on the web vault's sign-in page, and `webauthn-fallback-connector.html`, in a tab the extension opens), Duo, and single sign-on. They read their inputs from the address, so anyone can open or frame them with inputs of their choosing.

**What went wrong** (pre-launch security pass, October 27, 2026). Upstream's security key connector sent its answer to whatever `parent` its address named, unless it ran on upstream's own domains, and the server sent connector pages with no Content-Security-Policy and no X-Frame-Options. A phishing page could relay the vault's sign-in challenge, frame the connector with `allow="publickey-credentials-get *"` and itself as the parent, and receive the key's signature with no click, since Chromium asks the key at once. The browser marks such an answer `crossOrigin: true` with the phishing page as `topOrigin`, but webauthn-rs checks crossOrigin only when a key is added, so the server signed the attacker in: real-time phishing that defeats security keys.

**The rules now:**

- **A connector answers only the vault and PassBundle's extension.** The security key connector sends to its parent only when the parent is on the web vault's own origin or is PassBundle's extension (`apps/web/src/connectors/common.ts`), and, where the browser lists the pages around a frame (`location.ancestorOrigins`, in Chromium and Safari), only when every one of them is too. Firefox doesn't list them, so there the page that loaded the frame (`document.referrer`) must be on the parent's origin, and a frame whose referrer was withheld gets nothing. Otherwise it asks no key and sends nothing. It posts to that origin, never `*`, and takes stop and start only from its parent at that origin. The extension's tab posts to its own origin and asks no key inside a frame. Duo's and single sign-on's connectors already posted only to their own origin.
- **Only the vault and PassBundle's extension may frame a vault page.** Connector pages are sent with `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN` and a policy of their own: `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'self'; frame-ancestors 'self' chrome-extension://cenoidjoogmoieibpcokkafjnaabhokg`. They load only their own scripts, styles, images, and translations. Duo's connector leaves for Duo by navigating, which the policy doesn't limit, and there are no captcha connectors anymore (upstream removed them). The vault's other pages have the same frame-ancestors plus `ALLOWED_IFRAME_ANCESTORS`, which never reaches connector pages. Upstream's two extension IDs and every Firefox extension (`moz-extension://*`) are gone. The extension opens connector pages in tabs, never frames.
- **The server refuses every security key answer made in a frame.** Sign-in rejects an answer whose clientDataJSON says `crossOrigin: true`, whatever its `topOrigin`, and one that names any `topOrigin` at all (passbundle-server, `src/keykeeper_origins.rs`), whichever page asked for it. No PassBundle flow makes one: the web vault frames its own connector on its own origin, and the extension uses a tab. So a security key step framed by our own extension would be refused too, which leaves the extension's entry in frame-ancestors and in the connector's list with no use today; dropping either is a one-line change.
- **Upstream's deep links are gone.** The mobile and desktop return paths (`bitwarden://...-callback`, `https://bitwarden.com/...-callback`) and the mobile connector page handed a security key's answer or a Duo code to upstream's apps. PassBundle's own apps ask a security key themselves, and Duo's page hands its answer only to them, as `passbundle://duo-callback`, which finishes only the sign-in whose state it carries ("Two-step login in the apps").
- **HTTPS only.** When `DOMAIN` is https on a host name, every response carries `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000`. Never for localhost or an address (the Windows Hello test kit runs at https://localhost, the e2e tests at an address), where the server also takes off the one Rocket adds to a release build serving TLS itself. `HSTS_INCLUDE_SUBDOMAINS` (off by default) adds `includeSubDomains`, for a host whose every name below serves HTTPS; the hosted service turns it on (ops, hosting/app.env.example). The test server's Caddy sends `includeSubDomains` too, which covers only names under test.passbundle.com.
- **Embedding the vault elsewhere.** A vault framed by a page that `ALLOWED_IFRAME_ANCESTORS` allows can't ask a security key in that frame: the browser, the connector, and the server all refuse. That's intended.

**PassBundle's extension IDs:**

- **Chrome and Edge:** `cenoidjoogmoieibpcokkafjnaabhokg`, from the public key in brand.json (`extension.chromiumKey`), which `brand/apply.mjs` puts in the Chrome and Edge manifests and checks against `extension.chromiumId`. Every build loaded unpacked (CI's, the e2e tests', the test Mac's) has this ID. The private half stays with the owner, outside the repos, to pack a .crx with the same ID.
- **Firefox:** `extension@passbundle.com` instead of upstream's ID. Firefox gives each install a random `moz-extension://` origin, so no rule can name our Firefox extension by origin; Firefox uses the tab, not a frame.
- **Where they're set:** brand.json for the clients (the connector's list, `keykeeper-origins.ts`, comes from it at build time), and the server's default for `EXTENSION_IDS`, which a development build with another key overrides. Tier 1 checks that the server's frame-ancestors name brand.json's ID and that the built extension has it. A store listing gets an ID of its own (the Edge store always assigns one), added to both then. The installers' force-install IDs are still placeholders tied to the stores' update URLs.

**Tests:** `e2e/tests/connector-origins.spec.ts` starts a server named `vault.connectors.test` (WebAuthn won't take an IP address as a site's name) and uses Chromium's virtual authenticator. Another site's page (`fixtures/autofill/frames-security-key.html`) frames the connector with a real sign-in's challenge: the browser refuses the frame. With the server's header removed, as a proxy might, the connector refuses on its own, and an answer made in that frame by other means is refused by the server, while the same key answering on the vault's own page signs in. The connector pages work under their policy (the extension's security key tab, Duo's answer and its start, the email links' redirect) with nothing refused. The web vault signs in with a security key in its frame, and the extension in its tab, with PassBundle's ID. Unit tests cover the connector's rules, Firefox's referrer included (`common.spec.ts`, `webauthn.spec.ts`), and the server's (`keykeeper_origins.rs`: the refusals, the connector policy, and where HSTS applies). Against the builds from before the fix, the other site received `success|<answer>` with no click, and the server signed that answer in.

## Approving a sign-in from another device

Someone signing in on the web vault or the browser extension picks "Sign in with a device", and approves it from PassBundle's app on their phone or computer (or from the web vault or extension where they're already signed in). The asking side is upstream's GPL code; the server's rules and the approving app are ours. A key moves between devices, so this note and its vectors (`docs/vectors/device-approval-v1.json`) come before the code.

**Status (October 27, 2026):** built, with the security review's fixes. The server's rules (passbundle-server), the apps' prompt and Settings list with the core's crypto (passbundle-apps, `core/src/device_approval.rs`), and one fix to the web vault and extension (below). Tested end to end in tier 1: the web vault asks, our core approves, and the web vault signs in.

### The protocol

Upstream's, read from its GPL clients (`libs/auth`'s login-via-auth-request component and `AuthRequestService`, `KeyService.getFingerprint`, and the approval dialog in `libs/angular`). Nothing under `bitwarden_license` was opened.

1. **Asking.** The device asking makes an RSA-2048 key pair and a 25-character access code, and sends `POST /api/auth-requests` with the email, its device identifier, the public key (SubjectPublicKeyInfo, base64), the access code, and type 0 ("authenticate and unlock"). It shows the fingerprint phrase and waits on the server's anonymous websocket for an answer. The private key stays on it (in memory, and in the web vault's session cache while it waits).
