Upstream's web vault and extension offer "Sign in with a device" only on a device that signed in to the account before, and the server checks the same.
2. **Seeing it.** The account's other devices list it (`GET /api/auth-requests/pending`): its id, public key, the requesting device's type and identifier, address, and time.
3. **Approving.** The approving device wraps the **user key** (64 bytes, the same key the master password unlocks; not the master key, and no master password hash) to the request's public key with RSA-OAEP, SHA-1 for both the hash and MGF1, as an encrypted string of type 4, and sends `PUT /api/auth-requests/<id>` with its own device identifier, the key, and `requestApproved: true`. Denying sends `requestApproved: false` and no key (upstream's dialog wraps the key even to deny; our apps don't, and our server ignores it).
4. **Signing in.** The asking device reads the answer with its access code (`GET /api/auth-requests/<id>/response?code=`), opens the user key with its private key, and signs in with the password grant: the email, the access code as the password, `authRequest=<id>`, and its own device identifier and type. The server sends it the account's encrypted keys as at any sign-in; the user key it now holds opens them.

**The fingerprint phrase:** SHA-256 of the request's public key (DER), expanded with HKDF-Expand-SHA256 under the account's email in lower case to 32 bytes, read as one big-endian number, then written in base 7,776 from its lowest digit up, one word of EFF's long list per digit, five words joined by hyphens (about 64 bits). The asking device makes it from the key it made, the approving device from the key the server gave it.

**What the phrase protects:** the server, or anyone who could change what passes through it, relaying a public key of its own instead of the asking device's, so the user key would be wrapped to them. Then the two devices show different phrases. It only protects when the person compares them, so our apps put the phrase first and largest, and say to approve only if it matches. Finding another key with the same phrase means trying about 2^64 keys, and the request lasts 15 minutes. The phrase is no secret, and says nothing about the account's keys.

### The server's rules (passbundle-server)

Vaultwarden had the endpoints. What's ours (`src/api/core/keykeeper_device_approval.rs`, the `// Keykeeper:` blocks in `accounts.rs`, `identity.rs`, and `auth.rs`):

- **15 minutes**, from when the request was made, for everything: listing it, reading it, approving or denying it, the asking device reading its answer, and signing in with it. Upstream's purge deletes it then. (Vaultwarden let the sign-in happen only in the first 5 minutes, so an approval after that couldn't be used.) Reading or answering a request that has expired, or is gone (denied, replaced, or purged), answers 404 either way, so nothing depends on when the purge runs.
- **Who asks:** only a device the account knows, of the type it says it is (Vaultwarden's rule). The request must hold a public key of a sensible size and an access code of 8 to 128 printable characters.
- **Who signs in with it:** only the device that asked, as the type it asked as, from the same address, with the access code: an approval isn't a bearer token for any of the account's devices.
- **Rate limits:** the address's limit for unauthenticated calls, and the account's own login limits (DESIGN.md, "Login limits"): a request counts as a sign-in attempt. One request waiting per device (a new one replaces it, as the web vault's "Resend" does), five requests from one device an hour, and five waiting per account; past those, 429.
  - **What they cost, as a denial of service:** these limits count by the account's device, and a device identifier isn't a secret: every request a device makes carries it, so anyone who has seen one (a shared computer, a proxy's log, malware) and knows its type can make that device's five requests an hour, and stop it signing in with a device until the hour is up, or, with several of the account's identifiers, fill the account's five waiting requests for 15 minutes. That's all they get: every request still needs an approval, signing in with the master password is never limited this way, and a known device signs in with its password past the account's login limits too.
- **Scope:** listing, reading, and answering are the account's own; another account gets "doesn't exist". A device never sees or answers its own request. The answer is read only with the access code, from the same device type and address that asked.
- **Who approves:** only a session that signed in with the master password. Not a session that itself came from an approval (an approval can't approve the next device, so one mistaken approval doesn't spread), not an API key's, and not SSO's, which gets a trusted-device design of its own. The access token says how its session signed in (`keykeeper_signin`), and the refresh token keeps it across refreshes. Denying is open to any of the account's sessions: it hands nothing out.
- **Containment:** a session an approval began can't hand the account on, or take over another one: emergency access, on either side, needs a session that signed in with the master password or an organization's single sign-on (so members required to use single sign-on keep it), not one from an approval, an API key's, or one that doesn't say. As a grantor: inviting an emergency contact, changing a grant, confirming one, and approving a takeover early; as a grantee: starting a takeover, taking over, and setting the new master password. (Changing the master password, two-step login, and the API key already ask for the master password.)
- **Organizations' sign-in rules hold for it:** a sign-in with an approval proves no password, so it goes through the same "Require single sign-on" check as the password grant (someone an organization requires to use its single sign-on is sent there, linked or not), and again in its own place (`keykeeper_device_approval::approved_sign_in_rules`), which is where any other rule for every sign-in, such as an admin two-step rule, goes too. Two-step login itself applies as for a password. A session from an approval refreshes only while single sign-on isn't required of the account, as a password session does.
- **The key:** an approval must carry a type 4 encrypted string (RSA-OAEP with SHA-1, of a ciphertext's size). The server stores it as it came and can't open it: only the asking device has the private key. It never stores or returns a master password hash, whatever a client sends. The server never holds a key that opens a vault.
- **Once:** an approved request signs in one time (Vaultwarden's rule).
- **Events**, logged for the person and each of their organizations, as upstream's: requested (1010), approved (1513), denied (1514), the sign-in as a sign-in (1000), and failures as failed sign-ins (1005).
- **Notifications:** as upstream, the server tells the account's devices on its websocket (and push, where a push relay is set up), and tells the asking device on the anonymous websocket when it's approved. Our apps ask every 10 seconds instead (below).
- **Email two-step login** with a request sends the code only to the request's own account, and not once it's expired.
- **The server's own log** leaves out a request's access code: the answer's address (`/response?code=`) is logged without its query.

### Our apps

Design in `docs/core.md`, "Approving a sign-in", and `docs/phones.md`, "Approving a sign-in".

- **Seeing requests:** while the vault is unlocked and the app is on screen, the core asks the server every 10 seconds (one small request; the app's screens go on meanwhile). A prompt opens over the vault once per requesting device every 15 minutes; if the device sends its request again ("Resend"), the new one takes the open prompt's place, says its phrase is new, and holds Approve for 3 seconds, so it isn't tapped for the old one. Only when its identifier, type, and address all match the open one's: a new request that differs in any of them is another asker, so the open prompt closes and the new request prompts on its own. Settings, "Sign-in requests", lists every one waiting, to review. Expired requests disappear, an open prompt closes when its time is up or the request is answered elsewhere, and how long is left is by the server's clock.
- **The prompt:** "Approve sign-in?", the fingerprint phrase first and largest, then the device ("Web vault in Chrome"), its address, when it asked, and how long is left. Approve, Deny, or Later.
- **Approving asks it's you** first, by default: Windows Hello or Touch ID on a computer, the fingerprint or face on a phone, or the master password. The prompts say they're for approving a sign-in ("Approve sign-in" on phones; on a computer the desktop agent's prompt says "Approve a sign-in to PassBundle" where the OS shows its words, which Windows Hello's key prompt doesn't), and the app's master password dialog says the same. The core enforces it:
  - an approval within a minute of the person confirming;
  - one confirmation, one answer: an approval, a denial, or anything that goes wrong uses it up;
  - only the approval's own confirmation counts: the master password given for it, or the biometric prompt made for it, whose stored user key must be the open vault's. Unlocking, a passkey's check that it's the person, or asking for the master password again for an item never confirms an approval;
  - turning "Confirm before approving" off asks for the same confirmation first, and uses it up; turning it on asks nothing.
- **The core sends the key only to the request whose phrase the person saw.** It keeps the requests it listed, approves only one of them by the phrase shown, checks the server still has it waiting with the same public key, and wraps the user key to the key it listed. A public key that isn't RSA-2048 with the exponent 65537 (what upstream makes) is refused, with nothing sent.
- **Where the user key goes:** on a computer, it stays in Rust: the agent hands it to the core over its encrypted channel, the core compares it and wraps it. On phones, confirming with biometrics passes it through the app's native code exactly as biometric unlock already does: Kotlin opens it from the Keystore after the prompt (Swift reads it from the Keychain) and hands the bytes straight to the core, then zeroes them (Kotlin and Swift; Swift's copy is a `Data` it resets). It never reaches Dart, and the wrap to the request's public key is made and sent from Rust.
- **Phones** show a request while PassBundle is open, or the next time it opens. A prompt while it's closed needs push notifications, which wait on our own push service (APNs, FCM, UnifiedPush; "Clients" above).

### The web vault and the extension

Upstream's GPL code already approves: the extension shows its approval dialog when the server's websocket tells it, and the web vault's Settings, Security, Devices shows a device's waiting request and opens the same dialog. Both are held to the server's rules above. **Their approval is one click:** upstream's dialog shows the phrase, the device, its address, and the time, and Confirm sends the key, with no biometrics or master password asked. The confirmation is only in our apps. In a browser, approving needs only an unlocked session that signed in with the master password.

**One fix (passbundle-clients):** the asking side made its access code with the person's password generator settings, and PassBundle's add symbols. The code goes into the answer's address unencoded, which the client's own check refuses when it holds `#` or `/` and can't read with a `%`, so most requests could never read their answer. It's 25 letters and digits now (about 140 bits).

### Threat model

- **A phishing site relaying the phrase.** A fake sign-in page drives a real request from the attacker's machine and shows the victim that request's phrase, so the phrase matches. What stands in the way: the request must come from a device the account already knows (the attacker's machine isn't one, unless they have one of the person's device identifiers too); the prompt names the device and its address; it says to approve only a sign-in the person started themselves, on a device in front of them; requests are few and short-lived; and every request, approval, and sign-in is in the event log. A person who approves an attacker's request has handed over their vault, as with typing the master password into a phishing page. Not yet: where the address is, in this prompt.
- **Request spam (approval fatigue).** One request waiting per device, five from a device an hour, five waiting per account, the account's login limits, the address's limit, and only known devices may ask. Our apps prompt once per device every 15 minutes, so "Resend" doesn't bring a new prompt; a resent request shows in the open prompt's place. Nothing happens without an approval, and our apps ask for biometrics or the master password to approve. The web vault and extension don't (above): an unlocked browser session approves with one click.
- **Approving on a compromised device.** Malware on an unlocked approving device already has the user key, as with every password manager; approving gives it nothing new. In our apps, asking for biometrics or the master password stops someone who picks up an unlocked phone or computer, and turning that off asks for it too; in an unlocked browser, it doesn't. A session that came from an approval can't approve, or use emergency access on either side, so an attacker's approved device can't approve more, set up a takeover of this account, or start one of another account that trusts it.
- **A hacked server** can swap the public key (the phrase shows it), hide requests (a nuisance), or make up a request (approving it gives the key to whoever holds that request's private key: the phrase check is what stops a made-up one, as the person has no matching phrase on a device in front of them). It never sees the user key.
- **The access code** is a secret for the answer and the sign-in, sent only to the server over TLS. On its own it opens nothing: the answer needs the private key, and the sign-in needs the device that asked, from its address. It appears in a reverse proxy's access log (Cloudflare's, nginx's) as the answer's `?code=`, upstream's wire format; the server's own log leaves it out. That's acceptable: it works only from the address and device that asked, once, and for 15 minutes, and opens nothing without the private key, which never leaves the asking device.

### Tests

- **Vectors** (`docs/vectors/device-approval-v1.json`, made by `make-device-approval-v1.mjs` with plain Node and EFF's word list, and remade the same in tier 1): the phrase for an email and a key (and the same email with capitals, another account, another key), the user key wrapped with RSA-OAEP SHA-1 (encoded by hand with a fixed seed), and public keys the apps refuse. Upstream's crypto checks them (`check-device-approval-vectors.mjs`: the wrap opens, upstream's own wrap is the same scheme), and upstream's own code makes the same phrases (the clients' unit test `keykeeper-device-approval.spec.ts`). The core's tests make every phrase and open the wrap.
- **Server:** unit tests for the 15 minutes, which sessions approve, only the asking device signing in, the log leaving out the code, and what a request and an approval may hold; API tests for every endpoint and caller (`e2e/tests/api/device-approval.spec.ts`): the account's other device, the device asking, another account, no one, a session from an approval (before and after refreshing), an API key's, a denial, an expired request, too many waiting, five an hour from a device, an unknown device, signing in from another device or as another type, a key that isn't type 4, a master password hash never kept, "Require single sign-on", and emergency access refused to a session from an approval on both sides (inviting, changing, and confirming; starting a takeover, taking over, and setting the new password) while a single sign-on session invites and confirms.
- **The core against a real server** (`core/tests/server.rs`): listing, the phrase, approving only after confirming and with the phrase shown, the key opening with the request's private key only, signing in with it, a confirmation used up by a denial and by a failure, unlocking not confirming, denying, turning confirming off (after confirming), another account, and locking. On the vectors' vault (`core/tests/approvals.rs`): turning confirming off needs a fresh confirmation, the vault's own key confirms and another doesn't, unlocking doesn't, and locking forgets it. Through a real agent (`core/tests/agent.rs`): its approval prompt confirms, and a cancel doesn't.
- **The agent** (`agent/src/protocol.rs`): `keykeeper.biometric.approve` returns the key only after its own prompt, which says it's to approve a sign-in.
- **The app** (`app/test/sign_in_requests_test.dart`): the prompt, confirming first, a stale confirmation, denying, expiring, a request answered elsewhere, once per device every 15 minutes, a resent request taking the prompt's place with Approve held, a new request from the same identifier as another type or from another address closing the prompt and prompting on its own, Settings' list, and confirming before turning confirming off.
- **End to end** (`e2e/tests/device-approval.spec.ts`): the web vault asks to sign in with a device, the core approves through the app's own calls, and the web vault signs in; a denial; an expired request; and another account never seeing it.

### Not yet

- Push to phones while PassBundle is closed (our own push service).
- Where an address is, in these prompts: the server can now say it, from its own location database ("New-device check"), but upstream's dialog has no room for the database's credit.
- SSO sessions approving, with SSO's trusted-device design.
- Signing in with a device on one that never signed in to the account (upstream and Vaultwarden don't allow it).
- Our own apps asking to sign in with a device; they approve only.
- A confirmation in the web vault's and the extension's approval dialog (upstream's), to match our apps.

## Autofill

The goal is to be measurably better than upstream. 1Password currently does this much better than Bitwarden.

- **Corpus:** reduced fixtures of real login patterns. Multi-step logins, iframes, shadow DOM, single-page apps, one-time-code fields, signup and change-password forms, card forms.
- **Scoreboard:** every commit runs both the upstream extension and ours against the corpus. We publish the numbers.
- **Reference material** (compatible licenses only): Chromium autofill heuristics (BSD-3-Clause), Firefox form detection (MPL-2.0), and `apple/password-manager-resources` quirks data (MIT).
- **Per-site corrections:** handled by fill rules (next section), and shared, for people who choose to, as site fixes (see "Site fixes (opt-in)").

### How it keeps getting better

Most sites fill on the generic rules; the work is the long tail, found one site at a time. The loop:

1. **Finding them:** opt-in problem reports and shared site fixes (built, below) say which sites fail and how, as the shape of their sign-in fields, never values. MSP consoles see their staff's.
2. **A fixture for each:** every site we fix gets a reduced page in the corpus that reproduces its pattern (our own markup, not the site's code), so it can't break again unnoticed.
3. **The scoreboard:** CI runs our extension and upstream's against the whole corpus on every change and publishes the numbers. A change that makes any fixture worse fails.
4. **Curated fixes, shipped as data:** fixes we've reviewed ship in the extension as a list, like the site password rules: selectors and field roles only, never code from a server, so a compromised server or list can't run anything in a page. Fixes three people sent apply on their own server before that (built), and an MSP's apply to its clients at once.
5. **Generic first:** when several sites fail the same way, the fix goes into the generic matching instead of the list.

- **Not learned automatically:** nothing is learned from page contents in the background; every shared fix is something a person chose with the fill picker, and every curated one is reviewed by us.
- **Status:** steps 1 and the server half of 4 are built, and the corpus has started (`e2e/tests/autofill-corpus.spec.ts`): 12 sign-in pages, each one hard pattern (two steps on one page, a form drawn late, no form element, a shadow root, a same-site frame, decoy fields, a search box and a newsletter box nearby, labels wrapping fields, placeholders only, aria-labels only, a change-password form). Tier 1 fills each from the menu in a field, checks every field it should or shouldn't fill, prints the score, and fails when a page that filled stops filling, or when a known miss starts filling (so the list stays true). Today: 14 of 14 (one page is tried from two fields). The last miss, a username field the menu had no word for ("Account name", by aria-label), is fixed: a text field just before a sign-in password field in the same form is its username, whatever it's called. Next: add pages (one-time codes and card forms are tested separately today), run upstream's extension against it for the comparison, then the curated list.

### Choosing what fills where

Upstream fills a custom field only when its name exactly matches the page field's name, id, or label, so users end up reading HTML to make it work. Keeping the password from landing in the wrong field is just as hard. Ours:

- **Fill picker:** from the extension popup, or by right-clicking any field, choose "Fill this field with...". The list shows every value on the item (username, password, one-time code, each custom field, card and identity fields) plus "Never fill this field". One click fills it now and remembers the choice.
- **Rules travel with the item.** They are stored encrypted with the item, so they sync to every device. Site-wide "never fill" rules live in the user's encrypted settings.
- **Durable field identity:** a rule identifies a field by a fingerprint (autocomplete, name, id, label text, type, and position in the form), so small page changes don't break it.
- **Custom fields never fill on a guess.** They fill by rule, or by a confident label match ("Customer ID" against "Customer number") that the user confirms with one tap the first time.
- **Readable rules:** the item view and editor list an item's rules in plain language, for example "bank.example: the field with id custno gets the Customer ID field", and say when a rule's custom field is no longer on the item. The editor removes one; to change one, pick again in the extension (a new choice for the same field replaces the old one). The MSP console describes site fixes in the same words.
- **Storage (built):** a rule is a hidden custom field named `keykeeper.fill` whose value is a small JSON document (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/fill-rules.ts`). It's encrypted and synced like any field, and the server and other clients need no changes.
- **Where a rule fills:** only on the scheme and host (and port) it was made on, so a rule on an item that matches several sites, or one someone with edit rights added, can't send a value anywhere else, and an http:// copy of the site never gets one (a rule records "http" when it was made on an http page; one that says nothing is for https); only into a field the person can see; and, when the rule names a field id, only a field with that id (a name or label never stands in for it). For someone who can't see the item's passwords, a password or hidden field goes only into a password field. Our own fields (`keykeeper.*`) never fill a page field by name.
- **Rules keep upstream's limits (security review, November 2026):** a rule never fills a field upstream's matching never would (the field types autofill skips, search boxes, captchas and "forgot" fields, fields in a newsletter form), and on page load it keeps upstream's limits for page load: only empty fields, no one-time code (and a code only where the account may use one), and no username on a page without a password field. Unit tests cover each (`fill-rules.spec.ts`).
- **The picker keeps upstream's rules:** an item that asks for the master password asks first, for any value and for never-fill, and nothing fills, is saved, or is shared until the prompt succeeds (the pick waits in the background for at most five minutes, and only the prompt's own window, an extension page, can answer it); hidden fields aren't offered to someone who can't see passwords; a secret goes only into a page or frame the item's websites match, by the person's match settings, as upstream's autofill decides it; anything else in a frame the item isn't for asks first, and an http page that the item has as https asks first, with upstream's own warnings. "Fill this field with" is gone from the menu on blocked domains, as upstream's other autofill items are.
- **Built so far:** the right-click picker (`fill-field-menu.ts`). Value rules fill first, before upstream's matching. Never-fill fields are hidden from upstream's field selection, so a value meant elsewhere moves to the next fitting field. Readable rules in the item view and editor of the web vault and the extension (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/fill-rules.ts` reads and describes them, `libs/vault/src/keykeeper/fill-rules-*.component.ts` show them); the raw `keykeeper.fill` fields never show as custom fields. Unit tests cover the rules, the descriptions, removing one, and the menu. Tier 1 covers a custom field into a differently named field, never-fill, and a rule shown in the web vault, kept through an edit, and removed there, after which it no longer fills.
- **In the menu inside a field (built):** each login has a picker button beside it, which shows the same values; picking one fills the field the menu is in and remembers it, through the same code as the right-click menu (`fillFieldWith` in `fill-field-menu.ts`). The menu gets the values' names only, never the values. Arrow keys reach it like the menu's other buttons. Tier 1 picks a login's custom field for a site's Username box from the menu, then checks a normal autofill puts it there again.
- **The menu's words (November 2026):** "Fill this field with", each login's Username, Password, and Verification code, and "Never fill this field" come from the extension's translations, the same in the right-click menu and the menu in a field. The three values use upstream's own strings, which come translated; the menu's own two phrases are ours (`brand/messages.en.json`), English in every language until our strings are translated. Unit tests check a menu built from another language's words (`fill-field-menu.spec.ts`).
- **Next:** one-tap label-match suggestions, site-wide never-fill, and translated strings for the rules.

### Site fixes (opt-in)

When a site doesn't fill right, someone fixes it with the fill picker. Site fixes let that fix help everyone else on the same server, and tell us which sites need work, without passwords, custom field names, or browsing leaving anyone's device unless they choose. Decided October 2026: opt-in, anonymous, and nothing applies to others until several people agree.

- **What a fix is:** the site's host, how to recognize the field (the id, name, or label a fill rule records), and which kind of value goes there: the username, the password, the one-time code, or "never fill". Never a value, a custom field's name (it can say something about the person), a page address beyond the host, or anything else on the page.
- **Sharing is off until the person turns it on** (the extension's Autofill settings: "Share fixes I make with the fill picker"). With it on, each fix they make with the fill picker goes to their own server: PassBundle's hosted service, or the server their organization runs. Custom-field fixes stay on their device either way, and so does a fix whose field is known only by text with an email address or phone number in it (a label, a placeholder, an aria label: "Code sent to ..."); such a label is left out of a fix whose id or name decides. Fixes are for https pages only, so one made on an http page isn't shared.
- **Using shared fixes can be turned off** (the same settings: "Use fixes other people share", on by default). Off, the list isn't downloaded and none fill.
- **"Report a problem with this site"** (the extension's current-tab view) sends the host and the shape of the page's sign-in fields (types, ids, names, labels, and autocomplete hints; never values) with an optional note. It's one report per click, sharing or not, and the form says exactly what goes. An email address or phone number in a label, placeholder, or aria label (or an email address in an id or name) shows and goes as "(email address)" or "(phone number)".
- **Anonymous:** a fix is stored once, with a count of the different people who sent it. The server counts each person once by a keyed hash of their account and the fix (HMAC-SHA256 over the account id, a zero byte, and the fix's id), so it never stores who sent what; problem reports keep no account at all. The key is derived from the server's private RSA key, as event streaming's is (HMAC-SHA256 keyed with its PKCS#1 DER, over "passbundle site fix reporters v1"), never kept in the database, so a copy of the database can't tell who sent what (until October 2026 it was a random key in the database; the counts started over when it moved). Test vectors: `docs/vectors/server-derived-keys-v1.json`.
- **Bounded:** at most 20 problem reports per site a day, 5,000 in all, each kept 90 days; fixes that don't apply yet are forgotten after 180 days without anyone sending them again, and past 20,000 of them, the ones sent longest ago go. Each person sends at most 10 problem reports and 30 fixes at once, then one report every 6 minutes and one fix every 2: reports and fixes are kept without the account, so that's the only limit per person.
- **Confirmed fixes apply to everyone on the server** once 3 different people with verified email addresses have sent the same one (`SITE_FIX_REPORTERS`). Accounts that haven't verified their address aren't counted (security review, October 2026): making accounts is all it would take to be "different people", and a server that sends no email has no verified accounts, so there nothing applies by count. `SITE_FIXES_NEED_APPROVAL=true` raises the bar further: a fix applies only once the server's operator also approved it on the admin page's Sites list. The extension downloads the whole confirmed list when it unlocks and once a day, and matches it on the device, so asking doesn't tell the server which sites someone visits. A fix fills only where the person's own item has no rule for that field, with the same limits as their rules: the same host, a visible field, an id that decides, upstream's exclusions and page-load limits. On top of those (security review, November 2026), since anyone who runs the server, or a few accounts on it, could publish one: a fix fills only a fill the person starts (the popup, the menu in a field, a shortcut, the context menu), never on page load; only on https pages; and a password only into a password field, whoever it fills for. `{host, field: {id: "q"}, fill: "password"}` puts nothing in a search box.
- **MSPs:** fixes their own staff share also show in the MSP console, with who sent them (staff are accountable to their MSP). An MSP admin can apply one to every client right away ("Use for every client"); those MSP site rules reach the members of its clients and its staff the same way, without waiting for three people. Changes go in the MSP's activity log.
- **For us:** the server's admin page lists the sites with problem reports and the fixes waiting for confirmation, newest first. That's the private list of what needs work.
- **Privacy policy:** a section says what each choice sends and that it's off by default.
- **Tests:** unit tests for what a fix contains (and doesn't), sharing only when turned on, the downloaded list matched on the device, and fixes applied after the item's own rules with the same limits (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/site-fixes.spec.ts`); API tests for counting each person once, the threshold, what a fix and a report can hold, the daily cap, and MSP rules with their permission matrix (`e2e/tests/api/site-fixes.spec.ts`); and an extension test where an MSP admin turns sharing on, fixes a field with the fill picker, sees it in the console, uses it for every client, and the extension applies it to a login without a rule, and another that reports a site from the current tab's suggestions and checks the report carries the fields' shape and the note, never what's typed in them (`e2e/tests/site-fixes.spec.ts`).
- **Status:** built: the server (`keykeeper_site_fixes.rs`, a migration, the admin page's Sites list), the extension (`site-fixes.ts`, the Autofill settings section, and the report page, linked from Autofill settings and from under the current tab's suggestions, in the popup or a popout), and the console's Site fixes section.

### Open and fill

The open button beside a login in the extension opens the login's website in a new tab and fills that tab, once, as 1Password's Open & Fill does (decided November 2026; the owner's words: "only fill once in that tab"). Autofill on page load stays off by default; this is the one fill a site gets as it opens, because the person asked for it then.

- **Where:** the open button on a login's row in the popup's lists (and double-clicking the row, upstream's other way to open one), now "Open and fill <name>" with a sign-in icon; and in the item view, an "Open and fill" button beside each website's plain open, which still only opens. A login whose websites can't fill (ssh://, a pattern, one set to never fill) keeps the plain open. Both buttons are in the keyboard's tab order like the others; there is no right-click menu item or shortcut that opens a login, so none changed. The extension's Autofill settings say what it does, next to autofill on page load.
- **What fills:** upstream's own fill, through its orchestrator since web-v2026.10.1 (`AutofillOrchestrator.fillCipher`, which fills only the tab in front, still at the address the fill was aimed at), with the popup's Fill settings, so fill rules, never-fill fields, visible fields only, the one-time code rules, site fixes, and what someone who can't see an item's passwords may fill all apply. Stricter than the popup's Fill in three ways, since the person chose a login, not a form: never a new-password field (a sign-up or change-password form), never a frame the item isn't for (no question in the page, just nothing), and a username alone (a two-step sign-in's first step) only on a page that finished loading, so a page still arriving doesn't spend the fill on half a form. It never submits.
- **Master password re-prompt:** an item that asks for it asks in the popup before the tab opens, and cancelling opens nothing. The item view counts as asked, since the list asked before showing it, as upstream's Fill button there does. The background refuses a request for such an item that doesn't say it asked.
- **The address it opens:** one of the item's own websites. One saved without a scheme opens over https (upstream's plain open uses http), so the page it fills is never an http copy unless the website itself says http (`openAndFillUrl`, `libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/open-and-fill.ts`).

How it decides (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/open-and-fill.ts`, the background):

- **The tab:** the background opens the tab itself, when one of the extension's own pages asks (`fromExtensionPage`; a content script can't), and arms only that tab, by its id. It never arms a tab that already exists. Every other tab, even one on the same site, gets nothing.
- **The login:** the arming names the item, and the fill uses the item as it is then, so an edit, a deleted item, or changed permissions count.
- **Once:** the first fill that puts a value in a field spends it, whatever the tab does next.
- **A minute:** 60 seconds from the click. Long enough for a slow page, a redirect within the site, a form a script draws late, or a click on the site's own "Sign in" link; short enough that a tab left alone doesn't fill whatever it shows later.
- **The site:** a page fills only when its scheme, host, and port are the address it opened, or it is another of the login's websites by the person's match settings, as upstream's autofill decides it (`LoginView.matchesUri` with equivalent domains and the default match setting), over https. Every frame that gets a value passes the same check on its own address. A website set to never fill, itself or by the person's default, only opens. A site on the person's blocked list gets nothing.
- **What ends it:** closing the tab; a reload (the browser's own word for it, and where it gives none, the same address committing again); any top-level page that isn't the site, for good, so after a redirect through an identity provider or a phishing hop even the page it sends back to gets nothing; locking, signing out, or switching accounts; and the minute running out.
- **Only the tab in front:** like every upstream fill, never a tab in the background. Coming back to it within the minute fills it then.
- **Navigation while it fills:** the tab's address is checked again after its fields are read, only frames that described themselves at a checked address get the fill, and upstream's content script fills only if its page is still at the address it described.
- **State:** the background's memory. When Chrome restarts the extension's service worker it's forgotten, and the tab doesn't fill.

Threat model, what stops it filling a page it shouldn't:

- **Other tabs:** a phishing page open beside it, or a second copy of the site, isn't the tab the background opened, and only the background makes an arming, for a tab it just opened.
- **Redirects:** the page a redirect lands on is checked like any other. Another site ends it for good, so an identity provider, a phishing hop, and whatever they send back get nothing, and so does a downgrade to http.
- **Reloads and later visits:** once spent, nothing fills; a reload before the fill ends it; after a minute nothing does.
- **Slow pages:** it tries again for the minute (after a quarter second, backing off to every 3 seconds) until a field takes a value, never gives a page still loading a username alone, and checks the address again after reading the fields.
- **Frames:** an ad or a widget from another site in the page is checked on its own address and gets nothing.
- **Pretending to be the popup:** the request is taken only from the extension's own pages, must name the item and one of its own websites, and can only open a new tab.
- **Not covered:** a compromised sign-in page on the site itself, and an item whose own websites are wrong. A normal fill trusts both too.

- **Tests:** unit tests (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/open-and-fill.spec.ts`) for the site check (scheme, host, and port; an http copy; other websites by match settings; addresses that aren't websites), the arming (it runs out, one tab, reloads with and without the browser's word, another site ends it for good, the empty page a new tab starts with, another account), and the background: fills the tab once with the popup's settings except as noted and never submits; waits for a form drawn late; another tab; a tab not in front; a reload before the fill; a redirect elsewhere and back; a site it never heard the tab go to; an http copy; a redirect within the site and www by the default match; Host matching; the minute; frames; navigation while reading; the blocked list; locking and switching accounts; requests not from the extension, for a website not the item's, without the prompt, or while locked; and a website that never fills by default. Each guard was removed once to check its test fails. Also which websites open and fill (`libs/common/src/vault/keykeeper/open-and-fill.spec.ts`), the list asking for the master password before the tab opens (`vault-list-items-container.component.spec.ts`), and the item view's buttons, absent in the web vault (`autofill-options-view.component.spec.ts`). Tier 1 (`e2e/tests/open-and-fill.spec.ts`): it fills the tab it opened once, without submitting, and not after a reload or the same page again; a reload before the page had a form ends it; a redirect within the site fills, and one to another site (login.elsewhere.test, which the fixture server's /to-elsewhere sends to) gets nothing; another tab on the site gets nothing while the opened tab waits, and then the opened tab fills when it reaches the sign-in page; an item that asks for the master password asks before the tab opens, and cancelling opens nothing; and the item view's Open and fill.
- **Status:** built.

### One-time codes never become passwords

This is a hard invariant, enforced by tier 1 tests.

- Fields classified as one-time code inputs are never treated as password fields. Signals: `autocomplete="one-time-code"`, OTP, 2FA, MFA, or verification hints, and short numeric inputs that follow a login step.
- The save or update prompt never offers a value that equals the item's current TOTP code. It also never offers a value that looks like a code (4 to 8 digits) when the saved password is not itself all digits.
- Every password update keeps the old value in password history, and the notification has an Undo.
- The corpus includes many one-time-code page variants. Any save prompt on them fails the build.
- **Found in upstream:** the background keeps a sign-in's submitted form data for five seconds and fills a later username-less submission's gaps from it. A code pasted on the second step within that window becomes "alice-example changed her password to 123456". Fixed: a username-less submission whose only secret is 4 to 8 digits is never stored or merged. A username with a numeric PIN still saves.
- **Fields the page calls a code:** upstream skips fields tagged as codes. Ours also never takes a field for the password to save when the page calls it a code ("Enter the code we sent you", "Login code", an `otp` id) and nothing about it says password or PIN (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/one-time-code-fields.ts`). That covers passwordless sign-ins that show the email next to the code, which the value check can't tell from a PIN login. A PIN on the same kind of page still offers to save.
- **Tier 1 coverage:** tagged, masked (`type="password"`), and bare code fields, each on its own page and as the second step of a sign-in submitted inside the five-second window, after a calibration that proves the prompt still works for a real password change; and a passwordless sign-in with the email and a masked code on one page, next to the same page with a PIN, which still prompts. Unit tests cover the guard and the field check, and fail without them.

### An import's last items missing (upstream race)

Upstream's sync shares a request already in flight with anyone who asks for a sync meanwhile, and records the time they asked as when the vault was last synced. A sync that went out while an import was still being saved (the vault connecting to live updates right after sign-in starts one) answers with most of the items, and the import's own sync, asked for once it was done, took that answer; the time it recorded was after the import, so no later sync looked again, and the vault showed the import without its last items until something forced a sync. That was tier 1's flaky "1Password 8's CSV" (a missing "Juniper Studio", the file's last row). Fixed in `default-sync.service.ts`: a sync shares a request only if it went out after the sync was asked for, waits for an older one and asks again otherwise, and records the time its request went out. A unit test fails without it.

### Missed save prompts on fast sites (upstream race)

On a site that answers a sign-in at once (a fast intranet app, a local server), upstream's save and update prompts went missing about half the time, and the test fixture server answered sign-ins after 300 ms to hide it. Built November 2026; what the cause turned out to be, and what changed:

- **Waiting for the page after the sign-in:** the prompt waits until the tab has loaded the page the sign-in lands on. Upstream waited by adding a webNavigation listener once the sign-in request had finished, but a fast site's next page had often finished loading before the browser had that listener, so no event ever came and the prompt was dropped without a word. Measured with the delay gone: 12 of 24 sign-ins prompted without the fix, every one with it. Ours also checks the tab right after it starts waiting and a few times more (at 0, 250, 1,000, and 3,000 ms), listens only to that tab, goes on once, not at all if the submission was handled some other way, and gives up after a minute (`apps/browser/src/autofill/keykeeper/when-tab-loaded.ts`).
- **Every character typed:** the values the prompt offers were kept from the fields only after a tenth of a second's pause in typing, so a password typed quickly and sent with Enter came without its last characters, and the prompt offered to save the wrong password. They're kept at each keystroke now; the menu inside the field still waits for the pause. This is the "capture at submit" this note planned: the content script already sent the form's values at the submit itself (on the form's submit event and the submit button), but from those late copies.
- **Not changed:** a page that signs in with no submit event and no submit button the extension recognizes (a script that calls `form.submit()`) still relies on upstream's ask-the-page fallback while the request is in flight. Nothing in the page says when such a submission happens, so there's nothing to capture it at.
- **Tests:** unit tests for the wait (a page already loaded with no navigation event, a later event, once only, other tabs ignored, giving up) and for the prompt still showing when the page loaded first (`when-tab-loaded.spec.ts`, `overlay-notifications.background.spec.ts`), and for the last character before a quick submit (`autofill-overlay-content.service.spec.ts`); each fails without its fix. Tier 1's fixture server answers every request at once now, and `autofill.spec.ts` signs in five times on it, each with a last character typed and Enter at once, and needs a prompt every time with the password exactly as typed (no tries again); it fails without the fix.

## Script access (our Secrets Manager)

Bitwarden's Secrets Manager is a separate DevOps product under its source-available license, so we can't use it. Ours starts from what MSPs need: RMM scripts that use credentials (domain admin, client API keys) without pasting them into the RMM in plain text. The full design is `docs/script-access.md`, with test vectors in `docs/vectors/script-access-v1.json`.

- **Scoped tokens:** a token reads only the fields of the items it was granted, in one organization. It can't list or browse the vault.
- **Made for scripts:** a PowerShell module first (`Get-PassBundleCredential -Item "Domain admin"`), the command-line client later.
- **Short-lived and limited:** tokens expire (at most a year), can be limited to networks, and are revoked instantly. Binding to a machine comes with the desktop agent.
- **Only while their maker manages the organization (October 2026):** every read checks that whoever made the token is still a confirmed owner or admin of the organization (for an MSP's person, still an admin of the MSP), with an account that's on. Removing, revoking, or demoting them, or a client removing its MSP, stops their tokens, and apps are never asked to refresh such a token's copies, which would hand its maker the new password. See "Offboarding".
- **End-to-end encrypted:** a grant is a copy of the chosen fields, encrypted with a key derived from the token's seed, which the server never sees. The token key is also sealed with the organization key, so apps that hold it keep copies current after edits. Since October 2026 (version 2, `docs/vectors/script-grants-v2.json`) the sealed token key names its token and each grant's sealed spec names its token and item, and an app refreshes a copy only for an item in the token's organization that it can edit and see the passwords of, so a hacked server can't have it write anyone's items with a token key someone else knows (`docs/script-access.md`, "Keeping copies current"). Since November 2026 (version 3, `docs/vectors/script-grants-v3.json`) both seals also carry when the token ends, and an app refreshes a copy only before then, so a hacked server can't keep a token's copies current past its expiry. Revoking a token and taking an item out of it are still the server's alone: telling a newer seal from an older one would need state every refreshing app shares, so a hacked server can keep listing a revoked or narrowed grant as behind, and apps refresh its copy, until the token's sealed end, at most a year after it was made.
- **Audited:** every read is logged with token, item, time, and address. A token's first use from a new address shows in the MSP's activity log.
- **Status:** built (October 20, 2026).
  - **Settings, Joining and security:** an organization's owners and admins, and its MSP's admins, make tokens, see each token's reads, and revoke them. The token string shows once, with the lines a script needs.
  - **The module:** the server serves `PassBundle.psm1` with its own address filled in. It works in Windows PowerShell 5.1 and PowerShell 7 with nothing to install, and gives `Get-PassBundleCredential` and `Get-PassBundleSecret` (username, password, a current one-time code, or a custom field).
  - **Copies kept current:** the web vault and the extension refresh a grant's copy after its item changes, and the settings page shows a token as Updating until they have.
  - **Tests:** the API tests cover reads, refusals, networks, the 60-a-minute limit, refreshing, and who may do what. Unit tests check the limit with a clock of their own: 60 reads in any minute, however they're spread, so a token that read 60 times at once can't read again a second later. The module runs against the test server, and the vectors' checks run under Windows PowerShell 5.1 and PowerShell 7 on Windows. Upstream's crypto checks the vectors too.
- **Later:** rotation helpers that change a credential and update the item together, plus CI integrations if developers ask.
- **Clean room:** designed from public descriptions only. Never read Bitwarden's Secrets Manager code or SDK.

## Sending events to security tools

MSPs run security stacks (a SIEM or an MDR service such as Huntress, Blumira, or Microsoft Sentinel) that expect every business tool's audit events, and both Bitwarden and 1Password send theirs. Ours: an organization's events go to its security tools as they happen. What receivers get, and how to set up each tool: `docs/event-streaming.md`.

- **Where they go:** each organization has up to five destinations, of three kinds:
  - **Webhook:** an HTTPS POST of each event as JSON, signed the Standard Webhooks way: `webhook-id`, `webhook-timestamp`, and `webhook-signature` (`v1,` and the base64 HMAC-SHA256 of `<id>.<timestamp>.<body>`) with a secret per destination, shown once. An optional header (such as Authorization) carries a collector's own token, so HTTP collectors take events directly.
  - **Syslog over TLS:** RFC 5424 messages framed by octet counting (RFC 5425), facility "log audit", with a JSON or CEF message. A private certificate authority can be given for collectors on a company network.
  - **Microsoft Sentinel:** Azure Monitor's Logs Ingestion API, signed in as an app registration (client credentials) and sending to a data collection rule's custom stream. It trusts only the system's certificate authorities; a destination can't bring its own. Azure Government and Azure in China work through server settings, which are set only in the environment (never in the admin panel) and must be https and Microsoft's: a sign-in host of Microsoft Entra's, and a scope and ingestion domain under Azure Monitor's (`monitor.azure.com`, `.us`, or `.cn`). Only `EVENT_STREAMING_AZURE_TEST` lifts that, and only it allows a certificate authority of the server's own, for a test stand-in.
- **The MSP's feed:** an MSP's admins can add destinations for the MSP itself. They get every client's events, each tagged with the client and the MSP, and the MSP's own activity log (codes 9100 to 9199), so the MSP's SOC gets everything in one feed. A client's own destinations get its events without the MSP tag.
- **What an event says:** its id, time, type (upstream's code and a name in words), category, severity (low, medium, or high), the organization (id and name), who acted (a person's id and email, or "system" when nobody did, as with provisioning), what it was about (a member with their email, or an item, shared folder, group, or policy by id), and the address and device as upstream records them. Never item or folder names, vault contents, or secrets: the server can't read the vault, and an event says nothing the event log doesn't already keep.
- **Who sets it up:** an organization's owners and admins. Not custom roles, managers, or an MSP's technicians: a technician is an admin in each client but mustn't be able to send a client's activity somewhere the client and the MSP can't see. An MSP's admins for the MSP's feed, and in each client as its owners. A destination is reached only through its own organization or MSP (not an MSP's feed through a client, nor a client's through its MSP). One person sets up destinations in at most 20 organizations and MSPs (others there can add more). A destination stays with its organization or MSP when that person's account is deleted (it records who set it up as a foreign key that's then cleared), and then shares the worker's time as its organization's or MSP's own, but sends nothing: a destination sends only while whoever set it up still has access, checked on every delivery (November 2026; see "Offboarding").
- **Changes are events:** every change is in the organization's event log (types 1690 to 1692) or the MSP's activity log (9170 to 9172), all high severity and never dropped from a full queue, and goes to the other destinations like any event, saying which destination (its id, name, kind, and what the console shows of where it sends, never a secret), what changed (`changes`: name, settings, where it sends, new signing secret, turned on, turned off), and whether it's on. When the server turns one off, that's an event too: 1693 by no one (the system), or the MSP's 9173, with the destination's id and why. A destination that's turned off, removed, or pointed somewhere else gets one last event saying so first, so even a lone SIEM sees it: in the background, two at a time on the server, a few per organization or MSP and then one every 10 seconds, tried three times (now, after 5 seconds, after 30). Not kept across a restart.
- **Delivery:** every event saved to the event log is copied into a queue in the database (`event_deliveries`) for each destination that takes it, so a restart loses nothing.
  - **At least once:** an event can arrive twice (receivers drop repeats by its id), and events aren't promised to arrive in order.
  - **Fair time:** one worker per server sends to 16 destinations at once, but never two of one organization or MSP, and never more than two set up by one person (one whose account is gone counts as its organization or MSP). Turns go by the sending time used, not by turns taken: the person (who set the destination up) who has used the least goes first, then, among theirs, the organization or MSP that has used the least. Someone new starts level with whoever has used the least, and what everyone has used decays every five minutes. So one person with many slow destinations, or many organizations, gets no more of the worker than anyone else. A turn ends after 20 seconds, or 50 webhook events (400 syslog or Sentinel events). A webhook gets 5 seconds to connect and 10 to answer.
  - **Finding work:** each destination keeps when its earliest waiting event is due (`next_due_at`, updated as events are queued and after each turn), so the worker's look for work is one indexed query on the destinations, at most 500, never a scan of the queue. After a turn the worker reads the queue again once it has written that time, and writes it again if an event came in meanwhile: an event queued just as a turn ends finds the old time still due and leaves it, so without that second look the turn's new time (from a queue read without the event) would hide it until another event was queued.
  - **Slow isn't failing:** an answer slower than 5 seconds (10 for syslog and Sentinel), a turn that ends at its deadline (mostly spent sending) without catching up, or a turn whose sending averages more than a second an event, counts as slow. Only time spent sending counts, never the server's own waits (a busy database doesn't make a receiver slow). What it took is delivered; the destination is held back for 10 seconds, growing with each slow turn in a row to 10 minutes, and the console says "Slow" and why. Slowness never turns a destination off and never counts as failing.
  - **Failing receivers wait:** a failure holds the destination back for 10 seconds, then growing to an hour, with some randomness. So a slow or broken receiver, however many an organization or a person sets up, holds up only its own events.
  - **Retries:** for three days; then the event is dropped and counted. Everything goes as soon as one delivery works again.
  - **Floods merge:** what an app reports (item views, copies, fills, exports, notice clicks), failed logins, and single sign-on's refusals, which anyone can make many of, wait as one event per kind, organization, person, and minute (of the server's clock) with a `count`, `lastTime`, and up to 20 different `targets` and `ips`. So someone in several of an MSP's clients gets one event per client in the MSP's feed, never one for all of them. Once the worker takes an event to send, nothing more joins it; the next ones start another.
  - **What apps report is bounded:** `/events/collect` refuses a request with more than 500 events (413, nothing recorded, rather than keeping some), and our apps send a longer backlog in batches of 500, keeping a batch that fails for the next try. A person's apps get 30 requests in a row and then one every 2 seconds (429 past that).
  - **A bounded queue:** at most 10,000 wait per destination, kept as a running count. Past that the least important go first (low severity, then medium, then high, oldest first), counted. Changes to who can do what are kept and never dropped: members (except notice clicks, and single sign-on's sign-ins and refusals), groups, shared folders, the organization's settings, its single sign-on and domains, policies, its destinations, and the MSP's staff and clients; past twice the limit with only those, the oldest go. In an MSP's feed, the client with the most waiting loses first, so one client's member can't crowd out the others. Dropped events are still in the event log, which exports to CSV or JSON.
  - **Leases:** a worker holds a lease on a destination while it sends, released only by the worker that holds it, so two servers sharing a database don't send the same events at once.
  - **Stopping:** a destination that has failed for a day (and at least 10 times in a row), answers 410 Gone, or whose secrets can't be read is turned off (never one that's only slow), logged as above, and its organization's owners and admins (or the MSP's admins) get an email, at most once a day per destination, saying only what kind of trouble (an HTTP status, never what a receiver said) and that slowness alone never turns one off. Events keep waiting for it, so turning it back on sends what it missed. Turning one off on purpose drops what's waiting.
- **Rate limits:** five destinations per organization or MSP, and organizations and MSPs with destinations per person (both checked inside the transaction that adds one); at most 20 webhook requests a second to one destination; syslog in batches of 200 over one connection (at most three of a name's addresses tried, all within 30 seconds); Sentinel in uploads under its 1 MB limit, at most 5 a second; what apps report, as above; tests: one at a time per organization or MSP and per person, three in a row and then one every five seconds per person, four at once per server, and without holding a database connection while they wait on a receiver.
- **Secrets at rest:** a webhook's URL (collectors often put their token in it), its signing secret, a header's value, and Sentinel's client secret must be usable by the server (HMAC needs the secret), so they can't be hashed.
  - They're encrypted with AES-256-GCM under a key derived from the server's private RSA key: HMAC-SHA256 with a fixed label (`passbundle event streaming secrets v1`) over the key's PKCS#1 DER encoding, as upstream derives its pre-login key from the RSA key, so rewriting `rsa_key.pem` in another PEM form keeps the key. Each ciphertext is `v1.` and the base64 of a random 12-byte nonce and the sealed text, bound (as associated data) to its destination's id, its owner (organization or MSP), and its kind.
  - Test vectors: `docs/vectors/event-streaming-secrets-v1.json` (made by `make-event-streaming-secrets-v1.mjs` with Node's crypto alone) gives a throwaway RSA key in both PEM forms and its DER, the derived key, and for fixed nonces the associated data, plaintext, and `v1.` text, plus bindings that must fail. The server's unit tests check its derivation and sealing (split out for this, with the nonce given) against a copy, and tier 1 checks that the copy is this file.
  - That key lives where the RSA key does (the data folder, or its bucket), never in the database, so a copy of the database, such as the backups kept at another company, doesn't reveal them. Keep the data folder's backup apart from the database's. Replacing the RSA key (which also signs everyone out) leaves them unreadable: those destinations stop and ask for their secrets again.
  - Wiped from memory, as a best effort: the derived key, the RSA key's DER once the key is derived, the secrets' JSON while it's sealed or opened, and the opened secrets when they're dropped. Not wiped: the RSA key itself (the server keeps it to sign logins), the request a secret arrives in, copies the HTTP and TLS libraries make while sending (the URL, a header's value, the signing key inside the HMAC), Sentinel's access tokens (kept until shortly before they expire), and whatever the allocator leaves behind. The console never shows a secret again; a webhook appears as its scheme and host only, and the URL, the header's value, and the client secret are masked like passwords, each with a button to show what's typed.
  - A secret stays with exactly what it was entered for. Any change to a webhook's URL, its certificate authority, or its header's name asks for the header's value again; a change to its certificate authority asks for the URL again too (trusting another authority could expose what's sent, so it takes knowing the URL, which can carry a token); and a new host gets a new signing secret (shown once). Changing Sentinel's tenant, app, or endpoint asks for the client secret again. So another admin can't redirect one to themselves.
  - What receivers answer is stored with the destination's secrets taken out before it's cut to 200 characters: header values and their tokens, signing and client secrets, the URL, its query and each query value, and each path segment of 8 or more characters (collectors put tokens there), also decoded and as JSON escapes them. Where a cut (or reading only the answer's first 4 KB) leaves the start of one of those at the end, that start goes too.
- **Server-side request forgery:** a destination is chosen by an organization's admin, so the server mustn't become a way into its own network.
  - Webhooks are HTTPS only, with no user name or password in the URL; no proxies, no redirects.
  - The server resolves every name itself and checks every address it resolves to, and the request is pinned to those addresses (no second lookup that could answer differently). A name with any refused address is refused.
  - Public addresses only: for IPv6, global unicast (2000::/3) only. Private networks, addresses, or host names only where the server's admin lists them in `EVENT_STREAMING_ALLOWED_NETWORKS` (each with an optional port), for a self-hosted server whose security tools share its network. Loopback only through an entry that's itself loopback, never through a host name or a broader network.
  - Never allowed, whatever's listed: link-local addresses, cloud metadata services (169.254.169.254, AWS's fd00:ec2::/32, Google's fd20:ce::254, Oracle's fd00:c1::a9fe:a9fe and 192.0.0.192, Alibaba's 100.100.100.200, Azure's 168.63.129.16), multicast and reserved addresses, IPv4 written as IPv6 (::ffff:0:0/96, NAT64, 6to4, Teredo, IPv4-compatible), and anything the server's `HTTP_REQUEST_BLOCK_REGEX` matches.
  - Errors don't map the network: anything that goes wrong before a connection and its TLS handshake are complete (a refused address, a name that doesn't resolve, a closed port, a timeout, a certificate rustls rejects) gives the same message, with the details in the server's log only, and the Test button answers it 16 seconds after the test started, whatever it was, so it can't be timed either. After that, people see a receiver's status and the start of its answer (redacted, 200 characters, from the first 4 KB, read no further), and only the status when private networks are allowed.
  - A Sentinel endpoint must be under Azure Monitor's ingestion domain, checked when it's saved and again before each upload.
- **Status:** built (October 23, 2026): the server, Reporting > Event streaming in each organization's admin console, the MSP console's section, and JSON export of the event log (CSV is upstream's).
- **Tests:** unit tests for the signature (the Standard Webhooks reference vector), syslog and CEF formatting, Sentinel rows, the secrets' encryption and binding and their test vectors, the address classes and the allowlist, fair time (including 40 slow destinations of one person against one ordinary one on a worker with three slots), which events are kept and which merge, merged counts, when an event that came in while the due time was written makes the worker write it again, redaction (an echoed path the cut would split), slow versus failing, and email summaries. API tests with stand-ins on 127.0.0.1: a webhook receiver that checks signatures, a TLS syslog collector that splits octet-counted frames, and Microsoft Entra's token and Logs Ingestion endpoints. They cover the formats, an event queued just as the worker finishes with a destination (a rename right after adding two, in 18 organizations), retries, slow receivers (slow, not failing), one person's 20 slow destinations while someone else's keeps up (and two turns at a time at most), the 20-organization limit per person, one test at a time per person, stopping on 410 (the 1693 event, in the log and at another destination) and turning back on, the notices when turned off or pointed elsewhere, which destination each change event names, secrets that need entering again, floods of app-reported events and failed logins (capped, limited, merged), provisioning's events with the system as the actor, the MSP feed, refusals and their one message (after the same time) on a server with the default settings, reaching another organization's destination, an MSP's feed through a client or another MSP, and a client's through its MSP, the email, and a permission matrix for organizations (owner, admin, manager, user, custom role) and MSPs. A browser test drives the page (with the buttons that show secrets), the MSP console's section, and JSON export.
- **Reviewed** three times before merging (October 23, 2026), and the findings fixed as described above: connections held during tests; fairness, slow receivers, and leases; one person's many slow destinations starving everyone (fair time by person, two at a time, slow turns, the indexed look for work); flooding the queue, and a client's member blinding an MSP's feed (merging, the collect limits, kept changes, eviction by client); secrets redirected by editing; unbounded error bodies, and redaction before the cut; paused queues; SQLite transactions; racing edits; silent removal or repointing, and stops by the server; error text and timing that map a network; IPv6 and metadata ranges; the Sentinel endpoint, its server settings, and certificate authorities; email contents and frequency; how the key is derived and bound, and test vectors for it.
- **Not tested yet:** against Huntress, Blumira, and Sentinel themselves (they need accounts); a restart in the middle of sending (the queue is in the database, but no test restarts the server); the full queue's eviction order, by priority and by client (10,000 events is too many for a test; the unit tests check which events are kept); two servers sharing one database.
- **Later:** a client certificate for syslog collectors that ask for one, webhook batches, Splunk HEC's format, choosing which events each destination gets, and reading events through an API.
- **Clean room:** built from upstream's GPL event code and public documents (RFC 5424, RFC 5425, the CEF format, Standard Webhooks, Azure Monitor's Logs Ingestion API). Never from Bitwarden's integrations code.

## Running the server safely

A security pass over the server before launch (October 2026) found these, and these are the rules now. The deploy guide (`deploy/README.md`) says the same for whoever runs a server.

### The client's address

Login limits, script tokens' allowed networks, device approval's same-address check, and every log and event (organizations' event logs, what goes to their security tools, script token reads, the MSP's activity) record the visitor's address. Behind a proxy the server sees the proxy's, so it reads the visitor's from a header the proxy sets (`IP_HEADER`), and only when the request comes from a proxy it trusts (`IP_HEADER_TRUSTED_PROXIES`).

- **The problem:** the default header was `X-Real-IP`, trusted from any non-public address. The hosted service runs behind a Cloudflare tunnel, whose `cloudflared` reaches the server from the stack's own network, and Cloudflare passes `X-Real-IP` through as the client sent it. So a client chose its own address: past a script token's allowed networks, around every limit by address, and into the logs.
- **The hosted service:** `IP_HEADER=CF-Connecting-IP`, which Cloudflare sets itself and a client can't, trusted only from the tunnel's own address: the Compose stack gives `cloudflared` a fixed address on its network, outside the range Docker hands out (ops `hosting/compose.yaml`). The test server sits behind Caddy, which overwrites `X-Real-IP` with the address it got the request from (`header_up X-Real-IP {remote_host}`), trusted from 127.0.0.1.
- **The server refuses to start** when its `DOMAIN` is public (a global address, or a host name that isn't localhost, a single label, or reserved for local networks or testing) and `IP_HEADER` isn't set: the operator has to say what's in front of it (`CF-Connecting-IP`, `X-Real-IP` behind a proxy that overwrites it, or `none`). It warns when `IP_HEADER_TRUSTED_PROXIES` is `all` there.
- **Tests:** unit tests for what counts as a public `DOMAIN` and the refusal; an API test on a server that trusts no proxy, where a forged `X-Real-IP`, `CF-Connecting-IP`, or `X-Forwarded-For` doesn't get a script token past its networks, and one that starts the server on a public address without and with `IP_HEADER`; and the deploy smoke test (`deploy/smoke.sh`), which runs the image as the hosted service does, with a stand-in tunnel at its fixed address, and checks a forged header from anywhere else is ignored while the tunnel's is believed.

### Defaults on a public server

A server whose `DOMAIN` is a public `https://` address gets safer defaults for what its operator leaves unset: `SIGNUPS_VERIFY=true`, and 1 GB each for a person's attachments, an organization's attachments, and a person's Send files (`USER_ATTACHMENT_LIMIT`, `ORG_ATTACHMENT_LIMIT`, `USER_SEND_LIMIT`, in KB; `-1` for no limit, on any server). Upstream had no storage limits at all by default. The hosted service's settings name `SIGNUPS_ALLOWED` and `ORG_CREATION_USERS` too, on purpose. A server on localhost, a private address, or a name like `.local` keeps upstream's defaults, so self-hosting at home and the tests don't change.

### Confirming sensitive changes

Some changes let someone read what an organization does, or sign its people in, from outside PassBundle, or lower the bar for signing in: making or replacing its provisioning (SCIM) token, saving its single sign-on settings, adding or changing an event destination (an organization's or an MSP's; turning one on or off, and a new signing secret, too), making a script token or adding an item to one (or changing which of an item's fields it gets), and turning two-step login for admins, or an MSP's requirement for its staff, on or off. Each needs the person's master password, or a one-time code by email for an account without one, sent with the request (upstream's `masterPasswordHash` or `otp`, as for an organization's API key), so a session alone can't make them: one that single sign-on started from a client's own tenant, say, or a stolen session token. The web vault asks with upstream's verification dialog before sending. Removing something, sending a test event, and refreshing a script token's copy (which apps do on their own, with the same fields) need nothing. Who may make each change is checked first, so anyone else still gets "not found", and so are a change's own rules (only someone who meets two-step login for admins turns it off). Tests: an API test per change (none, a wrong password, the right one, and nothing changed until it's right), jest specs for the requests and the dialog, and the browser tests type the password where the web vault asks.

### The admin page

Leave `ADMIN_TOKEN` unset in production; turn the admin page on for a task, then off. Whoever has the token can do anything the page does, so the settings that could quietly send people's secrets elsewhere are set only in the environment and can't be changed there: `DOMAIN` (links in emails, passkeys), every `SMTP_*` setting (sign-in codes and links go by email), `IP_HEADER*`, `EVENT_STREAMING_ALLOWED_NETWORKS`, `HTTP_REQUEST_BLOCK_*`, `YUBICO_SERVER`, `DUO_HOST`, and `FILL_ASSIST_RULES_URL` (where the extension would fetch autofill targeting rules). A value saved on the admin page before is ignored.

### Logs

- A request's query is logged by name only, never its values (`?uri=[hidden]`): queries carry codes, tokens, emails, and the websites in people's vaults (the change-password lookup's `?uri=`, the breach check's `?username=`). Provisioning, single sign-on's identity routes, and a sign-in request's answer (with or without a slash at the end) are logged without any query.
- Rocket's form parser (which logs every form field at trace, passwords and codes sent to `/identity/connect/token` included) is off, and Handlebars (which logs what it renders: emails' links and codes) logs warnings only, whatever `LOG_LEVEL` says, its per-module overrides included.
- When an event destination can't be reached, the server's log gets the error without the destination's URL, path, query, or any of its secrets: collectors put tokens in their URLs.

### Unknown emails at prelogin

The prelogin (`POST /identity/accounts/prelogin`) answers every email with KDF settings, so it can't tell anyone which emails have accounts: an email with no account gets made-up settings, picked from a short list by a keyed hash of the email, so they stay the same from one request to the next (`unknown_email_kdf` in the server's `api/core/accounts.rs`). The list differs from upstream's:

- **Why:** upstream's list also has PBKDF2 at 5,000 and 100,000 iterations, which its own older accounts can have. A PassBundle account can't have 5,000 (changing the KDF refuses PBKDF2 under 100,000, and there are no accounts from before that), so that answer said the email had no account, straight from the API. And PassBundle's apps refuse PBKDF2 under 600,000 (docs/core.md, "KDF settings are checked before anything is derived"), so for about a third of unknown emails they said the settings were too weak, not "That email or master password isn't right.", which also told the two apart.
- **Now:** only settings an account can have that the apps accept: PBKDF2 at 600,000 (the default, in the list twice, as upstream gives it the extra weight), 800,000, and 1,000,000; and Argon2id at 3 iterations, 64 MiB, and 4 threads, and at 6 iterations, 32 MiB, and 4 threads (upstream's two). The list keeps upstream's length, so an email that got one of the others before gets the same one now.
- **Tests:** a server unit test checks every entry passes the server's own check for a KDF change and the apps' limits (PBKDF2 600,000 to 2,000,000; Argon2id 16 to 1,024 MiB, 2 to 10 iterations, 1 to 16 threads), and that the default is there twice. The apps' test against a real server signs in with an email that has no account and gets "That email or master password isn't right." every time (passbundle-apps `core/tests/server.rs`, `signs_in_syncs_saves_edits_and_trashes`), where before it failed when the email drew one of upstream's weak entries.
- **For the owner to decide (not changed):** the server still lets an account change its KDF to PBKDF2 from 100,000 iterations, as upstream does, while PassBundle's apps need 600,000. An account set between the two signs in on the web and the extension but gets "This account's security settings are weaker than PassBundle allows" in the apps. Raising the server's minimum to the apps' would make the two agree, and make 600,000 the floor everywhere.

### What the server fetches

Icons, change-password pages, and event destinations share one guard (`keykeeper_streaming::guard`): never cloud metadata and platform services (169.254.169.254, Azure's 168.63.129.16, Alibaba's and Oracle's, AWS's fd00:ec2::/32), link-local, multicast, or reserved addresses, or IPv4 written as IPv6 (NAT64, 6to4, Teredo, IPv4-compatible), whatever the settings say; private addresses only where the operator allows them. Every address a name resolves to is checked before connecting. With `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, or `ALL_PROXY` set, the proxy looks up names, so icons and change-password lookups are checked only for addresses written in URLs: the proxy has to refuse the rest (event streaming never uses a proxy). The change-password lookup, which anyone may ask for, as for icons, also gives each address (an IPv6 /64) 30 answers at once, then one every 2 seconds; looks up at most 60 new sites at once across the server, then one a second; has people asking about one site at once share one lookup; and reaches only ports 443 and 80.

### Crashes from text

A string sliced at a byte that isn't a character boundary panics, and the release builds that abort on a panic (`release-micro`, CI's `ci` profile) stop the whole server. SCIM sliced attribute names at byte 4, so a directory sending a name like "€€x" made every later list of users fail; and upstream's code that hides part of an email for two-step login cut at byte 2.
